PREFACE 


HE devout reader may feel some surprise at receiving 

- from the hand of a Religious of the English Church, a 
book written by the most famous of Papal controversialists. 
Let it be said at once that the De Ascensione mentis in Deum is 
the fruit of one of the great man’s annual retreats, and shows 
us something of the inner life of a public character, and of his 
mind when liberated in solitude and prayer. 

We have five such works of the Cardinal: De Ascenstone, 
1615 3 De aeterna felicitate Sanctorum (Of the eternal happiness 
of the Saints), 1616; De gemitu columbae (The Mourning 
Dove), 1617 5 De septem verbis Chrisit (The Seven Words), 
1618 ; De arte bene moriendi (Holy Dying), dated 1620, the 
year before his own holy death. 

These five books stand in a class by themselves among the 
works of a vigorous controversialist, and may be used with great 
profit by people who are repelled by Bellarmin’s controversial 
pleas or, at the best, indifferent to the mingled theology and 
politics of the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries. 

Robert Francis Romulus Cardinal Bellarmino was indeed, 
for Englishmen, the typical Jesuit upholder of the claims of the 
Bishop of Rome. He was, sometimes, too little papalist for 
some Popes, but we knew him as the opponent of our learned 
King James in the matter of the Oath of Allegiance, as the 
protagonist, with Baronius, against Venice and Paolo Sarpi, a 
public man accepted, by our robust nationalism, as the champion 
of a Catholic community ‘rightly struggling to be free.’ A 
caricature of the Cardinal decorates the old ‘ Grey-beard’ jugs 
still found when we disturb the pavements of London." © 

He was, indeed, no less than the Professor of Controversies 
in the Roman College; he was involved in the awkward affair 
of Pope Sixtus the Fifth’s Vulgate and its correction; he spent 


* Several turned up, together with some fragments of the Con- 
fessor’s shrine (now restored to their place), when we were digging 
the foundations of St. Edward’s House in Westminster. 

ill 


iv ; PREFACE 


all his immensely laborious and devoted life—with a short in- 
terval in the See of Capua—at the very heart of theological 
politics and theological administration. He 1s, in fact, a great 
figure, the great figure of the counter-Reformation, in so far 
as that movement is a work, not of devotion, but of argument 
and of political or social action. 

There is another aspect of counter-Reformation effort, or 
perhaps it should be called more simply another movement. 
It was a movement comprising the reform of the Spanish 
Carmelites, and the introduction by de Berulle of the reformed 
Carmelites into France, the work of Philip Neri, and of the 
Borromeos, of all those energies of the great bishops, of the 
Oratory, of the Lazarists and St. Sulpice, which have still their 
vivid results in the clear-eyed and serious piety of France. It 
is a movement not yet exhausted but everywhere made evident 
in the Christian dignity and spiritual vitality of the parochial 
clergy and its parishioners, too long obscured by the eminence 
of the Religious Orders and by the all-but-contented worldli- 
ness of many seculars. 

A greater movement still, the recovery of large elements of 
the Gospel by such as St. Teresa, the reassertion of the Divine 
Discipline of the Word, long ago trusted by St. Clement of Alex- 
andria, cannot be spoken of here. It is comparable not to the 
stirrings of learned Kings and Princes of the Church, nor even 
to the enlarged pastorates of St. Philip, St. Charles, de Condren, 
St. Vincent, but to that re-presentation of the spirit and manner 
of Christ which makes the Little Poor Man of Assisi an alight- 
ing point in the mind’s effort to pass from the present back to 
the first days of the Son of Man. 

From all these revivals, the lower and the higher alike, how 
remote—says our critic—is the memory of this stalwart of con- 
troversy, this champion of a peculiar, and already past, concep- 
tion of the Apostolic See ? 

These and such things easily occur to any one with a little 
knowledge of Reformation controversy. “They must not forbid 
us to profit by the spiritual experience and the spiritual teaching 
of learned divines on either side. All harsher elements must be 
set down, as was said of the Cardinal by a Pope—and Papal 
persons were not always satisfied with Bellarmin—to the troubles 
of the time and not to the character of the debater. 


~ PREFACE Vv 


Hesitation of a more important kind may be felt by privileged 
souls who recoil from our author’s word: ‘We mortal men, 
as It seemeth, can find no other ladder whereby to ascend unto 
God, than the works of God.’ 

‘There are who find, ‘ as it seemeth to them,’ that they ascend 
to God, not by attention to the visible, but by detachment from 
it. “hey would say, ‘ There is a light that shines through crea- 
tures, but another light that shines upon them, or rather the 
same Light in another mode. ‘There is a long and difficult 
lesson pieced out from the spectacle before us: but this lesson 
is not really followed with success, unless we receive at least a 
glimpse of the answer in another way, by a more direct path of 
communication. God speaks to us in manifold ways by His 
creation, and He speaks by His prophets. But He has besides 
a direct light for the soul; and by the help of this light, we partly 
understand both history and prophets. Indeed it is because he 
is taught in this more direct way that the prophet speaks and 
interprets the manifold and often contradictory aspects of the 
world.’ Good. ‘That is true enough. But note, the devout 
writer chooses his words very exactly. He does not say there 
is no other way to ascend to God, but there is no other /adder. 
Those who need no steps are not concerned with his saying. 
And every one that needs the steps must look above them before 
he begins to climb by the successive treads. He must look at 
once to what the steps lead to, and see them, as Jacob saw the 
ladder of his vision, coming down from heaven to earth, leading 
up from stony solitude to God. 

‘To deny, or at all to despair of finding significance in the 
works, is to run clean away from the lesson of the Incarnation, 
and to be weak, so far, in the Spirit of Christ. 

It is well if those who have found something of the immediate 
gift are zealous in detachment from lower satisfactions, as they 
are cautious already in accepting the lower guidance; well, 
if those who welcome the steps are determined not to’ rest in 
them, but to climb them, and to find in every occasion of life an 
opportunity for the grateful love, itself God’s gift, that prepares 
the soul for a higher rest, and lifts it as on wings to the unchang- 
ing sky. At the very lowest, the creatures and the wonderful 
inspired Scriptures that speak of them to us, are pointers showing 
the soul how it also may enter into the Heavenly Wisdom. 


Vi ; PREFACE 


Certainly those who follow for a few days the instructions 
of Bellarmin will find that he does not give them crutches they 
might dispense with, but a secret of escape from all worldliness 
and all despondency. He does not naturalize our prayer. He 
spiritualizes our daily walk. 

The translation has been most carefully and ably done; and I 
trust that the work, affectionately and devoutly Bestowed upon 
it, may, by the Divine mercy, find a harvest in the refreshment 
of souls already intent on prayer, in the invigoration of faith, 
and the breaking up of the ground of many hearts so that the 
springs of Divine love may flow, and in the liberation of seekers 
after God so that, according to His holy Will, they may find 
Him with the greatest possible directness and simplicity. 


P. N. W 


LIFE OF BLESSED ROBERT BELLARMIN 


N October the fourth in the year of our Lord, 
1542, at Montepulciano in Tuscany a son was 
born to Vincent Bellarmin and his wife Cynthia, sister 
of Pope Marcellus II. This child was later to become 
known to fame as Cardinal Bellarmin, a scholar and 
controversialist of great ability. He was named Robert 
after Cardinal Robert Pucci, and his pious father and 
mother began early to train their child in the practices 
of piety and devotion. Like many a boy to whom 
vocation to the priesthood has been given, he used, 
when quite young, to set up little altars and preach to 
such an audience as would gather round him in his 
own home. 

While still a lad he wrote a hymn on Saint Mary 
Magdalene which was preferred before others for in- 
sertion in the breviary published in 1568. 

In early youth Robert Bellarmin felt drawn to 
dedicate his life entirely to the service of God, but at 
first he was uncertain whether the vocation was to the 
Religious life or to Holy Orders. Needing counsel, 
he turned for advice to a father of the Society of Jesus, 
and it was not long before he saw clearly that he must 
seek admission to that Society. 

At the same time his cousin Richard Bellarmin 
was feeling the call to Religion, and the two youths 
were eager to make their response forthwith, but they 
yielded to the pleadings of their respective fathers who 
urged a year’s delay; this interval was so faithfully 
spent that the Society allowed it to be counted as the 
first of the five years’ novitiate. The year of probation 
and waiting was spent by the cousins at a house in the 

Vil 


Vill Lire oF Biessep RoBertT BELLARMIN 


country, where they gave themselves up to study and 
devotion and to teaching the children of the neigh- 
bourhood. 

At the end of the year they went to Rome, Robert 
stopping on the way to visit his father at Monte- 
pulciano. By this time the father had realized the 
truth of his son’s vocation and now he was ready to 
bless him, though in tears he said, ‘Go where God 
calls you, my son: in your person I sacrifice all the 
hopes of my family.’ 

So in September, 1560, the two cousins entered the 
novitiate of the Society of Jesus, and joy was in their 
hearts. Robert at first was employed in the kitchen, 
where he was as happy as when at his quiet studies, 
for his only desire was to do the will of God, and to 
follow the self-abasement of our Lord, and he rejoiced 
when difficult and distasteful tasks were committed 
to him. | | 

Presently came a change of work and he was sent 
to college to pursue his studies in science and natural 
philosophy. His health now began to fail, and phy- 
sicians foretold either an early death or a life of weak- 
ness and ill health; but infirmities only served to 
increase Bellarmin’s courage and he continued his life 
of prayer and mortification with great profit. 

After a while he was given a lectureship in Florence, 
and when he entered on his task he must have appeared 
more fit for the infirmary than for the lecture room, 
but his trust in prayer was great, and strength to fulfil 
his vocation and to complete his work was granted 
him and his health was soon seen to improve. 

His duties next took him to Flanders, and he is said 
to have preached at Louvain with such great fervour 
and power, that though not yet in sacred orders he 
influenced many of his hearers. 

The Flemish fathers of the Society desired his 


ordination, and for this permission was obtained from 


Lire oF Biess—ED RoBertT BELLARMIN 1X 


Rome and he was ordained priest by Bishop Cornelius 
Jansen at Liege at Easter, 1570. 

For seven years Bellarmin remained in Flanders, 
preaching and winning in a wonderful way numbers 
of persons who had hitherto been opposed to the Faith. 
Then he received the summons of Pope Gregory XIII 
to lecture in the Jesuit College in Rome, and there he 
lived a life of untiring labour and of great devotion 
till he was sent to France to assist Cardinal Cajetan 
in the political affairs in which he was deeply engaged. 
On the death of Gregory XIII Cajetan returned to 
Rome, taking Bellarmin with him. 

Not long after he was placed as Superior in the 
Jesuit house in Rome, and after three years there he 
became provincial Superior at Naples. 

In 1597 he was back in Rome, appointed Peniten- 
tiary by Clement VIII, but he obtained leave to abide 
in the house of the Jesuits which was near the Vatican. 
In March, 1599, Robert Bellarmin was nominated one 
of three new cardinals; he begged that he might be 
passed over, but his petition was not granted, and he 
humbly accepted the honour bestowed upon him. 

In 1602 he was made Bishop of Capua and at once 
went to live in his diocese, and gave himself to the 
special work of a bishop of his flock; but when the 
Pope (then Paul V) required his presence in Rome 
he felt it necessary to resign his see; and he then, 
living in Rome, gave himself up to prayer and study, 
and to the writing of theological books. 

His writings show a remarkable spirituality, notably 
the five short treatises, of which De Ascensione Mentis 
in Deum is one. This he called his * Benjamin,’ for 
it was written when he had reached the age of seventy- 
two, and in the prefatory dedication to Cardinal Aldo- 
brandini he says that to this little book he often turned 
to find consolation and help. 

Bellarmin foretold his own death some while before- 


x Lire oF BiesseD Bosert RELLARMIN 


hand. On the death of Paul V, when the cardinals 
gathered together to choose his successor, Bellarmin 
said to Cardinal Montalte, ‘ We are now about to elect 
a pope in the depth of winter: the next pope will be 
elected in the height of summer, but neither you nor 
I will assist at his election ’—and the forecast was 
fulfilled. | 

As time went on the Cardinal’s infirmities increased, 
particularly his deafness, and feeling he could no longer 
fulfil his duties he begged to be relieved of them, and 
leave was given for his retirement to a house of his 
own Society. He chose the Novitiate of Saint Andrew, 
and there took up his abode in August, 1621. His 
last illness soon came upon him, and during it he 
showed his usual patience and gracious manner to- 
wards those who waited upon him, and all were 
edified by his devotion. When death approached he 
asked for the last Sacraments, and received them with 
great humility and fervour, and on September 27th, 
while uttering the adorable and sweet Name of Jesus, 
he expired, thus ending a long pilgrimage of near 
eighty years. 

Robert, Cardinal Bellarmin was beatified by Pope 
Pius XI in May, 1923. 


TRANSLATOR’S NOTE 


ARDINAL Bellarmin’s treatise, De Ascensione 

Mentis, written in 1615, was a favourite book of 

devout persons of the day in France, in Flanders, in 
Spain, and in Germany, as well as in Italy. 

In the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries trans- 
lations into English were made and valued, but these 
appear now to be little known. A French translation 
by Pére Herman, S.J., has been published recently. 

The spiritual teaching of this short mystical treatise 
is so solid, robust, and alluring, that it will be good if 
it finds readers at the present time. 

The versions by Hall (1705) and by Dalton (1841), 
as well as a Douay version by J. B. (1616), have been 
of assistance in the preparation of this fresh translation 
into English. 

The outline of Bellarmin’s life which is prefixed 
has been gleaned partly from a French biography 
written by Pére Frizon, S.J., and published at Nancy 
in 1708; partly from the Cardinal’s autobiography 
published i Inii7.53,

DEDICATION 


To the most illustrious Cardinal Peter Aldobrandini 
Robert, Cardinal Bellarmin sends greeting. 


| ees September I wrote, by the help of God, a 
little book about the ascent of the mind to God 
by a ladder of created things. With permission of 
our holy father I kept that month for a retreat, setting 
aside all other business, in order to have leisure for the 
things of God. Though the little book was written 
during the retreat for my own private use, friends 
have urged me to publish it, and my desire is that it 
should bear your name on its title-page. 

If my labour prove to be of any value, it will be of 
use particularly to those who are occupied in public 
affairs, such as are the rulers of the Church. 

Now among the princes of the Church, burdened 
with a heavy weight of business, you yourself must 
be reckoned one of the chief: since you are cardinal, 
archbishop, chamberlain of the Holy Roman Church, 
a member of the Inquisition, and patron of many 
important works. | 

Another reason leads me to dedicate this work to 
you, namely my desire of handing down to posterity 
some record of the many kindnesses I have received 
at your hands and of my grateful appreciation thereof. 

The offering that I make, though it be very small, 
will, I trust, be none the less pleasing to you: we value 
a book not so much for its size as for the usefulness of 
its contents. I do not know what will be the criticism 
of readers of this little book: as for myself I have found 
it more useful than other little works of mine. I do 

x1X 


xx DEDICATION 


not read my other books unless necessity forces me to 
do so; this one | have read three or four times, and I 
am sure that I shall turn to it again. It is possible that 
I value the book not for its own merit, but because it 
is, as it were, my Benjamin, for I wrote it in my old 
age. 

Adee then, my dear Cardinal, this little gift from 
me, not only as a mark of my regard, but also as 
possibly fulfilling the office of counsellor. If at times 
you feel yourself overwhelmed with a pressure of 
business which threatens to hinder you from your 
accustomed study, and to snatch away the hour of 
prayer, let this little book gently remind you to with- 
draw your mind fora few moments from the crowd of 
cares, and turn it to the joys of the interior life. In 
meditation and in reading you will have leisure to see 
that the Lord he is God and that he is the only true, 
highest, and everlasting good, and that to possess him 
brings joy, to lose him, only misery. 


THE MIND’S ASCENT TO 
GOD 


INTRODUCTION 


Of the necessity of seeking God by a ladder of things 
created. The inscription of this book. 


Ho Scripture doth often admonish us to seek 
God diligently, for although God is not far 
from any one of us, since, as the Apostle says, ‘ In him 
we live and move and have our being’; ? yet we are far 
from God, so that unless we daily dispose our hearts 
to ascend, and make for ourselves a ladder into heaven 
and seek God very earnestly, we shall, like the prodigal 
son, come to feed swine in a land far off from our 
Father and our home.3 And to explain shortly how 
these states can exist together, to wit, that God is not 
far from us, because he always seeth us and before his 
eyes all things are present: he always thinketh of us, 
because he careth for us :4 he always toucheth us 
because he beareth along all things by the Word of 
his power: 5 but we are far from God, because we 
do not see him and have no power to see him, for he 
dwelleth ‘in the light which no man can approach 
unto,’® and we are ‘ not sufficient of ourselves to think 
anything as of ourselves ’;7 much less are we able to 
touch him and cleave to him with pious affection 


e.g. Deut. iv.29; 1 Chron. xvi. 11; Pss. lxix. 33, cv. 4; Isa. lv. 6. 
2 Acts xvii. 28. 3 St. Luke xv. rr. 4 x St. Pet. v. 7.

unless his right hand receive us and draw us unto 
himself. And therefore when David said, ‘My soul 
hangeth upon thee,’ he added immediately, * Thy right 
hand hath upholden me.’! And we are far from God, 
not only because we cannot see him, but also because 
being busied about temporal goods with which we are 
encompassed and overwhelmed, we very easily forget 
God and with a cold heart we scarce even with our 
lips pronounce his Name in psalms and sacred prayers. 
And so this is the cause why the Holy Ghost in the 
Holy Scriptures, as we were saying even now, doth so 
often admonish us to seek God. 

‘Seek ye after God and your soul shall live’;? and 
‘Seek his face evermore ’;3 and ‘ The Lord is good 
unto them that wait for him, to the soul that seeketh 
him’;4, and ‘Seek ye the Lord while he may be 
found’;5 and ‘In simplicity of heart seek him’; & 
and ‘ If thou shalt seek the Lord thy God, thou shalt 
find him, if thou seek him with all thy heart.’7 

But although this diligence in seeking God should 
be the duty of all the faithful, yet it most properly 
appertains to the prelates of the Church, as Saint 
Augustine, Saint Gregory, Saint Bernard, and other 
holy fathers witness. For they plainly write that a 
bishop cannot profit himself or others unless he dili- 
gently apply himself to meditation on divine things 
and the renewing of his own mind. Saint Augustine 
in his book The City of God saith: ‘The love of 
truth requireth a holy retiredness, and the necessity 
of charity a just employment, which if it be not imposed 
upon us we ought not to seek it, but betake ourselves 
wholly to the holy inquest of truth : but if we be called 
forth into a place the law and need of charity bindeth 
us to undertake it. Yet may we not for all this give 
over our first resolutions, lest we lose the sweetness of

5 Isa. lv. 6. 6 Wisd. i. 1. 7 Deut. iv. 29. 


INTRODUCTION 3 


that, and be surcharged with the weight of the other.’ : 
And the same Saint Augustine, speaking in his Coa- 
fessions of himself and of his frequent meditation on 
God by the consideration of creatures, saith: ‘I often 
do this ; it delights me, and when I can be spared 
from my necessary business I have recourse unto this 
pleasure.’ Saint Gregory in his book on the pastoral 
charge saith: ‘Let a prelate be equal unto any in 
compassion and before all in contemplation, that 
through the tenderness of piety he may take over the 
infirmities of others unto himself and by the height of 
contemplation in seeking after things invisible he may 
overpass himself’;3 and. Saint Gregory in the same 
connection brings forward the examples of Moses and 
of Christ. For Moses often went into the tabernacle 
and again came out. He went in, that he might con- 
template the hidden things of God; he came out, that 
he might bear the infirmities of his neighbours. And 
Christ himself in the daytime by preaching and work- 
ing miracles sought the salvation of those around him, 
but the nights he passed without sleep in prayer and 
contemplation. ‘ He continued all night in prayer to 
God,’ writes Saint Luke.4 Many things also like unto 
these may be read in the last chapter of the same book. 
Moreover, Saint Bernard, in order to admonish Pope 
Eugenius, formerly his pupil, not to give himself wholly 
to action, but sometime every day to recollect himself 
and to enjoy holy rest and heavenly food, wrote five 
books Of Considerations in which he not only exhorts 
him unto the daily meditation on divine things, but 
also plainly teaches him the manner and method of 
meditation and how by meditation to ascend, and by 
ascension to unite himself to God in understanding 
and affection. Neither does he admit that excuse which 
he might have put forward and which many do nowa-

days offer: to wit, that the over many businesses where- 
with the office of a bishop is accompanied would not 
afford him leisure enough to apply himself unto medi- 
tation on divine things. For none truly ought to give 
himself so wholly to outward businesses, but that he 
may take some time to strengthen his body with meat 
and drink and sleep. And if the body without doubt 
require refection and rest, with how much more reason 
doth the soul require her meat and sleep, and it is not 
possible for her amidst the encumbrances of so many 
important affairs, without this provision, to fulfil her 
office rightly. But the meat of the soul is prayer, and 
her sleep is contemplation, by the which ladders are 
framed in the heart, that the God of gods may be seen 
in Sion.? 

But we mortal men, as it seemeth, can find no other 
ladder_whereby-to-aseend_unto_God, than the works 
of God : for those who by the singular gift of God 
have by another way become. admitted into Paradise _ 
to hear God’s secrets which it is not lawful for man to 
utter, are not said to have ascended, but to have been 





for a man to utter.’ And that a man may one the works 
of God, that is by creatures, ascend unto the knowledge 
and love of the Creator, the book of Wisdom doth 
teach3 and the Apostle to the Romans:4 and reason 
itself doth sufficiently confirm it, since the efficient 
cause may be known by the effects, and the pattern 
by the image: and there can be no doubt that all 
creatures are the works of God and that man and the 
angels are not only his works, but also his images, as 
the Scriptures teach us. 

{ therefore, moved by these reasons, and having 
some small degree of leisure from public affairs and 


1 Ps, lxxxiv. 7. * 2 Cor. xii. 4. 3 Wisd. xiii, 4 Rom. i. 19. 


INTRODUCTION 5 


admonished by the example of Saint Bonaventura, who 
in a like time of leisure wrote a book entitled The 
Pilgrimage of the Mind to God, have essayed from the 
contemplation of creatures to make a ladder, by which 
we may in some sort ascend to God. And I have 
divided it into fifteen steps after the pattern of the 
fifteen steps of ascent unto the temple of Solomon, and 
of the fifteen psalms which are called Gradual, or Songs 
of Degrees. 


FIRST STEP 
CONSIDERATION OF MAN 
CHAPTER I 


General reasons for this Consideration. 


ne any one sincerely desires to raise a ladder by which 

he may reach to God, let him begin by considering 
himself. For each one of us is both the creature and 
the image of God, and nothing is nearer to us than 
ourselves. With good reason therefore does Moses 
say ‘ Give heed to thyself.’ On these words Basil the 
Great wrote a remarkable sermon. For if a man will 
look around him and consider what lies hidden within 
him, he will find the whole world, as it were in minia- 
ture, and thence he may easily rise up to the Creator 
of all things. 

But I have determined to examine, at the present 
time, only four general causes: viz. Who is my 
Maker ; The substance of which he made me ; The 
form he bestowed upon me; and The end for which 
he made me. 

Now, if I ask about my Maker, I find him to be 
God alone ; if I seek to know the substance of which 
he formed me, I find what is altogether nothing ; 
whence [ infer that all that is in me was made by God 
and that the whole of my being is of God. If I desire 
to know my form, I shall find that I am the image of 
God ; if I seek to know my end, I shall find the same 
God is himself to be my supreme and only good. 
And so I come to understand that my union with God 
and my need of him is great, that none but he is my 


CONSIDERATION OF Man | 7 


Creator, the Author of my being, that he is my Father, 
my pattern, my bliss, and all things. And if I come 
to understand this, is it possible that I shall fail to 
seek him with the greatest eagerness, to think of him, 
yearn after him, desire to see him and embrace him, 
or that I shall not shudder at the thick darkness of 
my heart which for so long a time has not thought 
on, or desired, or sought after God, who alone is all 
things to me? 


CHAPTER 2 
God our Maker. 


But let us consider each point carefully. I ask thee, 
my soul, who gave thee thy being : when a short 
time since thou wast nothing? Certainly thy natural 
parents did not form thee, for ‘ that: which is born of 
the flesh is flesh,’ and thou art spirit : not heaven or 
earth, nor the sun nor the stars produced thee, for 
they are bodies and thou art incorporeal : nor could 
angels or archangels or other creatures be the authors 
of thy being, for thou art not sprung from anything 
material, but thou wast created purely out of nothing. 
Therefore God alone without a co-worker, without 
assistant, with his own hands which are his understand- 
ing and will, gave thee being when he willed so to 
do. But perchance thou mayst say, it was not God 
himself but some created thing that produced my 
body, so that while the soul owes its being to God, 
the body ought to acknowledge its parents as its 
creators. Not so, but God, though making use of 
parents for the production of the fleshly nature, as the 
meanest servants are employed for the building of a 
house, is himself the builder, himself the creator, him- 
self the true father, not only of the soul, but also of 
the body, and he wills to be so called. 

And if the parents of thy fleshly nature were the 


8 ~~ ‘Tue Mrinp’s Ascent To Gop 


real authors of thy being, and, as it were, the builders 
of thy body, they would doubtless know the number 
of muscles in the human body, how many are the 
veins and nerves, how many larger bones, how many 
smaller, how much moisture and many other things of 
that kind, of which they are altogether ignorant, unless 
perchance they have learnt them by studying anatomy. 
Besides, when the body is sick, when limbs wither or 
are broken, if the parents were the real creators, they 
would certainly know how to restore by their own 
skill that which they, in the first instance, made : 
just as watch-makers know how to regulate watches, 
and builders know how to repair houses : but parents 
possess neither knowledge nor ability to do this, nor 
anything of the kind. 

Add to this, that the union of the soul with the body, 
which is a particular part of the active human nature, 
could be accomplished by none but an artificer of 
infinite power: for by what skill save divine could 
spirit be joined with flesh in a bond so close as to 
become one substance ? For the body hath no pro- 
portion to or likeness with the spirit. Therefore we 
know that he ‘who only doeth great wonders’ accom- 
plished it. Truly doth the Spirit of the Lord speak 
by Moses in the book Deuteronomy, ‘Is he not thy 
father that hath bought thee, hath he not made 
thee and established thee ?’” and by holy Job, ‘ Thou 
hast clothed me with skin and with flesh,’ and by the 
royal prophet, ‘ Thy hands have made me and fash- 
ioned me,’ and again, ‘Thou hast fashioned me and 
laid thine hand upon me,’ and by that very wise 
woman, mother of the Maccabees, ‘I neither gave 
you breath nor life, neither was it I that formed the 
members of every one of you; but doubtless the Creator 
of the world, who formed the generation of man, and 
found out the beginning of all things.’ 

t 2 Macc. vil. 22. 


CONSIDERATION oF Man 9 


The Wisdom of God, Christ our Lord, said, ‘ Call 
no man your father upon earth: for one is your Father, 
which is in heaven.’! And thus Saint Augustine 
addressed himself to God concerning his son Adeo- 
datus: ‘ Excellently hadst thou made him. I[ had no 
part in that boy but the sin.’ Well then, my soul, 
if God is the author of thy body and of thy soul, if 
he is thy father, thy sustainer, thy nourisher, then, if 
thou art anything, thou art his ; if thou hast anything 
it is from him thou hast it ; whatever thou hopest, 
from him thou hopest. Why dost thou not glory in 
such a Father ? Why dost thou not love him with 
all thine heart? Why dost thou not for his sake 
despise all earthly things ? Why dost thou suffer thy- 
self to be ruled by vain desires? Lift thine eyes to 
him ; fear not what thine enemy upon earth can do 
unto thee, when thou hast an Almighty Father in 
heaven! With what great trust and affection, thinkest 
thou, did David say, ‘I am thine, O save me.’3 O 
soul, if thou wouldst consider this, that the Almighty 
and everlasting God, who stands in no need of thy 
goods, and who, if thou perishest, loseth nothing, yet 
turneth not away his eyes from thee, and so loveth, 
protecteth, guideth, cherisheth thee as if thou wert 
his greatest treasure, indeed thou wouldst place thy 
whole trust in him, thou wouldst fear him as thy 
Lord, love him as thy Father, and there would be no 
temporal good or evil, however great, able to separate 
thee from his love. 


CHAPTER 3 


God created man out of nothing. 


Let us come to the substance out of which man was 
formed. 
It is indeed very mean, but the meaner it is, so 
1 St. Matt. xxiii.g. 2 St. Aug. Comf.ix.6. 3 Ps. cxix. 94. 


10 mote By k - Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


much the more doth it furnish a fitting ground for 
forming in us the virtue of humility : and there is 
nothing more profitable and more rare in this life than 
this virtue, and for that very reason there is nothing 
more precious or desirable. And indeed there is no 
doubt that the substance of the soul is itself nothing, 
and that nothing meaner or more worthless could be 

either formed or imagined. The substance out of 
_which the first man was made, what was it but red 
and barren earth, or dust and clay? Scripture saith, 
‘God formed man from dust of the ground ’;! and 
again, God saith to man, ‘ Dust thou art and unto 
dust thou shalt return.’? Wherefore the patriarch 
Abraham, remembering his baseness, said unto God, 
“Behold now, I have taken upon me to speak unto 
the Lord, which am but dust and ashes.’3 But even 
this is not the full extent of the baseness of the human 
substance, for clay and dust is not formed from another 
substance, but out of nothing. For ‘In the beginning 
God created the heaven and the earth,’ and indeed not 
out of another heaven and earth, but out of nothing 
did he make them, and so that from whence this proud 
animal called man was produced; comes to nothing- 
ness, whether you consider the soul or the body. 
Therefore there is nothing in man of which he can 
boast, as if he had not received it from God. Indeed 
the works of men, whatever they may be, whether 
produced by skill or labour, always possess something 
of themselves, concerning which, if they had sense, 
they might boast against their Maker. Thus a vessel 
of gold, a wooden chest, an ivory or marble house, 
could it speak, might say to the fashioner, ‘ To thee I 
owe my form, but not my substance, and that which 
I possess of myself is more precious than that which I 
have received from thee.’ But man, who has nothing 
from himself, or of himself, and is altogether nothing, 


tiGenin a. 2 ibid., iii. 19. 3 ibid., xvill. 27. 


CONSIDERATION OF Man II 


can boast in nothing. And very truly doth the Apostle 
say, ‘ For if a man think himself to be something, when 
he is nothing, he deceiveth himself’;* and, ‘ What 
hast thou that thou didst not receive P Now if thou 
didst receive it, why dost thou glory, as if thou hadst 
not received it?’ And Saint Cyprian agrees with 
him when he says, “We have nothing to boast of, 
since we possess nothing.’3 But thou sayest that men 
do many famous works and are deservedly praised on 
that account, in order that virtue, being praised, may 
increase. It is so, that men do many wonderful works, 
on account of which they may be praised and esteemed, 
but in the Lord, not in themselves, as it is written, ‘ He 
that glorieth, let him glory in the Lord’;4 and, ‘My 
soul shall make her boast in the Lord.’5 And when 
a man accomplishes a remarkable work, I inquire of 
what substance, with what power, by whose direction 
and assistance does he make it. Certainly he makes it 
out of a substance, and this substance God provided, 
and he did not produce it himself : he did the work by 
the direction and help of God, without whose direction 
and help man can do nothing good, because God 
worketh not as man worketh, as the second Council 
of Orange (a.D. 529) explains. And so it is fitting that 
God should employ the service of men for performing 
good deeds which he is able to do himself, so that man 
may confess himself on this account to be so much the 
more a debtor to God, and not in order that he may 
boast in himself, but that he may glory in the Lord. 

Therefore, my soul, if thou wilt be wise, lie down 
always in the lowest place, rob not God of his glory, 
whether in the smallest or the greatest matter, sink 
down into thine own nothingness, because that alone 
belongeth unto thee, and then the whole world will 
be powerless to lift thee up into pride.

12 ~ ‘Tue:-Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


But because this precious virtue of true humility 
had almost departed from the earth, and had no place 
in the books of the philosophers, nor in the customs 
of nations, the Teacher of humility came himself from 
heaven, and though being in the form of God and 
equal to the Father, ‘He emptied himself and took 
upon him the form of a servant, and humbled himself 
and became obedient even unto death,’! and said 
unto the human race, ‘ Learn of me, for I am meek 
and lowly of heart, and ye shall find rest unto your 
souls.” Wherefore, my soul, if perchance thou art 
ashamed to imitate the humility of men, be not ashamed 
to imitate the humility of God, who can neither deceive 
nor be deceived, and who ‘ resisteth the proud, but 
giveth grace to the humble.’3 


| CHAPTER 4 
Man was made in the image of God. 


WE are next to consider form, which is the third cause. 
And indeed the more unworthy the substance out of 
which man was made, so much the more precious and 
excellent do we find the form which was bestowed 
upon him. I pass by the exterior form, that is the 
shape of the human body which possesses a distinction 
of its own, superior to the forms of all creatures ; for 
this form is not a substantial but an accidental form. 
The substantial form of man which constitutes him a 
man, and which distinguishes him from the rest of 
animals, is the immortal soul, endowed with reason 
and free will, the image of God, modelled after the 
pattern of the highest Divinity. For thus we read 
that when God was pleased to make man, he spake 
thus, ‘ Let us make man in our image, after our like- 
ness : and let them have dominion over the fish of the 


1 Phil. ii.6. 2 St. Matt.xi.29. 3 St. Jas. iv. 6. 


CONSIDERATION OF Man Ties 


sea, and over the fowl of the air, and over the cattle, 
and over all the earth, and over every creeping thing 
that creepeth upon the earth.’! Man is then the image 
of God, not in respect of his body, but of his spirit ; 
for God is spirit, not a body ; and the image of God, 
says Saint Basil, is in the part which controls other 
living creatures.? 

Now man controls beasts, not by the bodily limbs 
which in many of the beasts are stronger than in man, 
but he controls them by the mind, which is endowed . 
with reason and free will. For man surpasses all living 
creatures, not by that which he possesses in common 
with them, but by that which distinguishes him from 
them, and in which he was made like unto God. My 
soul, lift up then thy mind to thy pattern, and con- 
sider any image, how all its goodness consists in its 
likeness to the original. For if perchance the pattern 
be misshapen, such as is the usual representation of 
the devil, then the value of the image is that it repre- 
sents accurately the deformed pattern ; and so deform- 
ity in the pattern will continue to be deformity, and 
deformity in the image will be its beauty. If the 
original be beautiful, the image will be very precious, 
that is to say, if it come up to the beauty of the original 
pattern as fully as possible ; and if the image possessed 
perception it would wish for nothing better than to be 
always attentive to its original, to imitate it, and to 
become as like it as possible. 

O soul, thy pattern is God, the nee beauty, light 
in whom is no darkness at all,3 at whose beauty both 
sun and moon are amazed. And in order that thou 
mayst more easily imitate the beauty of so great an 
original, and desire to copy it, thou wilt do well to 
seek in every way that in which thy whole perfection, 
all thy profit, all thine honour, joy, and rest, consist, 
and in short, that in which thy whole good stands, and 

1 Gen.i.26. 2 St. Basil,in Hexaem.hom.1o. 3 St. John.i. 5. 


I | Tue: Minp’s Ascent To Gop 
4 


consider that the beauty of God, who is thine example, 
doth consist in wisdom and holiness. For just as the 
beauty of the body is derived from the right proportion 
of the limbs and pleasing colouring, so in a spiritual 
being, for pleasing colouring there is the light of 
wisdom ; proportion of the limbs is righteousness ; 
and by righteousness is to be understood, not any 
particular virtue, but that general quality which com- 
prises all virtues. That spirit is therefore the most 
beautiful whose mind shines with the light of wisdom, 
and whose will is strong in the fullness of perfect 
righteousness. But, I say, God is thy pattern, O soul, 
wisdom itself, righteousness itself, and on this account 
he is beauty itself. And since both these good qualities 
are described in the Scriptures by the name of sanctity, 
in Isaiah the angels are said to cry, ‘ Holy, holy, holy, 
is the Lord God of hosts ’;1! and to those made in his 
own image God proclaims, * Be ye holy, for I the Lord 
your God am holy’;? and our Lord in the Gospel 
says, ‘ Be ye perfect, even as your Father which 1s in 
heaven is perfect.’ 3 

Therefore, O my soul, if thou dost desire to become 
the true image of God, and to be as like as possible to 
thy pattern, it behoves thee to love wisdom and right- 
eousness above all other things. True wisdom is to 
judge of all things according to their highest cause ; 
the highest cause is the divine will or law which mani- 
fests to men the will of God. If therefore thou lovest 
wisdom, it behoveth thee in all circumstances not to 
listen to what the law of flesh may prescribe, that which 
the senses judge to be good, that which the world 
approves, that which kinsfolk urge, and still less what 
flatterers suggest: but thou must be deaf to all these, 
and attend only to the will of thy Lord God; and 
thou shouldst always judge that to be useful, glorious, 
desirable, in short in every respect good, which is in 

I Isa. vi. 3. 2 Lev. xi. 44. 3 St. Matt. v. 48. 


CONSIDERATION OF Man 15 


agreement with the will and law of God. This is the 
wisdom of the saints, concerning which it is written, 
“I loved her above health and beauty, and chose to 
have her instead of light: for the light that cometh 
from her never goeth out. All good things together 
came to me with her.’! Furthermore, righteousness, 
which is the other part of spiritual beauty, comprehends 
all virtues which adorn and perfect the will, but 
especially charity, which is the mother and root of all 
virtues and of which Saint Augustine, in his last book 
on nature and grace, thus speaks, ‘ Love begun 1s 
righteousness begun, love increased is righteousness 
increased, perfect love is perfect righteousness ’; ? for 
he ‘ that loveth hath fulfilled the law,’ because ‘ love 
worketh no ill,’ and therefore ‘love is the fulfilling of 
the law,’ as the Apostle hath taught us. 3 

And again, ‘ Whoso keepeth his word,’ that is his 
commandments, ‘in him verily is the love of God 
perfected,’ as saith Saint John.4 ‘Those therefore who 
desire to become like unto the divine pattern, ought 
to obey him who says, “ Be ye imitators of God as dear 
children, and walk in love; ’5 for the Son is the image 
of the Father, and the whole good of an image is, as 
we have said above, to be very like its original. O 
soul, if thou didst fully understand this, and having 
become like unto thy pattern in the beauty of true 
wisdom and real holiness, wert pleasing in the eyes of 
the most high King, what peace wouldst thou enjoy, 
with what gladness wouldst thou exult ! How easily 
wouldst thou despise all the delights of the world ! 
And on the contrary, if thou didst consider how 
extremely displeased God is when he sees his image 
bereft of the light of wisdom and of the beauty of 
righteousness, defiled, disfigured, darkened, and man, 
who was placed in so great honour that he was like

unto God, now ‘ compared with the beasts that perish 
and become like unto them’! thou wouldst indeed 
shudder and tremble, and wouldst be unable in any

Thus far concerning form. 


CHAPTER § 


The intrinsic end of man ts the blessed vision 


of God. 


THERE remains the last, which we term the final cause. 

Now the end for which man was created is none 
other than the very God himself: but since the end is 
twofold, one within, the other without, we will shortly 
consider each separately. 

The intrinsic end of anything is the most perfect 
condition to which that thing can attain. Thus the 
intrinsic end of a palace is the finishing and perfection 
of the palace: for it is then said to be finished when 

¥ Psi xix. 32. 


a 


CONSIDERATION OF Man i 


there is nothing wanting of those things which the 
structure of the palace requires. The intrinsic end of a 
tree is that state of perfection which its nature requires: 
for the tree may then be said to have attained its end 
when it spreads its branches and produces leaves and 
is beautified with flowers, and a little later on, is laden 
with ripe fruit. And so man, who was created for the 
very highest end, may be said to have attained his end 
when his mind shall see God as he is: knowledge of 
all.things will follow this vision, and his will will 
enjoy that supreme good which he so ardently loved 
and desired, and the body, endowed with immortality 
and impassibility, and other glorious gifts, will become 
possessed of perpetual peace and joy. And since the 
essence of this final beatitude is the vision of God by 
which we, the very images of God—we ourselves, | 
say, shall attain to the perfect state and complete like- 
ness to our divine pattern, blessed John writes thus : 
‘ Now are we the sons of God, and it doth not yet 
appear what we'shall be: but we know that, when he 
shail appear, we shall be like him; for we shall see him 
as he 1s.’ ! 

O soul, couldst thou conceive what this is, ‘ We shall 
be like him, for we shall see him as he is,’ how 
quickly would all the clouds of earthly desire be dis- 
persed. God is supremely happy, and supremely 
happy because he always beholds himself as he is, 
and takes delight in himself the very highest good, 
most clearly seen and most ardently loved unceasingly, 
from all eternity. 

And this inestimable good he hath willed thee to 
share with the holy angels: for this most sublime and 
lofty end he created thee. This is the meaning of 
that word, ‘ Enter into the joy of thy Lord,’ 2 namely, 
be thou partaker of that joy in which God himself 
rejoices; and again, ‘I appoint unto you a kingdom, as

my Father hath appointed unto me; that ye may eat 
and drink at my table in my kingdom,’! that is, 
I will make you sharers of my kingdom and of my

and pleasure which I enjoy, and which God my Father 
enjoys. And who can think how great is the honour,

_ the happiness of the King of all kings, and Lord of all 
lords, our Lord God ? Indeed, one who raises up his 
thoughts and expectations to this great and high end, 
will be ashamed to strive after possession of the earth, 
or to be cast down on account of some loss of temporal 
things, or to rejoice over temporal gains: he will 
blush, I say, to regard with longing such pleasures 
as beasts eagerly hunt after, since he has been made a 
companion of angels, and partaker of the friendship 
of God, and a sharer in his inestimable benefits: for 
friends have all things in.common. 


CHAPTER 6 


The extrinsic end of man, the glory of God and 
his own salvation. 


Tue exterior end of anything is that one for whose 
sake the thing was made. ‘The end of a palace is the 
person who dwells in it, the end of a tree is its owner: 
the end of a man is only the Lord his God, for God 
not only made man, but made him for himself; he 
preserves him, feeds him, gives him his reward. So 
with good reason does he give a command and say, 
‘Thou shalt fear the Lord thy God and serve him.’ 2 
But, my soul, observe carefully, other things which 
were made for man are useful to man, not to them- 
selves; beasts of burden work for man, not for them- 
selves; fields, vineyards, gardens fill man’s stores, barns, 
purses, not their own; labour and sweat and fatigue

are the lot of servants, while to the master belong the 
gain, ease, and pleasure. But the Lord thy God, who 
is in need of nothing, doth indeed desire that man 
should serve him, but he willeth also that the profit 
and gain and reward of the service should belong to 
man. 

“O Lord, good and gracious and of great mercy,’ ! 
who, if he once begins to taste the sweetness of thy 
fatherly control or even a particle of it, will not serve 
thee with all his heart? What, O Lord, dost thou 
command to thy servants? ‘ Take,’ thou sayest, ‘my 
yoke upon you.’ What kind of yoke? ‘ My yoke,’ 
thou sayest, ‘is sweet and my burden 1s light.’ 
Who would not willingly bear a yoke which does not 
press but assists, and a burden which does not weigh 
down but refreshes ? Well indeed therefore dost thou 
add, ‘and ye shall find rest unto your souls.’ And 
what is this yoke of thine, which does not weary but 
brings rest? Is it not that first and great command-

to love goodness and beauty and love, all which indeed 
thou art, my Lord God? Rightly did David thy ser-

What is this, Lord ? Dost thou promise also a reward 
to those who keep thy commandments which in them- 
selves are far above gold and sweeter than honey-comb? 
Thus indeed dost thou promise reward and still further 
reward, as thine Apostle James saith, ‘ The Lord hath 
prepared a crown of life for them that love him.’ 5 
And what is this crown of life? It is something so 
great that we are not able either to conceive of it or to

imagine it. For thus saith Paul, quoting from Isaiah,

in keeping thy commandments there is great reward. 
And not only is that first and greatest commandment 
profitable to man who obeys, and not to God who

do perfect, adorn, instruct, enlighten the man who

if thou art wise, understand that thou wert created for 
the glory of God and for thine own eternal salvation, 
that this is thine end, this the centre of thy soul, this 
the treasure of thine heart. If thou attain this end, 
happy wilt thou be, but wretched if thou fall away from 
it. And so thou wilt consider that to be really good for 
thee which helps thee to thine end, and that to be 
really evil which causes thee to slip away from it. The 
wise man will not seek after nor flee away from pros- 
perity, wealth, health, honour, adversity, poverty, sick- 
ness, and dishonour ; but whatever leads on to the 
glory of God and his own everlasting happiness will 
be considered good and worth seeking after, while all 
those things which hinder these ends will be considered 
evil and such as to be avoided.

CHAPTER I 
The greatness of the world.and the greatness of God. 
\ \ J] E have made the first step in our ladder of ascen- 


sion to God to consist in the consideration of 
man, which is called the lesser world: now it is our 
purpose to raise above it a second step from considera- 
tion of that huge mass which we commonly call the 
greater world. Saint Gregory Nazianzus indeed writes 
in his second discourse on Easter, that God placed 
men, as it were, a great world within a less, which is 
true, if. we consider the angels apart from the world; 
for man, considered as part of the universe, is greater 
than the whole world of bodies, not in size but in 
power: but if the angels are included in the world, 
as here we understand it, then man 1s a little world 
placed within a greater world. Now in the great 
world which comprises the sum total of things, there 
are many most wonderful qualities, but notably these 
—greatness, multitude, variety, efficiency, and beauty. 
If we consider all these matters attentively in the light 
which God gives us, they will have great power to 
raise our soul so that we shall, as it were, faint in 
admiration of them, and then, returning to ourselves, 
we shall look upon everything outside God as naught, 

and shall consider it worthless. 
The earth certainly is large, very large, as the wise

man says, ‘Who can find out the height of heaven, and 
the breadth of the earth, and the deep?’! Andi its 
greatness may also be understood from the fact that 
in so many thousand years that have passed since the 
beginning of the world, the whole surface of the earth 
(and this is what the wise man calls the breadth) has 
not yet become known to us men, even to those who 
carefully explore it. And if I ask what is the size of 
the earth compared with the circuit of the highest 
heaven, astronomers answer, and not inaptly, ‘ No 
more than a point.’ For we see that the rays of the 
sun reach out to the stars at the opposite end of the 
y firmament as though no earth existed, though indeed 
it lies in the midst. And if it be, as is the general 
opinion of learned men, that every star in the firmament 
is larger than the whole globe of the earth, and yet 
these stars appear to be very small indeed on account 
of the enormous distance, who can conceive the extent 
of the sky in which so many thousand stars are 
shining? If then, in regard to the surface and the 
depth of the earth, the wise man said, ‘ The breadth 
of the earth and the depth of the abyss who can 
measure ?’ what would he say of the exterior surface 
of the sky and of the depth of the whole world from 
the highest part of the heavens to the lowest hell ? 
So very great is the bulk of this corporeal world, that 
no mind, no thought, can grasp it. Well then, my 
soul, seeing that the world is so great, how great 
is he that made it? ‘ Great is the Lord, and there is 
no end of his greatness.’ Hear Isaiah, ‘ Who hath 
measured the waters in the hollow of his hand, and 
meted out heaven with the span, and comprehended 
the dust of the earth in a measure ?’3 ‘Thus Saint

CHAPTER 2 


The number of created things and the infinite 
perfection of the Creator. 


Now who shall count the number of things created 
by one God the Maker of heaven and earth? ‘ Who 
can number the sand of the sea and the drops of rain ?’ 
saith the wise man.! 

But setting aside these very small things, how many 
metals are there, gold and silver, brass and lead, 
precious stones, gems, and pearls, both in the earth and 
in the sea? On the earth how many genera, species, 
and kinds of herbs, fruits, and plants, and how many 
partsineach ? What is the number of genera, species, 
and kinds of animals, perfect and imperfect, quadru- 
peds, reptiles, birds: how many genera, species, and 
kinds of fishes in the sea? Who can count them ? 
What of the races of men, of whom it is written, 
“The increase of man is according to thy secret 
counsel ’ P2 

Finally, ask how many stars there are in the sky? 
Concerning the stars we read in the Scriptures, which 
we know to be true, ‘ Tell the stars if thou canst 
number them.’3 And elsewhere it compares the num- 
ber of the stars with the sand of the sea. ‘ As the host 
of heaven cannot be numbered, neither the sand of 
the sea measured,’4 which is as much as to say 
they are countless. And of the angels Daniel writes, 
‘Thousand thousands ministered unto him, and ten 
thousand times ten thousand stood before him.’ 5 
And Saint Thomas, agreeing with Dionysius, afirms 
that the number of angels is so great that it surpasses 
the multitude of material things.® 

Thus the multitude of things which is wellnigh

infinite, created by one omnipotent God, shows that

some measure know him through his creatures, and 
because no single created being could fitly represent 
the infinite perfection of the Creator, he multiplied 
creatures, and bestowed on each a certain degree of

some idea of the goodness and perfection of the Creator, 
who in one most simple and perfect essence contains 
infinite perfections, somewhat after the manner in 
which a gold coin contains. the value of many copper

Therefore, my soul, whenever thy sight or thy mind 
is arrested by anything which seems worthy of admira-

risest to the knowledge of the perfection of thy Maker, 
which without doubt is far more great and wonderful. 
So will it come to pass, that created things, which, as 
Solomon teaches, are ‘a snare to the feet of the un-

and will not be the occasion of thy fall, but will form

to give himself to me, if I will despise these things. 
If kingdoms and earthly governments strike thee with

is the kingdom of heaven which abideth for ever, and 
which God, who cannot lie, hath promised to them that 
love him! If pleasures and delights begin to excite thy 
senses, say in thine heart: Spiritual delight is far more 
lovely than carnal delight, and delights of the mind 
are far more desirable than those of the body, for the 
latter are offered to us by a mortal creature, the former 
by the God of all consolation, and he who tasteth these

CHAPTER 3 


The variety of created things and God the source 
of all good. 


But although the multitude of created things is won- 
derful, and a proof of the multiform perfection of the 
one God, still more wonderful is the variety which 
appears in that multiplication, and it leads us more 
easily to the knowledge of God; for it is not difficult 
with one seal to make many impressions exactly alike, . 
or to cast numberless letters in the same mould, but 
to vary shapes almost infinitely, which is what God 
has done in creation, this is in truth a divine work 
and most worthy of admiration. I pass by genera 
and species, which every one agrees are exceeding 
various and diverse: in the separate forms of herbs, 
plants, flowers, and fruits what a great variety there 
is ! Shapes, colours, scent, taste, do not these vary 
almost infinitely ? - But what shall I say of man- 
kind; when in a large army, consisting of an immense 
number of men, scarcely two are found exactly alike ? 
It is the same with the stars and the angels; for ‘ one 
star differeth from another star in glory,’ as the Apostle 
testifies,2 and Saint Thomas affirms that although the 


1 2 Cor. vii. 4. a 1 Cor. xv. 41. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE GREATER WoRLD 27 


angels are more in number than corporeal beings, 
yet all are not only separate, but that all differ in their 
particular form. 

Raise now, my soul, the eye of thy mind towards 
God in whom are the ideas of all things, and from 
whom, as from an inexhaustible fountain, this wellnigh 
infinite variety springs: for God could not have im- 
pressed those numberless forms on created things un- 
less in a most eminent and exalted mode he had kept 
their ideas or patterns in the depth of his own Being. 
Therefore well does the Apostle exclaim, ‘O the 
depth of the riches of the wisdom and knowledge of 
God !’2 For truly fathomless must be the depth of 
that well, in which lie hidden the treasures of riches, 
of wisdom, of knowledge, able to produce such an 
infinite variety of things. 

Well also did Saint Francis, when alinerinered by 
the divine light, say unto the Lord, ‘ My God and my 
all,’ because the. various good things which are rarely 
found distributed among created things are found in 
altogether a higher and better way united in one God. 

But, my soul, thou mayst say, it may be that what 
you affirm be true, but the goodness of created things 
we see with our eyes, touch with our hands, taste with 
our mouths, we truly possess and enjoy: God we do 
not see, nor do we touch, nor taste, nor possess, and 
scarcely by thought can we apprehend him as we 
apprehend a thing placed at a great distance: and so 
it is not to be wondered at, if created things affect us 
more than God affects us. But, my soul, if thy faith 
is living and alert, thou must allow that after this life, 
which fleeth as a shadow, provided thou continue in 
faith and hope and love, thou shalt behold God himself, 
truly and clearly as he is in himself, and thou shalt 
possess him, and thou wilt enjoy him far better and 
more intimately than now thou art enjoying created

things. Hear the Lord himself, ‘ Blessed are the

Next I ask thee, what portion of this world belongs

that the creatures do not rule over thee, but thou over 
them, and that thou use them in such a manner as to 
find favour with God.

profitable: to have in this life such created things as 
are necessary, and in the next life to enjoy thy Creator 
himself, in whom, as I have repeatedly said, are all 
things, and that for ever and ever; or in this life to 
be eagerly labouring for the possession of temporal 
things and yet never to be satisfied, and at last to be 
suddenly bereft of all temporal goods and fall short 
of attaining to the eternal good things of eternity ?

his beloved ones but he bestows upon them, even in

and clean solid consolations to soothe their griefs, that 
temporal joys are in no way to be compared with them. 
Let this then, O my soul, be thy firm conviction: he 
who finds God finds all things; he who loses God 
loses all things. 


CHAPTER 4 


The ai Re implanted in created things and the 
Omnipotence of God. 


WE go on now to see how, by considering the power 
which God has bestowed upon created things, we may 
rise up to understand the infinite power of the Creator. 
There is nothing which is without a certain marvellous 
power, or force, or efficacy. Ifa lump of earth or a 
stone fall from a height, by the very force of its motion 
it works destruction, and anything resisting it is 
broken to pieces. When the Holy Spirit would des- 
cribe in the Apocalypse the tremendous force with 
which Babylon the Great, that is the whole company 
of the wicked, will be cast down into the depth of the 
abyss in the day of judgement, he tells us, ‘ And a 
mighty angel took up a stone like a great mill-stone, 


PUPS IXEVIe 4s AV.UIS, Wheat Se dXXKVis Ay A.Ver) in 2, COP. Vil. 4. 


30 ‘Tue: Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


and cast it into the sea, saying, Thus with violence 
shall that great city Babylon be thrown down, and shall 
be found no more at all.’ 3 

And water, which is smooth and soft, flows gently 
over the face of the earth, but when it is raging and 
foaming in rivers or torrents, it lays low and destroys 
everything in its path, not only cottages of the peas- 
antry, but even gates, and walls of cities, and marble 
bridges we have seen demolished by it. The winds, 
which are sometimes calm, and blow gently, may drive 
great ships on to rocks and uproot and overturn old 
oaks. I myself have seen a thing which I should 
not believe had I not seen it, an immense heap of earth 
cast up by a very strong wind, and the same heap 
carried to a certain village so that in the place where 
the earth had been cast up, there remained only an 
immense pit, and the whole village on which the earth 
fell was covered-and, as it were, buried. 

What shall we say about fire? How swiftly does 
a small fire become a flame great enough to bring 
houses and woods in a moment to nothing ! ‘° Behold,’ 
saith Saint James, “ how great a matter [wood, margin 
A.v.] a little fire kindleth.’ 2 

How many virtues do herbs contain ! In stones, 
and particularly in the loadstone and in amber, what 
wonderful powers do we perceive! Then in the 
animal world, we see some creatures very strong, as 
the lion, bear, bull, elephant ; others very clever, 
though very small, like the ant, the spider, the bee, 
the fly. Idonot now speak of the power of the angels, 
the virtue of sun and stars, which are far distant from 
us, but come to consider the wit of man, by which so 
many arts have been invented, that we are often in 
doubt whether nature yields the palm to art, or art to 
nature. 


Now, O soul, lift up the eyes of thy mind to God,

addresses thus: ‘ Who is like unto thee, O Lord, among 
the mighty ones ?’ ! and, ‘ Who only doeth marvellous 
things,’2 and, ° The blessed and only Potentate, the 
King of kings, and Lord of lords.’3_ For whatsoever 
strength created things possess, they derive from God, 
and they enjoy it as long as God pleases they should 
have it. For who but God caused that Jonah, shut 
up in the whale’s belly, should have no hurt from the 
waves of the sea, or the teeth of the whale ? Who but 
God shut the mouths of the hungry lions that they 
should not touch Daniel? Who but God preserved 
unhurt the Three Children in the burning furnace? 
Who said unto the stormy wind and to the raging sea, 
‘Peace be still,’ and immediately there was a great 
calm, who but Christ, the true God ? 

Now God derives not his strength and power from 
another, but his will is his power, anda power which 
none can withstand. He possesses always infinite 
power, and in comparison with it, all power of men is 
not merely small and mean, but it is altogether nothing: 
for thus saith Isaiah, ‘ All nations before. him are as 
nothing ; and they are accounted unto him less than 
nothing, and vanity.’ 4 

Are they not therefore foolish who fear created 
things and fear not God Almighty, and who trust in 
their own power or in that of their friends and not 
in God Almighty ? ‘If God be for us, who can be 
against us ?’5 and if God be against us who shall be 
forus? Wherefore my soul, if thou be wise, ‘ Humble 
thyself under the mighty hand of God.’® Cling to 
him with true devotion and thou shalt not need to 
fear what man or devil or any creature can do unto 
thee. But if perchance thou fallest from devotion, 


t Exod. xv. 11, marg.a.v. 7? Ps.cxxxvi.4. 3 1 Tim.vi.15. 
4 Isa. xl. 17. 5 Rom. vill. 31. 6 x St. Pet.v. 6.

and provokest thy God to anger, give thyself no rest 
until thou be at peace with him, for ‘ it is a fearful thing 
to fall into the hands of the living God.’ ! 


CHAPTER 6 


The created beauty of things and the uncreated 
beauty of God. 


Ir remains for us to consider the beauty of created 
things, of which the prophet saith, ‘ For thou, Lord, 
hast made me glad through thy works ’;2 and indeed 
as all things that God hath made are good, so also will 
they be found beautiful, if they be rightly regarded. 
But leaving other things aside, we will consider those 
things which the opinion and judgement of all men 
allow to be beautiful. In truth, great is the beauty of 
a green field, of a well-kept garden, of a pleasant wood, 
of a calm sea, of serene air, of springs, of rivers, of 
cities, of the glittering heavens bedecked with count- 
less stars shining like jewels. Then how delightful 
the beauty of a tree clothed with flowers, or laden with 
fruit, the forms of various four-footed creatures, the 
flight of birds, the play of fishes ! What shall I say 
of the beauty of the stars and of the moon, and still 
more of that very great and notable splendour of the 
sun, which at its rising animates the whole earth ? 

But nothing delights men more than the beauty of 
their own form, and it is to men that we are particularly 
speaking. We have often seen and grieved over, in- 
telligent men, enamoured of the beauty of women, and 
on the other hand, grave and honourable women led 
to foolishness by reason of the beauty of men, so that 
they put home, honour, children, and parents, life 
itself, and I might even say their eternal salvation, 
second to their love of beauty in men. These are plain 


1 Heb. x. 31. 2 Ps, xcli. 4. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE GREATER WORLD 33 


facts read in Holy Scripture in regard to David, Solo- 
mon, and Samson, and history is full of like examples. 

Now my soul, if such beauty has been bestowed upon 
creatures by God, their Creator, how great and how 
marvellous must be the beauty of the Creator himself, 
the All-beautiful ! For no one can give to another 
what he does not possess, and if men were so charmed 
with the form of the sun and stars, that they mistook 
those luminous bodies for gods, Wisdom saith, ‘ Let 
them know how much better the Lord of them is, for 
the first author of beauty hath created them.’* But 
the greatness of the beauty of God is learnt not only 
from the fact that the beauty of all creatures is found 
gathered together in a far higher degree in him, but 
also from this, that though he is invisible to us and 
only known by our belief in the Scriptures, and in the 
mirror of created things, so long as we are wandering 
apart from him, yet many of the saints have been so 
ardent in their love of him that some even hid them- 
selves in desert places and gave themselves up to the 
contemplation of him alone: such were Saint Mary 
Magdalene, Paul the first hermit, Anthony the Great, 
and others without number, concerning whom Theo- 
doret’s religious history may be consulted ; others, 
leaving wives and children and whatever possessions 
they had in the world, chose to live in monasteries, 
subject to the will of others, that they might enjoy the 
friendship of God; others again desired to spend 
their life freely in sharp pain, that they might be worthy 
to attain to the vision of his infinite beauty. Hear 
one of these, the holy martyr Ignatius, in his letter to 
the Romans: ‘Come fire and cross and conflicts with 
wild beasts, wrenching of bones, mangling of limbs, 
crushing of the body ; come grievous torments of 
the devil upon me—only may they aid me in attain- 
ing unto Jesus Christ.’ 2 

1 Wisd. xiii. 3. 2 Ignat. Ep. ad Rom. 5, trans. J. H. Srawley, D.D. 

D 


34 Tur Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


If then the divine beauty, not yet seen, but only 
believed and hoped for, stirreth up such a great fire 
of longing, what will it do when the veil is removed, 
and it shall be seen as it is in itself? This will 
happen, that, drinking of his pleasures as out of a 
river,! we shall neither wish to, nor shall we be able 
to, turn our eyes away from it for one moment. And 
~ is it any wonder that to angels and blessed spirits, who 
always behold the face of the Father who is in heaven, 
the vision brings neither weariness nor satiety, since 
God himself, always beholding his own beauty, hath 
been eternally satisfied with it? And as if into a wine 
cellar and into a garden of all manner of delights, 
a man shall enter, and never thence depart, for all 
eternity. 

Seek then this beauty, O my soul, sigh after it day 
and night: say with the prophet, ‘ My soul is athirst 
for God, when shall I come to appear before the living — 
God?’2 Say with the Apostle, ‘ We are confident and 
willing rather to be absent from the body, and to be 
present with the Lord.’ 3 

And fear not defilement from the fervent love of that 
beauty; for the love of divine beauty does not enfeeble 
the heart, but perfects it, sanctifies and does not injure 
it. The holy virgin and martyr Agnes truly said, ‘I 
love Christ, whose Mother is a virgin, whose Father 
knew not woman; when I love him I am chaste, when 
I touch him I am pure, when I receive him I am a 
virgin.’ 4 

But if thou desirest indeed the uncreated beauty of 
thy Lord, it behoves thee to fulfil what the Apostle 
subjoins : ‘ Wherefore,’ saith he, ‘we labour, that, 
whether present or absent, we may be accepted of 
him.’5 If God is pleasing to thee, thou oughtest to 
be pleasing to God. And indeed in the land of the

4 Prudentius. 5.2 Cor. v: 9. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE GREATER WORLD 35 


living, when we shall be in his presence, enlightened 
with his brightness, without doubt we shall be pleasing 
to God, as the prophet says, ‘ I will please God in the 
land of the living’; but in this pilgrimage we so 
easily become smirched and stained by the mire of 
sin; as the Apostle James saith, ‘In many things we 
offend all’; 2 and the prophet David, to show how few 
are undefiled in the way, pronounced a beatitude on 
the attainment of that state, ‘ Blessed are those that 
are undefiled in the way.’3 _ 

Therefore, O soul, if while absent from thy beloved 
and on thy pilgrimage, thou art desirous of pleasing 
him, it is not enough to wish to please him, but thou 
must, as the Apostle says, strive to please him, that 1s, 
with earnest care labour to avoid stains which are a 
blemish on the soul, and if any should cling to it, with 
no less zeal and labour to wipe them away. Do but 
see the young women who desire to please their hus- 
bands, how many hours they spend in arranging their 
hair, in powdering their faces, and removing every 
trace of stain from their clothes that they may give 
pleasure to the eye of a mortal man, who must shortly 
be turned to dust and ashes. What therefore shouldst 
thou do to please the eye of thine immortal spouse, 
who is always beholding thee, and who desires to find 
thee without spot or wrinkle ? Certainly it is necessary 
for thee to strive with all thy might, ‘ to walk in holi- 
ness and righteousness before him,’4 and carefully 
to remove everything which hinders true holiness and 
righteousness, and cut it off: nor to have regard to 
flesh and blood, nor to heed the words or judgements 
of men: for thou canst not at the same time please God 
and the world: as the Apostle saith, ‘If thou wilt 
please men thou art not the servant of Christ.’ 5
