THIRD STEP 
CONSIDERATION OF THE WORLD 


CHAPTER I 


That as our body finds its rest in the world, so our mind 
can rest in God alone. 


E, have considered the world as a whole; now 

let us pass on to its chief divisions, that from 
these we may raise a ladder such as it is possible to 
make, which will bring us to the contemplation of its 
Maker. } 

The first is earth, which although holding the lowest 
place and appearing less than the other elements, yet 
is in reality not inferior to water, and indeed in dignity 
and value it surpasses all the elements. Hence we 
read in the holy Scriptures that God made heaven and 
earth, as if to say they were the chief parts of the world, 
to which all the rest minister: for he made heaven, as 
it were, the palace of God and of the angels, and the 
earth the palace of man.. ‘ The heaven of heavens is 
the Lord’s and the earth hath he given to the children 
of men,’ saith the prophet.t And this is the reason 
why the heaven is full of shining stars and the earth 
abounds in stores of metals, precious stones, herbs, 
trees, and many kinds of animals: while the water 
abounds in fish alone, and the air and fire are elements 
barren and wellnigh empty. But these facts apart, 
there are three things in the earth particularly worthy 
of consideration, and if we approach to consider them 


t Ps. cxv. 16. 


36 


CONSIDERATION OF THE WoRLD a7 


with watchful mind we shall without difficulty be able 
to rise by means of them, to God. 

First, the earth is the very first foundation of the 
whole world, and if we had not this foundation man 
could neither walk, nor be at rest, nor work, nor spend 
his life in any kind of way. 

“He hath made the round world so sure,’ saith 
David, ‘that it cannot be moved.’! ‘ He laid the 
foundations of the earth that it never should move.’ 2 

Secondly, the earth, as a good nurse to man and 
living creatures, with great care brings forth herbs, 
fruits, seeds, berries, and countless other things of the 
kind. For thus God spake, ‘ Behold, I have given 
you every herb bearing seed which is upon the face of 
the earth and every tree in which is the fruit of a tree 
yielding seed, to you it shall be for meat, and to every 
beast of the earth.’ 3 

Thirdly, the earth produces stones and wood for 
building houses, and metals of brass and iron for 
various purposes, and gold and silver, of which is 
coined money, a convenient means for gathering to- 
gether-all that is necessary for the life of man. 

And that primary property of earth, namely that it 
is the place in which our bodies rest, which they cannot 
do either in water or air or fire, is a symbol of the 
Creator, in whom alone the human soul can find a place 
of rest: ‘ Thou hast made us for thyself, O Lord,’ says 
Saint Augustine, ‘and our heart is unquiet until it 
rest in thee.’4 King Solomon, if any one did, sought 
rest in his government, in riches, in pleasures : he 
maintained his extensive kingdom in peace, so that, 
as Scripture witnesseth, ‘ Solomon reigned over all the 
kingdoms from the river unto the land of the Philistines, 
and unto the border of Egypt: they brought presents, 
and served Solomon all the days of his life.’ 5 He had 


mE PS XCin). 2; a Ps. civ. 5. SATE te 20 308 
4 Conf.i. 1. 5 x Kings iv. 21. 


38 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


besides countless riches: forty thousand stalls of horses 
for his chariots and twelve thousand horsemen: and 
as we read in the same book, the fleet of Solomon 
brought gold from Ophir and precious gems in such 
quantities that silver was accounted of no value and 
was as plentiful in Jerusalem as stones in the streets. 
The number of pleasures he procured for himself 
seems incredible; but let us hear him speak himself: 
‘I made me great works; I builded me houses; | 
planted me vineyards; I made me gardens and or- 
chards, and I planted trees in them of all kind of 
fruits: I made me pools of water, to water there- 
with the wood that bringeth forth trees: I got me 
servants and maidens, and had servants born in my 
house; also I had great possessions of great and 
small cattle above all that were in Jerusalem before 
me: I gathered me also silver and gold, and the 
peculiar treasure of kings and of the provinces: | 
gat me men singers and women singers, and the de- 
lights of the sons of men, as musical instruments, 
and that of all sorts. So I was great, and increased 
more than all that were before ‘me in Jerusalem ; 
also my wisdom remained with me. And whatsoever 
mine eyes desired | kept not from them, I withheld 
not my heart from any joy; for my heart rejoiced in 
all my labour: and this. was my portion of all my 
labour.’! These words were written by the man who 
possessed rest in the highest degree, if rest is to be 
had in created things. For nothing was wanting to 
him—kingdom, riches, or pleasures, not even that 
which is reckoned of the very highest value, namely 
human wisdom, and there was not even lacking peace 
and quietness in the possession and retaining such 
good things for a length of time. Let us then ask 
whether he found rest in these things and if they were 
able to satisfy the capacity of his soul: he says, ‘ Then 
t Eccles. ii. 4-10. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE WoRLD 39 


I looked on all the works that my hands had wrought, 
and on the labour that I had laboured to do: and 
behold, all was vanity and vexation of spirit, and there 
was no profit under the sun.’! And so Solomon found 
not rest in these great riches, pleasure, wisdom, and 
honours, nor could he have found it had they been far 
more in number and much greater, for the human 
soul is immortal, and these things are mortal, and 
cannot abide long under the sun: nor indeed could it 
ever come to pass that the mind of man, capable of 
infinite good, should be satisfied with finite good things. 
For just as the human body cannot rest in the air how- 
ever open, nor in water however deep, because its 
centre is not air or water but earth: so the human soul 
cannot rest in aerial dignities, worthless riches, or in 
watery, that is soft and sordid, pleasures, nor in the 
false glory of human knowledge; but it can rest in God 
alone, who is the centre of souls, and the true and only 
place of their rest. 

O how true and how wise was the father of Solo- 
mon when he said, “ Whom have I in heaven but 
thee, and what is there upon earth that I desire in 
comparison of thee? For thou art the God of my 
heart and my portion for ever’;2 as if he would 
say: I find nothing in heaven, in earth, nor in any 
created thing, whether under heaven or upon the earth, 
able to bring me true rest: thou alone art the God of 
my heart, that is, thou alone art the solid rock for my 
heart—for the word ‘God’ in the Hebrew text in 
this place signifies “rock’—thou only art the most firm 
rock for my heart, in thee alone will I rest, thou alone 
art my portion, mine inheritance, all my good. All 
other things are nothing, and nothing can fill and 
satisfy me, and this not for one or two days or a year, 
but for ever. Thou alone sufficest me for ever, all 
other things fail to satisfy even for one day. 


t Eccles. ii. 11. 2 Ps. xxiii. 25. 


40 Tur Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


Dost thou not learn then, my soul, that God alone 
is thy rock, in whom thou canst rest, that all other 
things are vanity and vexation of spirit, which indeed 
have no existence, but only appear to have, and do not 
afford solace, but only affliction, since they are gained 
by labour and with grief held in possession ? 

Therefore, if thou art wise, despise all things that 
pass, lest they carry thee away with them: and abide 
in one alone and cleave in a bond of love, to him who 
abideth for ever. Lift thine heart to God in heaven, 
lest it become corrupt upon earth; learn true wisdom 
from the folly of many, in whose name Wisdom 
speaketh and saith: ‘ Therefore have we erred from 
the way of truth, and the light of righteousness hath 
not shined unto us, and the sun of righteousness rose 
not upon us: we have gone through deserts where 
there lay no way: but as for the way of the Lord, we 
have not known it: we wearied ourselves in the way 
of wickedness and destruction. What hath pride pro- 
fited us, and what good hath riches with our vaunting 
brought us? All those things are passed away like a 
shadow and we were consumed in our own wicked- 
ness.’ ! 


CHAPTER 2 
We are safely grounded in God. 


Bur there is another reason why the firm rock is a 
symbol of our Lord, which the Wisdom of God ex- 
plained to us in his Gospel, when he said that a house 
built upon a sure rock remains immovable, if the rain 
falls upon it from above and the winds blow upon it 
from the side and rivers rush upon it from beneath: 
while a house built upon the sand can stand against 
none of these things, but at the first onset of rain or 
wind or flood, it is cast down and the ruin of that house 


1 Wisd. v. 6. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE WoRLD AI 


is great! Thine own house, O soul, whose rooms 
and halls consist of various powers and virtues, will be 
secure if it be thus founded upon God, that is, if thou 
believest firmly in God, if thy whole trust is in God, if 
thou art rooted and grounded in the love of God, so 
that with the Apostle thou canst say, ‘ Who shall 
separate us from the love of Christ ?’; 2 and be assured 
that neither spiritual wickednesses which are above 
us, nor fleshly desires which are beneath us, nor the 
enemies of our household, namely relations and friends, 
which attack from every side, will ever prevail against 
thee with their scheming. The strength of spiritual 
powers is indeed great and their craftiness also, but 
greater is the power and greater the wisdom of the 
Holy Spirit, who keeps guard over that house which is 
builded in God. ‘The flesh fighteth powerfully against 
the spirit, and fleshly desires continue to attack the 
strongest ; but the love of God easily conquers carnal 
love and the fear of God easily casts down the fear of 
the world. 

Lastly, a man’s foes are they of his own household,3 
and these by their advice draw souls into the company 
of sinners: but the soul who believes that she hath 
in the Lord, father, brother, and spouse in heaven, 
not only easily despises carnal friends and relations, 
but also knows how to hate them, and can say with 
the Apostle, ‘I am persuaded that neither death nor 
life, nor angels nor any other creature, shall be able to 
separate me from the love of God, which is in Christ 
Jesus our Lord.’4 But the wretched soul whose house 
is built upon the sand cannot long abide, and in a short 
time the ruin of that house will be great, since it 
believes a lie and trusts in a reed for a staff, and its 
god is either the belly or money, or vapour of honour 
which all very quickly pass away and perish, and drag 
to everlasting destruction the soul that clings to them.

CHAPTER 3 


Earth nourishes the body by the power of God, who 
alone 1s the support of the whole man. 


Now a second property of earth is this, that, as a good 
nurse, she produces liberally herbs and fruits to be the 
‘food of men and other animals. And this very property 
leads us to the Founder and the true nourisher: for it 
is not the earth in itself, but God who produces all 
good things in the earth. For thus saith the Holy 
Spirit by the mouth of David, ‘ These wait all upon 
thee, that thou mayst give them meat in due season. 
When thou givest it them they gather it and when 
thou openest thy hand they are filled with good’; * 
and the Lord in the Gospels saith, ‘ Behold the fowls 
of the air: for they sow not, neither do they reap, nor 
gather into barns, yet your heavenly Father feedeth 
them’; 2 and the Apostle, “ Nevertheless God left not 
himself without witness, in that he did good, and gave 
us rain from heaven, and fruitful seasons, filling our 
hearts with food and gladness.’3' And that is true 
which is said in the beginning of the book Genesis, 
‘ Let the earth bring forth grass, the herb yielding 
seed, and the fruit tree yielding fruit after his kind.’ 4 
For indeed the earth doth bring forth grass and trees 
which bear fruit, but by the power bestowed upon it by 
God, by which very power God himself produces and 
preserves and gives the increase. 

And so when David invites all created things to 
praise the Creator, he joins to the company ‘ fruitful 
trees and all cedars’;5 and the three children with 
Daniel are exhorted with all the rest of creation, all 
the things that grow on the earth, to bless the Lord 
and ‘ praise him and exalt him above all for ever.’ 6 


I Ps. civ. 27. 2 St. Matt: vi. 26. 3 Acts xiv. 17. 
4 Gen.i. 11. 5 Ps. cxlvili.g. © Song of the Three Children. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE WorLD 43 


And since all things praise God after this manner, 
with how great affection oughtest thou, O soul, to bless 
and praise God for all the benefits which thou enjoyest 
so freely, beholding in them the hidden hand of God 
bestowing all things, and the love of thy God not 
hidden, but plainly to be seen showing itself most pure 
and paternal, never ceasing to bless thee from heaven 
and to provide for thee in all things. But this is little 
in the eye of thy Lord God: for it is he who in thee, 
as in his own spiritual field, brings forth the most 
splendid fruit of love. For love is not of the world, 
but it is of God, as the beloved disciple says in his 
Epistle: From charity, as from a divine and heavenly 
tree, are produced the white and sweet-scented flowers 
of holy thoughts, verdant leaves of words profitable to 
man’s salvation, fruits of good works, by which God 
is glorified and the neighbour assisted, merits are 
acquired and preserved unto life eternal. 

But woe to those who, after the manner of senseless 
beasts, desire satisfaction from the fruits of the earth 
and eagerly collect them and hide them away, thinking 
not of their Maker nor giving him thanks. The souls 
of such are like the earth which God cursed and which 
brought forth thorns and thistles. For when God is 
not sowing the seed of holy thoughts, what can fill 
their minds but murders, thefts, treachery, and other 
things of that kind, and what is their speech but of 
blasphemy, perjury, cursing, heresies, banquets, con- 
tumely, false witnessing, lying, and such things learnt 
from their father the devil ? And what fruit do they 
at last produce but those poisonous fruits which we 
have said are eagerly pondered by them and which 
the Apostle calls ‘ works of the flesh’?2 ‘These are 
indeed thorns which at first pierce the minds of those 
who produce them, with the sharp pricking of fears 
and cares, then they pierce the fair repute, the bodies 


t x St. John iv. 7. 2 Gal. v. 1g. 


Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 
44 


and minds of neighbours, making serious and often 
incurable bruises which afterwards bring grievous harm 
to others. 

But passing by these things, do thou, my soul, if 
thou art a little garden belonging to the heavenly 
husbandman, beware lest thorns and briars be found 
in thee, but with all care cherish the tree of charity, 
- the lily of chastity, the spikenard of humility: beware 
lest any one persuade thee to think that these heavenly 
seeds of virtues spring from thyself and not from thy 
Lord God, who is the Lord of virtues, the inspirer of 
holy purposes; but see that thou attribute the preser- 
vation and increase and perfection of the fruit of good 
works not to thyself, but to him, and let thy strength 
be always in him. 


CHAPTER 4 


The treasures of the earth are as it were a shadow of 
the eternal good things of God. 


THERE remains the last glory of the earth, namely that 
it contains within itself, gold, silver, and precious 
stones. But in truth the earth does not produce these 
precious things by its own power, but he produces 
them, who speaks by Hageai, ‘ The silver is mine and 
the gold is mine.’! O thou lover of men, is this not 
pleasing to thy loving-kindness, that not only were 
stones, wood, iron, brass, lead, and other kinds of things 
which are necessary for building houses, ships, and 
various instruments produced from the earth, but also 
gold and silver and precious stones for adorning and 
beautifying them ? And if thou bestowest these things 
on those who wander about the earth and often even 
upon thine enemies who blaspheme thy Name, what 
wilt thou give to thy beloved ones, blessing thee and 
reigning with thee in heaven? Thou wilt give, not 
t Hag. il. 9. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE WoRLD 45 


a small piece of silver, nor tiny precious stones, but 
that city of which the Apostle John speaks in the 
Apocalypse, saying, ‘ And the building of the wall of 
it was of jasper, and the city was pure gold, and the 
foundations of the wall of the city were garnished with 
all manner of precious stones, and the twelve gates 
were twelve pearls.’! _ 

But we are not to think that the heavenly Jerusalem, 
the city that is above, is adorned or builded with gold 
and gems and pearls belonging to this earth: we know 
that these words are used by the Holy Spirit in speak- 
ing to us because we are unable to perceive the better 
and greater things; but most certainly that land which 
is the home of the elect of God will be far more beautiful 
than all the cities of this pilgrimage, just as a city all 
gold and precious stones surpasses the village of huts 
of rustics, which are built of mud and straw. 

Raise therefore, my soul, the eyes of thy mind to 
heaven, and think how highly the good things therein 
should be valued, since the gold, silver, and precious 
stones which here are so highly regarded,. when com- 
pared with them, are scarcely worthy of the name of 
mud or straw. Add to which that the gold, silver, and 

earls which we consider so valuable, are perishable, 
while those which glitter in the heavenly city yonder 
are incorruptible and everlasting. 

But if by the hands of the poor thou wilt lay up in 
the heavenly city this same corruptible gold and silver, 
which if thou art wise thou wilt surely do, it will be- 
come incorruptible and will remain with thee always. 
For the Truth, who cannot lie, saith, ‘ Sell what thou 
hast and give to the poor, and thou shalt have treasure 
in heaven ’;2 and in another place, ‘ Sell that ye have 
and give alms: provide yourselves bags which wax 
not old, a treasure in the heavens that fadeth not, where 
no thief approacheth nor moth corrupteth.’ 3 

t Apoc. xxi. 18. 7 St. Matt. xix. 21. 3 St. Luke xii. 33. 


46 Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


O unbelief of the sons of men! A liar promises 
ten for a hundred, and that he will repay to a lender 
the whole sum of money, and he is believed: God, who 
cannot lie, promises to him who bestows alms treasure 
in heaven, and to give even more than a hundredfold, 
and life eternal: the avaricious man is fearful and 
_ cannot be persuaded to believe it, and would rather 
hide his treasure where rust consumes it and where 
thieves break in and steal, than lay it up in heaven 
where neither thief steals nor rust consumes. 

But, O unhappy man, even if thieves do not rob, 
nor moth or rust corrupt, to whom will belong those 
things which thou hast gathered together with such 
great labour? ‘Thine, for certain, they will not be, 
and yet they might have been thine if thou hadst laid 
them up in the heavenly treasury by the hands of the 
poor. For experience shows that the things which 
avaricious persons collect fall into the hands of spend- 
thrifts who divide, and in a very brief time scatter, 
what their avaricious parents have gathered together, 
and meanwhile the sin of avarice remains and will 
remain for ever and the worm of conscience dieth not 
and the fire of hell is not quenched.t_ Therefore, my 
soul, learn by the follies of others and hear thy Lord 
and Master crying to thee, “ Take heed, and beware of 
covetousness : for a man’s life consisteth not in the 
abundance of the things which he possesseth.’ The 
covetous man gathers and hoards that he may have 
wherewith to live a long life, but the contrary happens, 
for when he little thinks it, he dies, and his substance 
so eagerly gathered together and hoarded up produces 
the worm which dieth not and kindles the fire which 
is not quenched. O unhappy, covetous man, didst 
thou so carefully amass money in order to prepare fuel 
for the fire of hell, so that it should never go out? 
Hear the blessed James in the last part of his Epistle: 

t St. Mark ix. 48. 2 St. Luke xii. 15.

‘Go to now, ye rich men, weep and howl for your 
miseries that shall come upon you. Your riches are 
corrupted, and your garments are moth-eaten. Your 
gold and your silver is cankered; and the rust of them 
shall be a witness against you, and shall eat your flesh as 
it were fire.’ ! 

Ye indeed, saith blessed James, because ye are rich, 
think yourselves happy, and ye are called so: but in 
truth ye are wretched and more wretched than all 
needy persons, and you have great cause for mourning 
and lamenting on account of the great miseries which 
will certainly come upon you: for the superfluous riches 
which you held fast and which you suffered to become 
rotten when you should have bestowed them upon the 
poor, the superfluous garments which you had and 
which you preferred to see eaten by the moth rather 
than covering the poor, and the gold and silver which 
you preferred to see worn away by rust rather than 
spent upon food for the poor, all these things, I say, 
will bear witness against you in the day of judge- 
ment, and the moths and rust of your riches shall be 
turned into burning fire which shall forever be devour- 
ing your flesh and never consume it, the fire not 
quenched and the grief never ended. ‘Therefore let 
us conclude with the royal prophet: fools have said, 
* Happy are the people that are in such a case,’ namely 
having riches in abundance; but in truth, ‘ Blessed 
are the people who have the Lord for their God.’ 2

FOURTH STEP 


CONSIDERATION OF WATERS AND 
CHIEFLY OF FOUNTAINS 


CHAPTER I 


W ater cleanseth bodily stains ; God cleanseth 
spiritual. 


Me tes holdeth the second place among the 
elements: and if rightly looked upon, a step 
may be made of it to assist the heart’s ascent towards 
God. And first let us consider water in general, and 
afterwards the particular ascent towards God which 
we learn from wells. Water is moist and cold, and on 
this account it has, as it were, five properties: it 
washes and wipes away stains; it quenches fire; re- 
freshes and tempers the heat of thirst; joins many 
and different things into one whole; and lastly, so low 
as it sinks, so high will it rise again. All these proper- 
ties are plain symbols and footsteps of that God who is 
the Creator of all things. - 

Water washes off bodily stains ; God washes off 
those that are spiritual. ‘Thou shalt wash me,’ says 
David, ‘and I shall be whiter than snow.’ For though 
we say contrition cleanses the heart from sin, that is 
from stain, and sacraments, priests, almsgiving, and 
other works of piety, cleanse : yet all these are either 
the instruments or dispositions, and the author of the 
cleansing is God alone. ‘ I, even I, am he that blotteth 
out thy transgressions for mine own sake.’! And so 
the Pharisees, who murmured against Christ, said, 

t Isa, xlii. 25. 
48 


CONSIDERATION OF THE WATERS 49 


‘Who can forgive sins but God alone ?’’! They were 
not mistaken in attributing to God alone the supreme 
power of forgiving sins, but they were wrong in not 
believing Christ to be God, and therefore they were 
blaspheming and speaking truth at the same time. 
And not only does God cleanse the stains of the heart 
after the manner of water, but he wills to be called 
by the Name: for thus writes Saint John: ‘ He that 
believeth on me, as the Scripture hath said, out of his 
belly shall flow rivers of living water. But this spake 
he of the Spirit, which they that believe on him should 
receive: for the Holy Ghost was not yet given; because 
that Jesus was not yet glorified.’ Therefore the Holy 
Spirit, who is indeed God, is the living water, and of 
this water Ezekiel says, ‘I will sprinkle clean water 
upon you, and ye shall be clean from all your filthi- 
ness.’ 3 

And since that heavenly uncreated water far surpasses 
in power created and earthly water, we will note three 
differences between the washing of created and un- 
created water. First, created water washes the stains 
of the body, but not all: for there are many which it 
is powerless to cleanse without the help of soap or 
other instruments; but uncreated water entirely cleanses 
all stains ; thus we read in a passage shortly before 
that quoted above, ‘ And ye shall be cleansed from all 
your iniquities.’ Secondly, created water seldom so 
Wipes away all stains as to leave no trace or mark of 
the stain; but uncreated water so cleanses that the 
thing cleansed is made more shining and beautiful 
than it was before it was defiled. ‘ Thou shalt wash 
me,’ saith David, ‘ and I shall be whiter than snow ’; 4 
and the Lord saith by Isaiah, ‘ Though your sins be as 
scarlet, they shall be as white as snow; though they 
be red like crimson, they shall be as wool.’5 And 

1 St. Luke v. 21. 2 St. John vu. 38. 3 Ezek. xxxvi. 25. 

Ae SOT ee 5 Isa.i. 18.

lastly, created water cleanses natural stains which do 
not withstand the person who cleanses; but uncreated 
water washes away voluntary stains which cannot be 
washed unless the soul itself be willing, and yields 
willingly to the cleanser. But so great and admirable 
is the virtue of this water that it sweetly penetrates 
_ hearts of stone: and it is resisted by no hardened 
heart, but it makes it unable to resist, as Saint Augus- 
tine aptly reminds us. 

Who can understand, O Lord, how wonderfully 
thou dost breathe faith into the hearts of the unbeliey- 
ing, and pourest humility into the hearts of the proud, 
and instillest charity into the hearts of thine enemies, 
so that a man who a short time before was breathing 
threats and slaughter and persecuting thee in the 
persons of thy disciples, is suddenly changed, and for 
thy sake and for thy Church willingly bears the threats 
and blows of persecutors ?! It is too much for me to 
penetrate into these thy secrets and I had rather know 
the efficacy of thy grace by experience than by search. 
And as I know that that water of thine is a gracious 
rain, refreshing thine inheritance, as thy prophet sang,? 
I would humbly beseech thee that I may be found in 
thine inheritance and that it may please thee to cause 
thy grace to descend upon the soil of my heart, that it 
continue not as a land without water, dry and barren 
before thee, such as it is in itself, and unable even to 
think anything good. But let us pass on to the rest. 


CHAPTER 2 


Water quenches fire: God quenches the flame 
of evil desires. 


Water quenches fire, and that heavenly water, namely 
the grace of the Holy Spirit, in a wondrous manner 
quenches the fire of carnal desires. Fasting and afflict- 


t Acts ix. I. 2 Ps, lxviii. 9. 


CoNSIDERATION OF THE WATERS 51 


ing of the body indeed avail much in this quenching, 
but only when they are used as means of the grace of 
the Holy Ghost: otherwise of themselves alone they 
are of no great value. For love it is which controls 
the affections and movements of the mind; it rules 
all men, and to it all men submit. Love will not be 
forced, and if it be hindered in one path it will find a 
passage by another. Love fears nothing, it ventures 
everything, conquers all things, thinks nothing hard 
or impossible for itself: in short, love yields to nothing 
but a greater or more powerful love. Thus a love 
altogether carnal, whether it pursues riches or pleas- 
ures, yields only to the love of God, and as soon as the 
water of the Holy Spirit begins to be instilled into the 
heart of a man, carnal love forthwith begins to cool. 
Saint Augustine may be our witness, who when he was 
accustomed to indulge his lust, considered it impossib.e 
to do without alliance with a woman: yet when he 
began to taste the grace of the Holy Spirit he ex- 
claimed: ‘ How sweet did it at once become to me, to 
waive the sweetness of those toys! and what I feared 
to be parted from was now a joy to part with. For 
thou didst cast them forth from me, thou true and 
highest sweetness. Thou castedst them forth, and for 
them enteredst in thyself, sweeter than all pleasure, 
though not to flesh and blood; brighter than all light, 
but more hidden than all depths, higher than all 
honour, but not to the high in their own conceits.’ ! 


CHAPTER 3 


Water assuages thirst: God satisfies the desires of 
the heart. 


Besipes this, water slakes thirst: and nothing but that 
heavenly water can put an end to the various trouble- 


t Conf. ix. 1, trans. Dr. Pusey.

some and wellnigh infinite desires of the human heart. 
Thus the Truth itself, speaking to the woman of 
Samaria, taught plainly, ‘ Whosoever drinketh of this 
water shall thirst again : but whosoever drinketh of 
the water that I shall give him shall never thirst.’ ! 
The case is plainly this, that ‘the eye is not satis- 
_ fied with seeing, nor the ear filled with hearing.’ 2 
Nothing that is offered to a man can satisfy his desires, 
since he is capable of an infinite good, and all created 
things are circumscribed within certain limits: but 
he who begins to drink the heavenly water, in which 
all things exist, seeks for nothing else and desires 
nothing more. But of this we have spoken above, in 
our discourse concerning the soul’s rest in God as 
the Creator of its being. 


CHAPTER 4 


Water unites material things : God accomplishes 
union of spirits. 


WATER joins together and reduces to one such things 
as it would seem scarcely possible to unite: thus many 
grains of flour are wrought into one loaf by being 
mingled with water, and many particles of earth by 
the addition of water to them are made into bricks. 
But with far greater easé and permanence doth the 
water of the Holy Spirit work, making many men to 
be ‘ of one heart and of one soul,’ which in the book of 
the Acts of the Apostles 3 is said of the first Christians, 
upon whom the Holy Ghost had immediately before 
descended. And the Lord, when about to go to the 
Father, commends and foretells this unity which the 
water of the Holy Spirit should accomplish when he 
said, ‘I pray not for these alone, but for them also 
which shall believe on me through their word, that 
they all may be one, as thou, Father, art in me and I 
t St. John iv. 13. 2 Eccles. 1. 8. 3 Acts iv. 32. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE WATERS 53 


in thee, that they also may be one in us,’ and a little 
after, “ That they may be one, as we are one, I in them 
and thou in me, that they may be made perfect in one.’ ! 
And to this unity the Apostle exhorts in the Epistle 
to the Ephesians, ‘ Endeavouring to keep the unity of 
the Spirit in the bond of peace. ‘There is one body and 
one Spirit, even as ye are called in one hope of your 
calling.’ O happy union, which makes many persons 
to be one body of Christ, which is ruled by one head, 
eating of the one bread, drinking of one cup, living 
by one Spirit, and cleaving to God becomes one spirit 
with him !3 What can a servant more desire than to 
become not only a sharer in all the goods of his Lord, 
but also to be made one with him in an indissoluble 
bond of love, one with him the Omnipotent, all-wise, 
and altogether lovely, Lord ? And this does the grace 
of the Holy Spirit accomplish, as living and life-giving 
water, when it is received in a devout heart and pre- 
served with all diligence and care. 


CHAPTER 5 


When water descends it rises again: the Grace of God 
as a fountain springs up unto eternal life. 


AcaIN, water rises to a height corresponding to the 
depth to which it has sunk. And since the Holy 
Spirit comes down from the highest heaven upon earth, 
so in the man who receives him into his heart, he 
becomes a ‘well of water springing up unto everlasting 
life,’4 as the Lord said to the woman of Samaria; 
that is to say, when a man is born again of water and 
the Holy Spirit and bears in his heart that indwelling 
Spirit, he lifts the fruit of grace up to the point whence 
that grace descended to him. ‘Therefore, my soul, 
since thou hast been taught and stirred by the words

CHAPTER 6 


The spring of water an image of God as the Fount 
of being. 

I come now to the likeness to God which we see in 
springs of water; for thence the mind may rise to 
contemplate the exceeding and wondrous excellence of 
the Creator. Not without cause does Holy Scripture 
speak of God as the ‘ well of life,’! ‘the fountain of 
wisdom,’ 2 ‘ the fountain of living waters.’3 And that 
he is the fount of being may be gathered from the 
words of God himself spoken to Moses,‘ I am that I 
am: I am hath sent me unto you.’4 And all these 
names the Apostle seems to have gathered into one, 
when he says, ‘ In him we live and move and have our 
being.’5 For in him we exist as in the fountain of 
being, in him we live as in the fountain of life, and 
in him we move as in the fountain of wisdom, for 
“Wisdom is more moving than any motion: she 
passeth and goeth through all things by reason of her 
pureness.’ 6 

Here among ourselves, water has this quality, 
namely, that from it rivers spring, and if they cease 
to draw from their source they gradually become dry: 
but the spring itself is not dependent on rivers because 
it does not receive its waters from them, but holds 
them in itself, and shares them with others. This is 
a true symbol and picture of the Divinity, for God is 
the very true fountain of being: he receives being 
from no other, and all things receive their being from 
him: God receives his being from no other because 
the Essence of God is his existence and his existence 
is his Essence, so that it can neither happen nor be 
conceived that God should not always have existed, 
or that he will not always exist. Other things may

4 Exod. iii. 14. 5 Acts xvii. 28. 6 Wisd. vii. 24.

exist for a time, and a while after cease to be; since 
existence does not of necessity belong to their essence: 
as, for example, from the nature of man, since man Is a 
reasonable creature, he cannot be a man without being 
a reasonable creature : and if existence were of the 
essence of a man, it would be impossible that he 
should not always exist, but since existence is not of 
‘the essence of his being, it is possible for him both to 
exist and not to exist. God is the fount of being 
because in his own Essence is included the act of 
always existing. And this is signified by the words, 
‘Iam that I am,’! that is, I am Being Itself and I 
receive not my Being from any other, but I have it 
within myself: to me alone it belongs that my 
Essence is to be. And hence it is that eternity and 
immortality are proper to God, as the Apostle saith, 
‘To the King eternal, immortal, the only God,’ 2 
and ‘ Who only hath immortality.’3 And indeed all 
other things receive their being from God, so that 
unless they are always in dependence on him and 
preserved by a certain inflow from him, they must 
forthwith come to naught: hence the same Apostle 
saith that ‘ He bears all things along by the word of 
his power,’ 4 because unless created things are sus- 
tained by God they cease to exist. ‘Therefore, my soul, 
consider and wonder at the infinite goodness of thy 
Maker, who so lovingly bears up and preserves all 
things, though he himself needs them not, and wonder 
no less at the patience of thy Creator, and imitate it, 
since “He is so kind to the unthankful and to the 
evil,’ 5 that he sustains those who blaspheme him and 
preserves those who deserve to be brought to naught, 
and let it not seem to thee burdensome if thou art 
sometimes required to bear the infirmities of thy 
brethren, and to do good to them that hate thee.

But the pre-eminence of him that is the fountain of 
being does not consist only in receiving his being from 
no other fountain and in communicating being to all 
other things. Waters of springs and waters of rivers 
are of the same nature, and although the waters of 
springs do not receive their waters from other springs, 
they yet have some other causes of their being, namely 
mists, and they again have other causes, until we reach 
the final Cause, which is God. Now, my soul, God is 
thy Creator, and his Being is not of the same kind as 
the being of created things, but is separated from them 
by an infinite distance of dignity and nobility and 
excellence, and he is truly and really the fountain of 
being, but he is wholly without any cause. 

A well of created water, as was said, is not derived 
from other water, yet it is from another source: the 
uncreated fountain of being has nothing before him- 
self, depends on nothing, lacks nothing: nothing can 
hurt him, but all things depend upon him and he is able 
to extinguish all created things and ‘at a beck can 
cast down all the world’ as the most valiant Maccabeus 
says.t Admire, O my soul, this pre-eminence, this 
beginning without beginning, cause without cause, 
infinite, boundless, immense, and absolutely necessary 
Being, since all other things in comparison with him 
are mere contingencies. 

And perchance it was of him that the Truth himself 
spake,‘ One thing only is necessary,’ 2 therefore cleave 
unto him alone, serve him alone, take thy delight in 
the love and desire of him alone: in comparison with 
him despise all other things, or at least do not disturb 
thyself with care about much business, seeing but one 
thing is necessary for thee, and that will suffice for 
thyself and for all others; let this one care be thine, 
that thou never fall from his grace, and study always 
and everywhere to please him.

CHAPTER 7 
God the fountain of life has life in himself. 


AND in truth we most rightly say that God 1s the 
fountain of life, for he lives and has life in himself, 
yea is himself eternal life. ‘This is the true God and 
‘eternal life,’ saith Saint John,‘ and all things that live 
receive life from that fountain, and if he ceased to 
bestow life they would fail and turn again into their 
dust as the holy prophet David says.? 

To beget a being like unto oneself is a property of 
living beings. God begets his Son, wholly like unto 
himself, God God, and the Living One the Living 
One: for ‘as the Father hath life in himself, so hath 
he given to the Son to have life in himself,’ as Saint 
John witnesseth in his Gospel.3 Now the Father hath 
life in himself because he is the fountain of life and 
receiveth life from no other: and he hath given to the 
Son to have life in himself, because he hath given him 
the same life which he himself hath, and for this reason 
the Son also is a fountain of life, but a fountain of life 
from the fountain of life, as he is God from God, and 
Light from Light. Who can explain, nay, who can 
conceive, what that life of God is, and what is that 
fountain of life in which living things in earth and in 
heaven have their source ? Life as it is known to us 
in this state of exile is nothing else than an inward 
principle of movement: for those things are said by 
us to be alive which in some way move themselves; 
and for this reason we commonly speak of the waters 
of rivers as living waters, and those in pools as dead, 
since the former appear to be moved of themselves 
and the latter cannot move unless they be driven by 
winds, or by some other external force. Thy God, O 
my soul, doth most truly live, and he is the author and 


1 1 St.Johnv.20. 2 Ps. civ. 29. 3 St. John v. 26. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE WATERS 59 


the fountain of life. For he himself impresses this 
truth on us in many passages of Holy Scripture: ‘ I 
live, saith the Lord ’’;1 and the prophets often repeat, 
‘ As the Lord liveth ’;2 and God, lamenting over his 
people, saith, ‘ They have forsaken me, the fountain 
of living waters.’3 And nevertheless he neither moves, 
nor 1s he moved by any other : ‘I am God, I change 
not,’ 4 and elsewhere, ‘God is not the son of man 
that he should repent.’5 And in the Church’s hymn 
which we say daily :— 

O God, Creation’s secret force, 

Thyself unmoved, all motion’s source, 


Who from the morn till evening’s ray, 
Through all its changes guid’st the day.6 


And though God begets the Son, he begets him with- 
out changing; and although he sees, hears, speaks, 
loves, pities, judges, it is without moving that he does 
all these things; and although he creates and preserves 
things or, on the contrary, destroys and scatters them, 
and again renews and changes them, yet he himself 
works resting and changes things while himself un- 
moved. 

How then doth he live if he moveth not, and how 
can he not live if he is the fountain and author of life ? 
This difficulty is easily removed. For to be alive it is 
quite sufficient for the living thing to act of itself and 
not to be moved by another: for the most part, life 
in created things is a principle of internal movement, 
because created things are imperfect and need many 
things in order that they may fulfil the functions of 
life, but God is infinite perfection and needs nothing 
outside himself: he acts of himself and is not moved 
by another, and he has no need of movement or 
change. Created things need change that they may 
generate and be generated; because they beget outside 

t Num. xiv. 28. 2 2 Kings iii. 14. a ers ier ay 

4 Mal. iii. 6. 5 Num. xxiii. 19. 6 Brev. Rom. None. 


60 Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


themselves and the thing which is begotten must be 
changed from not being into being: but God begets 
the Son within himself, and within himself produces 
the Holy Spirit. ‘The Son needs not to undergo change, 
nor is the Holy Spirit changed from not being to 
being, because they receive that being that always was; 
-and that they receive not in time but in eternity. 
Created things need movement for increase, because 
they are born imperfect: but God the Son is born 
most perfect, and God the Holy Ghost is breathed 
forth also and produced most perfect. 

Created things need the movement of alteration, 
that they may obtain the various qualities of which 
they are in need: but God is in need of nothing, see- 
ing his Essence is absolute perfection. 

Created things require to move from place to place, 
because they are not everywhere: but God is wholly 
everywhere. 

Besides this, created things need many things in 
order to see, hear, speak, or work, because though 
indeed they have a certain life, yet it is imperfect and 
very poor: but God needs nothing outside himself in 
order to see and hear all things, to speak or to work 
all things in all; because he has not only life, but life 
most rich and happy, and he himself is life and the 
fountain of life. 

Let us take an example in the act of seeing: in 
order that a man may see he needs power of vision 
which is. distinct from the soul which properly lives: 
he needs an object, some coloured body placed outside 
himself: he needs sunlight or else light from some 
other luminous body: he needs a medium, that is a 
clearly-marked object: he needs a sensible species 
which can be drawn from the object to his sight: he 
needs the bodily organ, that is the eye, made of various 
humours and fleshy membranes: he needs sensitive 
spirits, and optic nerves through which those spirits 


CONSIDERATION OF THE WATERS 61 


may pass: he needs distance duly proportioned: he 
needs the application of power. 

See how many minute things men and other crea- 
tures need that they may accomplish one vital action: 
but God, who in truth holds all life within himself, is 
in need of nothing at all: his infinite Essence is for 
him power, object, species, light, and all things else 
of himself, by himself, and in himself. God sees all 
things that are, that have been, that will be; and what 
they can be he clearly knows: and before the world 
was, God saw all things and nothing new has increased 
his knowledge or vision by the creation of all things. 
What therefore shalt thou be, O my soul, when thou 
art a partaker of his life? What great demand did 
God make upon thee when he bid thee lay down that 
bodily, animal life, imperfect as it is, and needy, lay it 
down for thy brethren and for God himself, that thou 
mayst become partaker of an eternal life most rich 
and blissful ? And if this command to despise life is 
not a great demand, how slight and small it should 
seem to thee to be bidden to bestow freely thy dead 
riches on the poor, and to abstain from carnal pleasures, 
truly to renounce the devil and his pomps, and to 
yearn with the whole affection of thy heart after that 
life which alone is the true life. 


CHAPTER 8 


God the fountain of wisdom. 


Ir is now time for us to ascend, in such manner as we 
are able, to the fountain of wisdom. ‘ The word of 
God most high is the fountain of wisdom,’ ! and he 
rightly says ‘ most high,’ or ‘in the highest’: for the 
fountain of wisdom flows down most abundantly and 
richly upon the holy angels and the souls of the blessed 
dwelling in heaven ; but to us who are wanderers in 


1 Ecclus. 1. 5. 


62 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


the desert, it is not so much wisdom itself that comes, 
but as it were an effluence or fragrance of wisdom. 
Wherefore, my soul, seek not after higher things than 
it becomes thee to seek, ‘Scan not the majesty lest 
thou be oppressed by the glory.’* Marvel at the 
wisdom of him of whom the Apostle speaks as the 
- only wise God.? 

Congratulate those happy souls who drink of the 
fountain of wisdom, and although they do not com- 
prehend God, because his Being is the fountain of 
wisdom, yet they see the face of God, that is of the first 
cause, without any veil, and illuminated by his splen- 
dour, they rightly judge concerning all things, and in 
that noonday light of wisdom they fear not the darkness 
of error, nor the obscurity of ignorance, nor the dimness 
of opinions. 

Aspire to that bliss, and that thou mayst safely 
attain to it, love the Lord Jesus Christ with all thy 
heart; in him are all the treasures of the wisdom and 
knowledge of God: 3 and he himself said in the Gospel, 
‘He that loveth me shall be loved of my Father, and 
I will love him and will manifest myself to him.’ 4 
And what is that ‘I will manifest myself to him’ but, 
I will manifest all the treasures of the knowledge and 
wisdom of God which are hidden in myself? Most 
certainly all men naturally desire knowledge, and 
though at the present time many persons find a certain 
satisfaction in worldly pleasures, yet when we lay down 
the body which is corruptible and now weighs down 
the soul, we shall find the fire of this desire for know- 
ledge blazes above all other desires. How great then 
will be thy happiness, O soul, when Christ thy Lover 
and thy Beloved shall show thee all the treasures of the 
knowledge and the wisdom of God !_ But in order that 
thou be not defrauded of so great a hope, be diligent

in keeping the commandments of Christ: for he hath

FIFTH STEP 
CONSIDERATION OF THE AIR 
CHAPTER I 


The body lives by breathing air, the soul by praying. 


F we consider the nature of the element of air we find 

it to be a remarkable instructor of good conduct, 

not only for the teaching of moral philosophy, but also 

in sacred theology, for the unfolding of mysteries and 

raising the mind to God. So let attention be given to 

the various advantages which, by the ordering of provi- 
dence, accrue to the human race through air. 

First of all, since air serves for breathing it pre- 
serves the life of animals and men upon earth. In the 
next place, it is so far necessary for the use of eyes, ears, 
and tongue, that if perchance it be withdrawn, even 
if we have all other things, every one would by degrees 
become blind, deaf, and dumb. And lastly, so greatly 
is air needed for the movements of men and other 
animals, that were it withdrawn, all movement would 
be hindered, and all the arts and almost all the works 
of men would of necessity cease. To begin with the 
first. 

If men understood that respiration is needed for 
their souls no less than for their bodies, many would 
be saved who are now perishing. The body is in need 
of continual respiration, for the natural heat which 
causes the heart to beat is tempered by the help of the 
lungs which inhale the cold and exhale the heated air, 
so that life is maintained which without this respiration 
cannot be preserved ; and for this reason it is a com- 


64 


CONSIDERATION OF THE AIR 65 


monly accepted fact that living and breathing are one 
and the same, since every one who breathes lives, and 
he who leaves off breathing, ceases also to live. And 
thou, O my soul, art in need of constant respiration 
in order to live the spiritual life which is the grace of 
God, and this consists in sending forth fervent sighs 
to God by prayer and receiving from God fresh sup- 
plies of the Holy Spirit. And this 1s signified by those 
words of the Lord that “men ought always to pray and 
not to faint ’;* it behoves thee always to be breathing 
and receiving the good Spirit that the spiritual life in 
thee be not extinguished ; and this he repeats, saying, 
‘Watch ye therefore and pray always.’2 And the 
Apostle confirms this in his former Epistle to the 
Thessalonians, saying, *‘ Pray without ceasing ’;3 and 
the Apostle Peter is in agreement with him when he 
writes, ‘Be ye therefore sober4 and watch unto 
prayer.’ 5 7 
And it is indeed true wisdom that we who stand in 
need of the help of God at all times, should at all 
times séek it. Our Father indeed knows what things 
we have need of, and is ready to supply them boun- 
tifully, especially such things as pertain to our eternal 
salvation; but he wills to bestow them by the means 
of prayer, because this is more honouring to him and 
of more benefit to us than if he gave all things to 
those who sleep and do nothing. And so our most 
bounteous Lord exhorts and urges us to ask when 
he says, ‘ I say unto you, ask and it shall be given you, 
seek and ye shall find, knock and it shall be opened 
unto you; for every one that asketh receiveth, and he 
that seeketh findeth, and to him that knocketh it shall 
be opened.’® And what that is which is specially to 
be asked for, and which will without doubt be given, 
he explains a little after, saying, ‘ If ye then, being evil, 
2 St. Luke xviii. r. 2 ibid., xxi. .36. 3 1 Thess. v. 17. 
4 Vulg. prudentes. 5.1\St. Petziv. 7. 6 St. Luke xi. 9. 
F 


66 | Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


know how to give good gifts unto your children, how 
much more shall your heavenly Father give the Holy 
Spirit to them that ask him ?’! This therefore is what 
we must chiefly and earnestly ask for, and which will 
without doubt be given us, if properly sought, namely 
the Holy Spirit, through whom we have respiration 
in God, and so preserve our spiritual life; as holy 

David did, who said in the Psalm, ‘I opened my 
mouth and drew in spirit’; that is, I opened my 
mouth by desire, by sighs, beseeching with groans 
unutterable, and I drew in the most sweet breath of 
- the Spirit of God, which cooled the heat of evil desires 
and established me in every good work. 

This being the case, who will say that those persons 
who spend whole days, even months and years, without 
sighing after God and without drawing in breath from 
him are living according to God? For not to breathe 
is a sure sign of death, and if to breathe is to pray, not 
to pray isa sign of death. The spiritual life by which 
we are the children of God consists in love: ‘ Behold,’ 
saith Saint John in his Epistle, ‘what manner of love 
the Father hath bestowed upon us, that we should be 
called the sons of God.’ 3 And who is there that loves 
and does not desire to see the person whom he loves ? 
Who is there that desires and does not seek the object 
he desires from him who-he knows will give it if he be 
asked ? He then who does not earnestly pray that he 
may behold the face of his God doth not desire to see 
him: and he who desireth not, loveth not: he who 
loveth not, liveth not. Does it not therefore follow 
that we must consider those to be dead to God, though 
in the world they are counted living, who do not give 
themselves seriously to the work of prayer? More- 
over, a man who breathes and lives cannot be said to 
be truly praying if he pours forth prayers with the 
bodily voice alone: for wise men define prayer to be 

1 St. Luke xi. 13. SOPs, cxixe 24 1s 3 r St. John iii. 1. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE AIR 67 


not the lifting up of the voice into the air, but the 
uprising of the mind to God. 

Therefore, my soul, make no mistake, and do not 
think that thou art alive in God if thou art not earnestly 
seeking him with thy whole heart and sighing after 
him day and night. Say not that thou art hindered 
by other occupations from divine converse and hast 
no leisure for prayer: for the holy Apostles were 
always occupied, and that too in the work of God and 
the salvation of souls, insomuch that one of them said, 
‘ Beside those things that are without, that which 
cometh upon me daily, the care of all the churches. 
Who is weak, and lam not weak? Who is offended, 
and | burn not ?’;* and yet this same Apostle, besides 
the frequent mention of his own prayers, writes to the 
Philippians, ‘ Our conversation is in heaven,’ because 
even in the midst of business he was living in heaven 
by desire, and never forgot him whom he loved, other- 
wise he could not say, ‘I am crucified with Christ. 
I live, yet not I, but Christ liveth in me.’ 2 


CHAPTER 2 


The air considered to be the means by which men 
live, hear, and speak. 


ANOTHER property of air is that it is the medium by 
which the various kinds of colours reach our eyes, all 
manner of sounds reach our ears, and without which 
we could neither see nor hear and not even speak. 
On which account we ought first of all to thank God 
for that he has willed to endow our nature with so 
remarkable a benefit. Next it behoves us to admire 
the wisdom of our Creator in a work of such great 
subtilty and delicacy that the air has real body and is so 
extensive that it fills a space wellnigh measureless, and 


I 2 Cor. xi. 28. 2 Gal. il. 20. 


68 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


yet it is not seen, nor is it felt, by reason of its extreme 
rarity. The ancient world marvelled at the fineness 
of one line which Apelles drew with his pencil; but 
that line could be seen and touched and could in no 
respect be compared with the fineness of this most 
slender veil, which surrounds all men, touches all men, 
_ yet by reason of its exceeding thinness is discerned by 
none. But our wonder is increased when we perceive 
that though the air is most subtile and so very fine, yet 
when it is cut it is with the greatest ease joined again 
and closed as though it had never been cut. A broken 
spider’s web or a very fine veil when broken cannot 
be so mended by any artist that the former rent no 
longer shows. And to this we may add, and it is a 
fact most worthy of admiration and proper to the 
wisdom of God alone, that innumerable varieties of 
colours pass at the same time, without mingling, 
through the same part of air. Who is there who when 
placed in an open and high spot at night and in the 
moonlight, can gaze at the stars in the sky, the fields 
on the earth filled with flowers, and at the same time 
see the houses, trees, animals, and many other things, 
and deny that the forms of all these things are con- 
tained by that part of the air which is near him and at 
the same time are so contained as not to intermix 
with one another? And who can understand, who 
can comprehend this? For how can it be that a sub- 
stance so very fine should contain at one time such a 
great variety of objects ? Moreover, when at the same 
time and place the song of birds on the one hand, and 
on another various musical instruments or the murmur 
of falling waters are heard, is it not a matter of necessity 
that those various colours, those sounds and various 
kinds of sounds, should be received at one and the 
same time in that very air which receives so many 
forms of colours ? 

Who accomplishes this, my soul, but thy Maker, 


CONSIDERATION OF THE AIR 69 


“who only doeth wondrous things’; and if his 
works are so wonderful, how much more wonderful 
will he himself be ? 

But there is another use for this wondrous subtilty 
in the air, namely that it does not hinder, but assists 
the motion of all things which pass from place to place. 
We all know the great labour which is needed to draw 
ships through the water, although this is liquid and 
easily parted: sometimes indeed neither wind nor oars 
are sufficient, but it becomes necessary to use in addi- 
tion the forces of horses or oxen. And if perchance 
roads have to be opened through mountains or hills, 
what great labour and what length of time are needed 
to open a way, however short. And horses run, birds 
fly, without labour, indeed with the greatest ease, 
through the air ; darts and missiles are shot ; and in 
their various duties men go up and down, walk about, 
run to and fro, move their feet, arms, hands, upwards, 
downwards, to the right and to the left: and the air 
spreading on all sides hinders them no more than if it 
were not of a bodily but of a spiritual nature, or even 
than if it were nothing at all. 


CHAPTER 3 


In the air there is manifest a certain image of the 
sweetness and kindness of God. 


Lastiy, we may add that the air by nature so yields 
itself to everything and changes according to every 
shape, and suffers itself to be cut and broken through, 
in order to serve the convenience of man, that it seems 
to be given to man as a teacher of humility, patience, 
and charity. But, my soul, the thought that should 
strongly stir and kindle the love of thy Creator is this : 
that this very air represents the amazing sweetness 


t Ps, cxxxvi. 4. 


70 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


and exceeding loving-kindness of the Creator towards 
mankind. 

Collect thyself, I pray thee, O soul, and consider 
attentively, that thy Lord is always present in all his 
creatures, that he works with all, and in a manner 
infinitely tender, accommodates his work to the nature 
of each one, as if he were to say with the Apostle, ‘I 
became all things to all men, that I might by all means 
save some.’! With necessary agents he co-operates 
that they may act according to need: he co-operates 
_ with voluntary agents that they may act voluntarily : 
with free agents that they may act with freedom. 

So he moves the fire and bids it ascend: the earth, 
and bids it remain beneath: water, and bids it slide 
along sloping places: the air, and bids it pass whither- 
soever it is driven: the stars, and bids them circle 
continually in their orbits: he bids the grass, shrubs, 
and \plants to bring forth fruit according to their own 
nature; and creatures of the earth, the air, and the 
water, to do what their nature requires of them. 

And if the sweetness of God thus so appears in 
co-operating with his creatures in the works of nature, 
what may we suppose does he in the works of grace ? 
God indeed bestowed upon man free will, yet in such 
a way that he might control him by rule, terrify him 
with destruction, and allure him with kindness. ‘God 
will have all men to be saved’;2 but he wills it in 
such a manner that they should also will it, and there- 
fore he sweetly goes before them, incites them onwards, 
leads them and guides them, in a marvellous way. 
These are the contrivances of the Wisdom of God, of 
which Isaiah speaks : ‘ Declare his doings among the 

people.’3 And indeed at one time he greatly terrifies 

the wicked, at another lovingly exhorts them, now 

gently warns, now corrects in pity, in such a manner 
as he sees to be right for their mind and character.

Hear how gently the Lord dealt with the first sinner : 
« Adam,’ saith he, ‘where art thou?’ and when he 
answered ‘I heard thy voice in the garden and I was 
afraid, because I was naked; and I hid myself,’ the 
Lord went on with the same gentleness, ‘ Who told 
thee that thou wast naked? Hast thou eaten of the 
tree whereof I commanded thee that thou shouldst not 
eat?’ And Adam, warned by this gentle correction, 
doubtless repented, as the Scripture saith, ‘ She’ 
(namely the Wisdom of God) ‘ preserved the first 
formed father of the world, that was created alone, and 
brought him out of his fall.’ 2 

Hear again how tenderly and sweetly he corrected 
by his angel, and invited all the children of Israel to 
repentance: an angel of the Lord came up from Gilgal 
to the place of weeping and said, ‘I made you to go 
up out of Egypt, and have brought you into the land 
which I sware unto your fathers; and I said, I will never 
break my covenant with you. And ye shall make no 
league with the inhabitants of this land; ye shall throw 
down their altars: but ye have not obeyed my voice: 
why have ye done this? . . . When the angel of the 
Lord spake these words unto all the children of Israel 
the people lifted up their voices and wept. And they 
called the name of that place Bochim [i.e. weepers]; 
and they sacrificed there unto the Lord.’3 Now that 
the weeping was very great and universal and a sign 
of true penitence, is evinced by the new name given 
to the place, an everlasting memorial to posterity, 
namely that the place should be called ‘ the place of 
weeping, or of tears.’ 

What shall I now say of the prophets? In all their 
sermons surely it is this that they proclaim, that 
“God willeth not the death of sinners, but that they 
should be converted and live.’4 ‘ They say,’ saith the 
holy Jeremiah, ‘ If a man put away his wife and she go

their Epistles concerning the fatherly mercies of the

There remains at the last to quote the words of 
Saint Prosper in which he declares the kindness of 
God not only towards the good but also towards the

‘Grace,’ he says, ‘is chiefly remarkable in petr- 
suading by exhortations, in admonishing by examples, 
in deterring by dangers, in spurring on by wonders, 
in bestowing understanding, in inspiring counsel, in 
illuminating the heart and seasoning it with the affec- 
tions of faith. But the will of man acts in subordina- 
tion to and in conjunction with this grace, being stirred 
up by the aforesaid aids to co-operate with the work 
of God in itself and begin to reach out to the reward, 
and if it fails, laying the failure to its own change- 
fulness ; if it prosper, counting the prosperity as a 
favour bestowed by grace. And the favour is bestowed 
upon all in numberless ways, some hidden, some mani- 
fest: and if many are refused it, it is owing to their 
own wickedness, and if many receive it, it is of divine 
grace and the human will.’ 2

CHAPTER 4 


Exhortation to the soul to imitate the kindness 
of God. 


SEE now, my soul, if thy Creator is so sweet and 
gentle towards his servants, and suffers sinners with 
such amazing kindness that he may convert them: and 
if he so consoles the righteous that he may increase 
their righteousness and holiness: shouldst thou not 
bear kindly with thy neighbours, and become all things 
to all men, that thou mayst win all to thy Lord and 
God ? 

Consider with thyself to what high degree of excel- 
lence the Apostle exhorts thee when he says, ‘ Be ye 
therefore followers of God as dear children: and walk 
in love, as Christ also hath loved us, and hath given 
himself for us, an offering and a sacrifice to God for a 
sweetsmelling savour.’ ! 

Imitate God the Father, ‘who maketh his sun to 
rise on the evil and on the good, and sendeth rain on 
the just and on the unjust.’ 2 

Imitate God the Son, who, taking human nature for 
our sake, spared not his own life that he might snatch 
us from the power of darkness and from everlasting 
death. ; 

Imitate God the Holy Ghost, who freely pours forth 
his most precious gifts that he may make us who are 
carnal to be truly spiritual.

SIXTH STEP 
CONSIDERATION OF FIRE 
CHAPTER I 


The hatred of God for sin is as a consuming fire. 
Cae is an element so pure and noble that God has 


willed to call himself by that name according to 
what Moses and Paul affirm, ‘ Our God is a consuming 
fire’;* and when first God appeared to Moses, he 
willed to be seen in the fire which seized on the bush 
and yet did not consume it: ‘ And the angel of the 
Lord appeared,’ saith Moses, ‘ in a flame of fire in the 
midst of a bush: and he looked, and, behold, the bush 
burned with fire, and the bush was not consumed.’ 2 
And when God came to give the law to his people, he 
came in the likeness of fire; for thus saith the same 
Moses, *“ And Mount Sinai was altogether on a smoke, 
because the Lord descended upon it in fire.’3 In like 
manner, when the new law was to be promulgated, the 
Holy Spirit appeared unto the Apostles in tongues of 
fire. And lastly, those who are nearest to God in 
heaven, the seraphim, that is the fiery ones, are so 
called because from that divine and most burning fire 
they receive fervour and ardour beyond all other 
angels. 

From these truths it is not difficult, considering the 
element of fire, its nature, and properties, to form 
therewith for ourselves a step by which, with the help 
of prayer and meditation, we may rise up to God: 
indeed it will be easier to ascend with Elijah in a 

1 Deut. iv. 14; Heb. xii. 29. 2 Exod. ili. 2. 3 ibid., xix. 18. 

75 


76 Tue Mrnp’s Ascent to Gop 


chariot of fire than to form a ladder from either earth, 
water, or air. 

Let us therefore proceed to consider the properties 
of fire. The nature of fire is such that in different 
things it acts in different and often in contrary ways: 
it forthwith consumes wood, hay, or stubble: gold, 
silver, precious stones it purifies and makes more 
beautiful: iron, which naturally is black, cold, hard, 
and heavy, is transformed by fire, its qualities are 
changed, and it is gradually rendered clear, hot, soft, 
and light, so that it shines like a star, burns as fire, 
dissolves like water, and becomes so light that a black- 
smith can very easily move and lift it. 

All these things manifestly correspond with the 
working of God. And first, according to the Apostle 
in the former Epistle to the Corinthians, wood, hay, 
stubble signify evil deeds which cannot stand the fire 
of the divine judgement.!_ And we can hardly believe 
how sorely all sins displease God who is most pure fire, 
and with what zeal he consumes and destroys them, 
if so be they can be destroyed by patience, that is, 
if he who has sinned is in a condition to do penance: 
for all sins are remitted by penitence. But if the sinner 
is incapable of penitence, as are devils and men who 
have passed out of this life, the divine wrath of God is 
turned upon the wicked themselves, ‘For the un- 
godly and his ungodliness are both alike hateful to 
God,’ saith the Wise Man.?,_ And how real and severe 
this hatred is, the devil is a witness, who once sinned 
and when he was the most noble angel and, as Saint 
Gregory testifies, the chief of the first order of angels 
and the most glorious of the beings created by God, 
yet was cast out of heaven and despoiled of all super- 
natural grace and glory and changed into a hideous 
monster and enslaved to eternal destruction. Christ is 
also witness, who came down from heaven to destroy the 

t x Cor. iii. 12. 2 Wisd. xiv. 9. 


CONSIDERATION OF FIRE 77 


works of the devil, that is sin,t and is therefore called 
‘the Lamb of God who taketh away the sins of the 
world.’ But who is able to declare or even to con- 
ceive the sufferings he bore, that the justice of God 
might be completely satisfied ? ‘Who, being in the 
form of God, took upon him the form of a servant’; 3 
‘who, though he was rich, yet for your sakes became 
poor ’;4 who had ‘ not where to lay his head’;5 and 
though he himself had made heaven and earth yet 
‘came unto his own and his own received him not’; 6 
who, when he was reviled, reviled not again; when he 
suffered, he threatened not, but committed himself to 
him that judgeth righteously; ‘who his own self bare 
our sins in his own body on the tree.’7 “ He humbled 
himself and became obedient unto death, even the 
death of the cross,’ ’ ‘ by whose stripes ye were healed’; 9 
and at last bound, spitted on, scourged, crowned with 
thorns and crucified with extreme insults and greatest 
agony, poured forth all his blood and life, and he bore 
all these things that he might destroy the works of the 
devil and blot out our sins. The law of God is witness, 
which forbids all sin and punishes it, and leaves not even 
an idle word unpunished.te And how great must be 
his hatred of crimes and evil deeds, who cannot endure 
an idle word? ‘The commandment of the Lord is 
pure and giveth light unto the eyes’: he turns away 
from stains and darkness; there can be no communion 
between light and darkness, nor between righteousness 
and iniquity.! 

And lastly, hell is witness, for God prepared it for 
the wicked, and for sinners who, when they might 
have been washed in the Blood of the immaculate 
Lamb, either refused or despised it: for it is just that

where sin is eternal the punishment should be eternal: 
but what is the kind of punishment and how great, it 
is terrible to think. Of this matter we will speak 
further when we come to the last step in our ladder. 

Therefore, my soul, seeing that the hatred of God 
against sin is so great, if thou lovest God above all 
things, thou shouldst also hate sin above all things. 
Take heed lest thou be deceived by persons who 
extenuate or excuse sins, see that thou art not deceived 
by their false reasonings. If thou art not displeased 
by sin in thyself and in others, thou dost not love God: 
and if thou dost not love God, thou art lost. Again, 
if thou be not ungrateful to Christ, how much thou 
shouldst think thyself indebted to his love, his labours, 
his Blood, and his death, who hath washed thee from 
thy sin and reconciled thee to his Father: and will it 
be grievous unto thee to suffer something for Christ, 
or for his sake, and by his grace to strive against sin 
even unto blood ? 

And lastly, if thou canst not endure the hell of 
eternal fire, neither shouldst thoy endure sin in any 
form: but rather turn aside from it and from the 
slightest suspicion of it, as thou wouldst shun the face 
of aserpent. Make, then, a firm and steadfast resolve 
to hate sin above all things and to love God above all 
things. 


CHAPTER 2 


Fire perfects the more precious metals and God crowns 
the good works of man. 


Fire indeed doth not destroy, but perfects and makes 
more bright and glistering gold, silver, and precious 
stones, since (as the Apostle explains) those metals 
signify good and perfect works which are approved by 
the fire of the divine justice, and will receive a great 


CONSIDERATION OF FIRE 79 


reward.! Those works God proves because they are 
his gifts, and when he crowns our merits, says Saint 
Augustine, he crowns his own gifts.2, They were done 
at his bidding and by his own aid, by his bounteous 
gift of power to accomplish them, and directed by the 
law given by him, and according to the rules prescribed 
by him. Now gold signifies works of charity, and how 
should not works of charity be specially pleasing to 
God, when God himself is Love !3 Silver signifies 
works of wisdom, namely of those who turn many to 
righteousness,4 and that these things also are greatly 
approved by God and are pleasing to him, the Wisdom 
of God declares, ‘ He that shall do and teach them, the 
same shall be called great in the kingdom of heaven.’ 5 

Precious stones are the works of the chaste soul, 
of whom it is said, ‘ Her continent mind cannot be 
valued,’ ® and this is the reason why in the Church’s 
office in praise of holy virgins there is read the Gospel 
of the finding of the one pearl of great price. And we 
may learn how pleasing to God is the purity of virgins, 
from the prophet Isaiah, who in the name of God fore- 
told to eunuchs, namely to those who * have made 
themselves eunuchs for the kingdom of heaven’s sake’ :7 
‘Even unto them will I give in mine house and within 
my walls a place and a name better than of sons and of 
daughters. I will give them an everlasting name that 
shall not be cut off.’ Saint Augustine in his book on 
holy virginity shows that this passage is to be under- 
stood of holy virgins, both men and women. 

There are three particular works which in the united 
opinion of the doctors merit an aureole in the kingdom

to doctors for their excellent wisdom, of whom Daniel 
says, ‘ They that turn many to righteousness shall shine

their incomparable chastity, for which cause the virgins 
in the Apocalypse are said to be singing the new song, 
which no other can sing: “ These are they,’ saith Saint

are virgins, and they follow the Lamb whithersoever

But it is not only the charity of martyrs, or the wisdom 
of doctors, or the purity of virgins that will be proved 
by the fire of divine justice and receive a full reward ; 
but all other kinds of good works, wrought in charity, 
will be reckoned among the vessels of gold and will 
endure the divine fire and receive their own reward. 
For in the last judgement the Lord will say, ‘ Come, 
ye blessed of my Father, inherit the kingdom prepared 
for you from the foundation of the world,’ 4 to those 
who gave bread to the hungry and drink to the thirsty, 
shelter to the stranger, clothing to the naked, comfort 
to the sick and to those cast into prison. And that no 
one should be without a reward, the Lord even prom- 
ised the same to any one who out of love should give 
even a cup of cold water in the name of a disciple. 5 

Dost thou understand, O soul, what a difference 
there is between some works and others? What 
course could be more foolish and wretched than if, 
being in a certain place and at a time in which thou 
couldst with slight labour gather together gold, silver, 
and precious stones, thou shouldst prefer, at great

cost and hard labour, to collect wood, hay, and stubble ? 
O that thou wast wise and didst understand and wouldst

will be searched by the fire of the divine judgement, 
and will be proved, and the former things be praised

things, and thine eyesight is clouded by the veil of 
things present, so that thou dost not gaze upon the 
clear truth, pray to God and with great longing say 
to him with the blind man in the Gospel, ‘ Lord, that 
I may receive my sight ’;? or with the prophet, “Open 
thou mine eyes that I may see the wondrous things of 
thy law ’:3 for it is indeed an altogether wonderful 
thing that works done in charity should become gold, 
silver, and precious stones, while works not done in 
charity are turned into dry wood, hay, and stubble. 


CHAPTER 3 


Fire makes black tron to become bright : God leads a 
sinful soul to the knowledge of the truth. 


Let us now come to the consideration of a second 
property of fire. Hitherto we have been learning from 
fire how God works in those who depart this life with 
evil works, or in those who come to the end of life full 
of good works. Now by another similitude borrowed 
in like manner from fire, we may understand what 


God works in those whom he calls from sin to peni- 
tence.

A sinner is like iron which at a distance from the 
fire remains black, cold, hard, and heavy: but if per- 
chance it be placed in the fire, it becomes shining, soft, 
hot, and light. Every sinner lacks interior light and 
walks in darkness, and in this resembleth the blackness 
of iron, for though he is not only skilful in human 
knowledge and business, but also apparently esteemed 
for his good judgement: vet in the matter of discerning 
between the true good and true evil he is blind and 
more wretched than any blind man. For a blind man 
sees naught, and therefore cannot move without a 
guide : but the sinner thinks he sees what he does 
not see, or mistakes one thing for another and thinks 
good evil and evil good, the great small and the small 
great, the long to be short and the short long, and for 
this reason is continually mistaken in his choice. 

It is this which the Apostle speaks of concerning 
the idolatrous Gentiles, “ Having the understanding 
darkened . . . through the ignorance that is in them, 
because of the blindness of their heart ’’;! and this it 
is with which the Lord himself in the Gospel so often 
upbraids the Scribes and Pharisees who were blind 
leaders of the blind It is this which the prophet 
Isaiah said to the Jews of his time, “ Hear, ye deaf; and 
look, ye blind, that ye may see’;3 and to those to 
whom he preached the coming of Christ, who should 
open the eyes of the blind, and speaking concerning 
the new Covenant, in the Person of God, he adds, ‘ I 
will bring the blind by a way that they knew not; I 
will lead them in paths that they have not known; 
I will make darkness light before them and crooked 
things straight.’ 4 

And lastly, will not the wicked acknowledge so 
much, after this life when punishment has begun to 
open their eyes which had been closed by sin? They

will say, ‘ Therefore have we erred from the way of

And it is no wonder that they who have turned 
away from God in heart and will are blind: for ‘ God 
is light, and in him is no darkness at all ’;2 and from 
this the same Apostle concludes, ‘ He that saith he is 
in the light and hateth his brother, is in darkness even 
until now,’ and a little later, ‘He that hateth his 
brother is in darkness and walketh in darkness and 
knoweth not whither he goeth, because the darkness 
hath blinded his eyes.’ 3 

Now the blindness in sinners is not caused only by 
their turning away from God, who is light: but also 
*“ their own wickedness hath blinded them,’ as Wisdom 
saith: 4 for the passions of the mind, love, hate, wrath, 
envy, and the rest, which are included under the name 
of wickedness, so blind the mind that it cannot clearly 
see the truth; just in the same way.as coloured spec- 
tacles cause white objects to appear red, or spectacles 
made in a particular way cause small objects to appear 
large or large objects to appear small, those very 
distant to be near and those near to be far off. 

He who ardently loves, loves a thing which is very 
beautiful and very good, and so much the more as he 
considers it to be necessary for himself, and in com- 
parison with it everything else may be neglected or 
lost. And on the contrary, he who exceedingly hates 
the same thing considers it misshapen, useless, evil, so 
much the more as he considers it hurtful to himself, and 
on this account to be cast away at all costs. 

But if this ugly, black iron is moved into the fire, 
that is, if a sinner begins to turn from his sin and to 
turn towards God, as the prophet says, “ They had an

eye unto him, and were lightened,’! forthwith he
