light to see the plain truth of the matter, according to 
the word of the same prophet, ‘In thy light shall we 
see light.’ And then, when the coloured glasses of 
the passions have been broken and clear ones put 
in their place, the crystal of pure charity, a man counts 
eternal things great and temporal things small, indeed, 
mere nothings, as indeed they are: and he clearly 
perceives that all created ornament and beauty 1s in 
no way comparable to the light of wisdom and truth 
which 1s in God and which is God. 

And so with Saint Augustine he cries, ‘ Too late 
have | loved thee, O thou Beauty of ancient days, yet 
ever new.’ 3 And since Christ saith, ‘ Ye shall know 
the truth and the truth shall make you free,’4 he 
that is enlightened and set free by the light of truth 
from hindrance of concupiscence, avarice, ambition, 
and other passions, rejoices with the prophet and cries, 

Thou, O Lord, hast broken my bonds in sunder. I 
will offer to thee the sacrifice of thanksgiving and will 
call upon the Name of the Lord.’ 5 


CHAPTER 4 


fire makes cold tron to glow: the grace of God makes 
the words and deeds of a man effectual. 


, Now fire does not only cause dull iron to glow, but 
also it changes cold iron into hot, and not only makes 
it hot, but so burning and glowing that it seems to be 
fire itself. Great is our Lord and great is his power. 
See how a man, by nature cold and timid, frightened by 
anything, not venturing to speak or daring to attempt 


anything a little difficult, so soon as he is kindled by 


t Ps, xxxiv. 5. a Ps, Xxxvl. 9. 3 Conf. x, c. 27, no. 38. 
4 St. John viii. 32. 5 Ps. cxvi. 14. 


» CONSIDERATION OF FIRE 8 5 
the fire of divine charity, he immediately becomes 
bolder than a lion, the lion that is known to terrify all 
by its roaring, and to hold every one spellbound, and 
to whom nothing is difficult, nothing seems hard, and 
when the fire is strongly burning he says with the 
Apostle Paul, ‘I can do all things through him that 
strengtheneth me.’! But we will discuss that efficacy 
of fire under different heads: and we will speak shortly 
first of the efficacy of words and then Set) of the 
efficacy of deeds. 

There are now and there have been in the past, 
many preachers of the word of God in the Church. 
What then is the reason, that so few are converted by 
the exhortations and expostulations of so many men? 
In truth, in great cities daily during Lent twenty, 
thirty, or even forty sermons are preached, and yet 
when Lent is over there appears to be hardly any 
change in the life of the city: the same vices, the same 
sins, the same coldness, the same dissoluteness, as 
before is seen. I find no other cause than this: for 
the most part the discourses delivered are learned and 
flowery, but the soul is wanting, the life is wanting, 
the fire is wanting: in short that great charity is want- 
ing which alone can animate the words of the speakers 
and kindle and inflame and change the hearts of the 
hearers. And I say it is not because a persuasive voice 
and ease of movement is lacking to many preachers: 
for a machine for attacking a fortress having no ammu- 
nition might make a great noise, but would have no 
effect. What is to be desired is that preachers should 
be animated by a great love for God and the salvation 
of souls, and this not feignedly but truly, not strained 
but as it were springing up naturally from the depth 
of the heart. 

Saint Peter knew nothing of rhetoric ; he was skilled 
only in steering his boat, in casting and mending his 

aePhil. ivs 13; 


86 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


nets: but as soon as the Holy Spirit descended upon 
him in fiery tongues and filled him with burning 
charity, he began forthwith to speak so powerfully, so 
eagerly, and so effectually in the midst of the city of 
Jerusalem, that by one sermon many thousands were 
converted to penitence and faith;1 and yet we do not 
read that in his sermons he used great straining of the 
voice, or that he tired himself out with excessive 
action. Saint Bonaventura reports that Saint Francis 
was unlearned, and that he had not given great atten- 
tion to rhetoric, and yet when he preached to the people 
they listened to him as to an angel from heaven: for, 
saith he, his words were like burning fire, penetrating 
the inmost heart ;2 and as is told us in the chronicles 
of the order of Friars Minor, when sometimes after 
supper he would speak to the people a few extempore 
words, his hearers were so greatly moved to penitence 
that the day seemed to be like Good Friday itself. 
How came so much movement, so great fruit, from a 
few words? From this, namely, that the holy preacher 
was as a coal enkindled and his word as a burning torch, 
as Ecclesiasticus records of Elijah.3 We have written 
sermons of Saint Vincent, Saint Bernardine, and of 
other saints, which some persons would consider scarce 
worth reading on account of the very great simplicity 
of the words: and yet we know that many thousands 
of men were converted to God by those sermons, and 
that they were always heard with attention by a re- 
markable number of people, because those simple words 
came from fiery and glowing hearts. 

Lastly, the divine fire shows its power not less by 
deeds than by words. God decreed to subdue to him- 
self by the Apostle Peter the city of Rome, head of the 
state and queen of the nations. He decreed also that 
of the rest of the Apostles, some should be sent to

3 Ecclus. xlviii. 1- 


CONSIDERATION OF FIRE 87 


Ethiopia, some to the Indies, some to Scythia, others 
to farthest Britain, that by their means the world’s 
idols should be destroyed, the standard of the Cross be 
raised, laws and customs changed, and the tyranny of 
Satan be overturned. If any one had foretold these 
events to the Apostles when they were fishing on the 
Lake of Gennesareth or when they were seeking a 
refuge at the time of the Lord’s Passion, they would 
have seemed as dreams or old wives’ tales: and yet 
very soon afterwards all these things came to pass, 
and by no other force than by that of the burning love 
which the Holy Spirit kindled in their hearts. 

‘““ Love casteth out fear,’! and ‘ Charity endureth 
all things, hopeth all things.’? It thinketh all things 
possible and with the Apostle cries out, “I can do all 
things through him that strengtheneth me.’3 And 
so we see, through the work and labour of these men 
armed with charity alone, idolatry overturned through- 
out the world, the Church of Christ founded in every 
place, the trophy of the Cross set up in every kingdom, 
and this without any army of soldiers and without any 
machinery of war. 


CHAPTER 5 


Fire softens hard iron: the grace of God conquers 
the stubborn heart. © 


Next, fire has also this property, that it softens hard 
iron so that it can easily be stretched and spread out 
and turned by the workman into flat blades or figures 
of any shape. Fire hath great power over iron, but 
far greater is the power of God when it works upon 
the stubborn and obstinate hearts of mortal men. Hear 
Saint Bernard in his book, De Consideratione : ‘ The 
heart is hard, because it is not alarmed, and because 
it feels nothing. What, therefore, is a hard heart ? 
™ r St. John iv. 18. 21 Cor. xiii.7. . 3 Phil. iv. 13. 


88 Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


It is the heart not wounded with compunction, not 
softened with piety, not moved with prayers, not 
yielding to threats stiffened by punishment; it 1s 
ungrateful for kindness, regardless of counsel’; and 
again, “It is the heart which neither fears God nor 
reverences man.’! And to the truth of all this 
Pharaoh is witness, for the more plagues were sent 
upon him by God, the more he was hardened, and the 
more the mercy of God shone forth in his removal 
of the plague, so much the more was he moved to 
despise and contemn God. But when it pleases God 
to kindle a spark of the true fire of love in a heart, how- 
ever hard it be, the heart is quickly softened and melts 
like wax, and no prolonged or hard obstinacy with- 
stands his power: but the heart which has been stony 
at once becomes of flesh ;2 and when the Spirit of 
the Lord breathes upon the frozen snows, the waters 
flow.3 

We have an example in the Gospel : the woman in 
the city who was a sinner could not be urged to for- 
sake her sin either by the admonition of her brother, 
the rebuke of her sister, the honour of her family, nor 
even her own disgrace, and yet one ray of Christ, 
penetrating her heart and there kindling a spark of 
divine love, so suddenly transformed her into another 
creature that this noble woman did not blush to 
approach the feet of Christ at a feast in public, and 
dissolving into tears to make of those tears a bath for 
the feet of Christ and to wipe them with her own hair 
instead of with a towel, and in the vehemence of her 
love to kiss them and anoint them with most precious 
and sweet-smelling ointment, to signify that she would 
henceforward dedicate herself and all that was hers to 
the service of Christ. Therefore she heard that saying 
of the Lord, ‘Her sins which are many are forgiven, 
for she loved much.’ 4

But we may be allowed to bring forward an example 
of much later time. William, Duke of Aquitaine, lived 
in the time of Saint Bernard. He was obdurate and 
persistent in holding to Anacletus the schismatic Pope 
and in opposing the lawful Pope. He had banished 
all the Catholic bishops from his dominions and 
bound himself by oath never to make peace with 
them, and because every one knew how hardened in 
evil was this man’s heart and feared his cruelty and 
pride, no one durst admonish him. It then pleased 
the Lord to visit the hard heart of this man by his 
servant Bernard, and to enkindle within his breast 
a great spark of divine love: suddenly from being a 
lion he became a lamb, no longer proud but humble, 
and instead of stubborn, most submissive. At a 
word from Saint Bernard he embraced the Bishop of 
Poictiers and with his own hand replaced him in his 
throne, and, a thing which seems to surpass all wonders, 
when he had sought from a certain hermit a punish- 
ment for his soul on account of the sins he had con- 
fessed and was bidden to put upon his bare flesh a 
brazen breastplate and so to fasten it with a key that 
no one would be able to loosen it, he obeyed forthwith, 
and when he was sent by the hermit to the Pope to 
receive his absolution, he went. But the Pope was 
doubtful about his penitence and desired to test his 
patience, and ordered him to make a pilgrimage to 
Jerusalem and to seek absolution from the Patriarch 
of that city ; William at once set out on his journey 
and fulfilled the Pope’s command. And at last this 
very powerful prince became a humble monk, and 
there was scarce any one of his age who surpassed 
him in humility, patience, devotion, and piety. This 
indeed is ‘ the change of the right hand of the Most 
High,’! this is the force of the divine fire which no 
hardness of heart can resist.

CHAPTER 6 


Fire makes heavy things become light: the grace of 
God leads souls into the way of righteousness. 


THERE remains the last property of fire, that it lessens 
the weight of heavy objects and lifts them easily aloft. 

Why is it that men who are not fervent in divine 
love are hard of heart, and the prophet says of them, 
‘ How long will ye blaspheme mine honour,! and have 
such pleasure in vanity and seek after leasing 3 p73 
There is one reason in particular, that ‘ the body which 
is corrupt, presseth down the soul,’ as Wisdom saith,3 
and ‘a heavy yoke is upon the sons of Adam from the 
day that they go out of their mother’s womb till the 
day that they return to the mother of all things.’ 4 
And that this yoke is heavy which presses down the 
soul within the mortal body the same writer explains 
a little farther on, adding ‘ Wrath, envy, trouble, and 
unquietness, fear of death,’ and the rest,5 which are 
usually called passions of the mind, do so oppress a 
man that he looks at nothing but the earth to which 
he clings prostrate, and can neither rise up to seek after 
God nor can he hasten to run in the way of God’s 
commandments. But when the divine fire from on 
high begins to be enkindled in the human heart, at 
once those passions are weakened and put to death, 
and the heavy pressure begins to be lessened : and if 
the heat is increased, the human heart is set free with- 
out difficulty, it can. take wings as of a dove, and can 
say with the Apostle, ‘ Our conversation is in heaven.’ © 
And when the heart 1s expanded by the fire, it says 
with David, ‘ I will run the way of thy commandments 
when thou hast set my heart at liberty.’ 7 

Indeed since our Saviour said, ‘ I am come to send

fire on the earth, and what will I, if it be already 
kindled ?’! we have seen many persons made so free 
and light-hearted, that they have parted with all 
affection for worldly honours and riches, and have said 
to Christ ascending into heaven, * Draw us after thee.’ 2 
Hence we find so many monasteries built, so many

virgins, to whom it was not only easy to run the way 
of the commandments, but also to rise up by the way 
of the Counsels and ‘to follow the Lamb whitherso- 
ever he goeth.’ 3 : 

O blessed fire, which consumest not, but enlight- 
enest, and if thou dost consume, thou consumest 
but the harmful vapours, lest they should quench 
the life. Who will give to me to be seized upon by 
that fire which, with the light of true wisdom, will purge 
and take away the darkness of ignorance and the dull- 
ness of a false conscience: which will change the cold 
of indolence, indevotion, and negligence into a burning 
love which will never suffer my heart to be hardened, 
but will always soften it with its own heat and make it 
obedient and devout: which will at last remove the 
heavy burden of earthly cares and earthly desires and 
raise my heart on high on the wings of holy contem-

that I may say with the prophet, ‘ Rejoice the soul of

SEVENTH STEP 


CONSIDERATION OF THE HEAVENS, THAT 
IS OF THE SUN, MOON, AND STARS 


CHAPTER I 


The sun considered as the dwelling-place of God 
and most beautiful. 


F, will not here labour to raise for ourselves a 
step towards the consideration of God by means 
of consideration of the heavens: for we have a leader 
in the royal prophet who sings in the Psalms, * The 
heavens declare the glory of God and the firmament 
showeth his handiwork.’! And since there are two 
seasons in which we may rise on the wings of contem- 
plation from the heavens to God, namely the day and 
night, he writes of the former in the same Psalm, ‘ In 
them hath he set a tabernacle for the sun: which 
cometh forth as a bridegroom out of his chamber, and 
rejoiceth as a giant to run his course. It goeth forth 
from the uttermost part of heaven, and runneth about 
unto the end of it again: and there is nothing hid from 
the heat thereof.’2 Of the latter he writes in another 
Psalm, ‘I will consider thy heavens, even the works 
of thy fingers: the moon and the stars, which thou hast 
ordained.’ 3 
Let us begin with the former season. The Holy 
Spirit by the mouth of David proclaims four praises 
of the sun: first that it is the tabernacle of God; 
second that it is exceeding beautiful; third that it runs 


e Ps. SineT eA eho ob eh 3 Ps. vill. 3. 


92 


CONSIDERATION OF THE HEAVENS 93 


its course always most swiftly and without becoming 
weary; fourth that it shows its power chiefly in be- 
stowing light and heat. It is on account of all these 
properties that Ecclesiasticus hath written, ‘A marvel- 
lous instrument, the work of the Most High. ... 
Great is the Lord that made it.’ ! 

First, then, God, the Creator of all things, places 
his tabernacle in the sun, as being a most noble crea- 
ture, that is, amongst all corporeal things God chose 
the sun a royal palace and divine sanctuary, to dwell 
in. God does indeed ‘fill heaven and earth,’? and 
“the heaven and heaven of heavens cannot contain 
him ’;3 yet he is said to dwell more in that place 
where by working marvels he manifests greater signs 
of his presence. 

But since in the Hebrew text it is said that ‘he 
placed his tabernacle in them,’ that is in the heavens, 
we gather from this place in the Psalm a second excel- 
lence of the sun which does not contradict the former. 
The sun is a great thing, for which God hath prepared 
a most spacious, beautiful, and noble palace: for he 
willed that the heaven itself should be the palace of 
the sun in which it might freely take its course and 
work, and that the sun itself should be the palace of 
himself, the most high King. And so just as we learn 
the greatness and dignity of the sun from the fact that 
its tabernacle is the heaven, so we learn the greatness 
and splendour of God from this, that his tabernacle 
is the sun, doubtless a wonderful instrument, and than 
which nothing more wonderful is to be found among 
things created. 

Moreover David, when he would point out the 
remarkable beauty of the sun from things known to 
us, compared it with a bridegroom going forth out of 
his chamber: for never do men adorn themselves 
more, or desire to display their comeliness and their 

t Ecclus. xliii. 2, 5. 2 Jer. xxill. 24. 3 2 Chron. ii. 6. 


G4 i kia Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


beauty more, than when they are bridegrooms: for 
then it is that they desire beyond measure to please 
the eyes of the bride whom they so ardently love. 
But if we could steadfastly behold the sun and if we 
could approach so near to it as to discern its size and 
beauty, we should not then need the similitude of a 
bridegroom to understand its incredible magnificence. 
It is certain that all the beauty of colours depends on 
light, and that when there is no light the beauty of 
colours vanishes; hence nothing is more beautiful 
than light ; and God himself who is Beauty itself 
was pleased to be called light. “ God,’ says Saint John, 
is light, and in him is no darkness at all,’* and in 
corporeal things nothing is more lightsome than the 
sun and therefore there is nothing more beautiful. 
Add to which, that the beauty of lower things, and 
particularly the beauty of man, soon fades, while the 
beauty of the sun is never destroyed, is never lessened, 
and continually makes all things glad with its unchang- 
ing splendour. Do we not perceive, how at sunrise 
everything as it were rejoices? Not alone are men 
made joyful, but winds blow sweetly, flowers open, 
grass springs up, birds make the air sweet with their 
song. Hence that aged blind man Tobit when the 
angel said to him, ‘ Joy be ever with thee,’ answered, 
* What joy shall I have, who sit in darkness and see not 

the light of heaven ?’ 2 
Then, my soul, come, think with thyself: if the 
created sun at his-rising doth so rejoice all things, 
what will the uncreated Sun do, who is beyond com- 
parison more beautiful and bright, when to the pure 
of heart he shall arise to be seen and contemplated not 
for a short season but for ever? And how sad and 
unhappy will that hour be to the wicked when they 
shall be sent away to everlasting darkness, where the 
rays of neither created nor uncreated sun will ever

penetrate ! And what will be the greatness of the 
joy of that soul to whom the Father of lights will say, 
‘Enter thou into the joy of thy Lord.’ ! 


CHAPTER 2 


From the course of the sun we perceive the greatness 


of God. 


Arter this the same prophet celebrates the most won- 
derful course of the sun: ‘It rejoiceth,’ saith he, “as : 
a giant to run his course.’2 A mighty giant surely, if 
he steps out according to the size of his body and runs 
swiftly according to his strength, covers an immense 
distance in a short space of time. And as the prophet, 
in order to explain in such fashion as he was able, the 
beauty of the sun, had compared it to a bridegroom, 
so he afterwards compared it toa giant, in order that, 
using the same resemblance, he might explain in such 
manner_as was possible the swiftness of its course. 

But if he had not compared it with a man, however 
great and strong he may be, but to flying birds, arrows, 
winds, lightning, we should yet be very far from arriv- 
ing at the truth: for if that be true which we perceive 
with our eyes, namely that the sun runs in four-and- 
twenty hours through the whole compass of its orbit, 
and if the compass of the sun’s orbit surpasses almost 
by an infinite degree the course of the earth, and if the 
orbit of the earth includes about twenty thousand miles, 
which is absolutely true—then without doubt it must 
follow that each hour the sun runneth many thousands 
of miles: and if I say every hour, I may say it also of 
every quarter of an hour, or of almost every minute. 

If any one will take notice of the sunrise or sunset, 
particularly in a clear horizon, such as one finds at sea 
or in a plain, he will perceive that the whole body of 


t St. Jas.1.175; St. Matt. xxv. 21. De abe dv als 


96 Tur Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


the sun rises above the horizon in a short space of time, 
as it were in less than the eighth part of an hour: and 
yet the diameter of the sun is much greater than the 
diameter of the earth, which notwithstanding contains 
seven thousand miles. 

I myself was once desirous of learning what space 
of time the sun would take in setting. At the begin- 
ning of its setting I began to read the Psalm Miserere 
mei Deus: and I had scarce read it twice through when 
the whole sun had set. 

And so in the short space of time during which 
the Psalm Miserere can be twice read, the sun must 
traverse in his course a distance much greater than 
seven thousand miles. Who would believe this, unless 
reasoning certainly proved it? And if we add, that 
the body which moves so quickly is a mass greater than 
the whole earth, and accomplishes this swift movement 
without break or fatigue, and, should God command, 
could continue thus for ever : certainly every one but 
he who is stupid or a dunce must admire the infinite 
power of the Creator. 

Truly therefore writes the Wise Man, ‘ This is a 
marvellous instrument, the work of the Most High,’ 
and truly, “ Great is the Lord that made it.’ 


CHAPTER 3 


The sun gives light and heat; God gives true 
wisdom and charity. 


THERE remains the property of light and heat, of which 
David says, * There is nothing hid from the heat there- 
of.”t ‘This one luminous body placed in the midst of 
the universe gives light to all the stars, to the whole 
atmosphere, to all the sea and the whole land, and with 
its life-giving heat makes all the plants of the earth, all 


Lo Pssxrixe 4. 


CoNSIDERATION OF THE HEAVENS 97 


the seeds and trees, live and blossom, to bring fruit to 
perfection, and puts forth its power even beneath the 
earth, producing all kinds of metals. 

Wherefore Saint James in the beginning of his 
Epistle compares God himself with the sun: ‘ Every 
good gift and every perfect giving cometh down from 
the Father of lights, with whom is no variableness, 
neither shadow of turning.’ _ As the sun is the parent 
of corporeal lights, God is the Father of spiritual lights, 
but in three respects there is great unlikeness between 
Godandthesun. For first, the sun, as it fills the whole 
earth with its light and heat, has need of constant 
change, but God, being whole in all places, needs 
no change: and therefore Saint James saith, * with 
whom is no variableness.” In the second place the 
sun, because it is always changing its place, makes, 
by turns, day for some and night for others, shines 
upon some and shadows others: but God never moves 
and is always present to all: and so Saint James 
adds, ‘ with whom is no shadow of turning.’ And 
lastly, what is greatest of all, all gifts and benefits 
which come from the earth are produced by the sun 
as the father of corporeal lights. These are indeed 
good, but they are not the best, nor are they perfect: 
indeed they are limited, temporal, failing, and do not 
make a man good: and those who will may use them 
amiss, and many men use them to their hurt: but from 
the Father of spiritual lights all the most excellent 
and perfect gifts descend, making him who possesses 
them very good and perfect, and no man can mis- 
use them: but they lead him who perseveres to the 
state of true happiness which is the sum of all good 
things. 

Wherefore, my soul, inquire what are those best 
and perfect gifts which come down from above from 
the Father of lights, and when thou hast found them, 

t St. Jas. 1. 17- 
H 


98 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


eager for them and with all thy strength strive to 
obtain them. 

But there is no need to seek far, for the sun itself 
will show them sufficiently. The sun accomplishes all 
by light and heat, and these, namely light and heat, are 
gifts bestowed by the Father of corporeal lights: so 
also the best gifts and perfect givings which are from 
above and descend from God, the true Father of lights, 
are the light of wisdom and the disposing of charity. 

The light of wisdom, which makes a man truly wise 
and which no one can misuse, and which leads one to 
the fountain of wisdom in the heavenly country, is that 
which teaches a man to despise temporal things and 
to esteem heavenly things ; it teaches a man ‘ not to 
trust in uncertain riches, but in the living God ’;! it 
teaches him not to make this place of exile into his 
fatherland, and not to love this pilgrimage, but rather 
to endure it: and lastly it teaches him to pass this 
present life in patience, since it is full of dangers and 
temptations, and that death is to be an object of desire, 
for “ blessed are the dead that die in the Lord.’ 2 

The ordering of true charity, what is it, but to love 
God without limit and without measure, who is the 
end of all desires: and other things which are but 
means to an end, to love with measure and only to such 
a degree as is necessary to the end, that is to the attain- 
ment of blessedness. 

For certainly there is not one among the children of 
men who in respect-of the care of his body will so over- 
turn this order as to love health moderately and a 
bitter draught immoderately, when he knows that the 
one is the end and the other but the means. How does 
it happen that many who desire to be considered wise 
persons, keep no measure in heaping up riches, in 
seeking after carnal pleasures and in pursuing honours, 
as if in these good things consisted the end of the

a

heart’s desire: but in loving God and in seeking 
eternal happiness they are satisfied with so little as if 
it were the means to an end and not the end of all 
other things? ‘The reason is this, that they have the 
wisdom of this world and not the wisdom that is from 
above which cometh down from the Father of lights, 
and because they have not ordered charity and so have 
not true charity, which cannot but be ordered; for they 
are filled with covetousness, which is not of the Father 
but of the world. Thou therefore, my soul, while thou 
art wandering as a pilgrim away from thy native land 
and amongst enemies, who make a pretence of true 
wisdom and true charity, and for wisdom offer crafti- 
ness and for charity, covetousness: pour forth thy 
groans with all thy heart before the Father of lights, 
that he will cause the best and perfect gifts, namely 
the light of wisdom and the ardour of ordered charity, 
to descend into thy heart: that, filled therewith, thou 
mayst with sure foot run the way of God’s command- 
' ments’ and reach that native land where thou wilt 
drink of the very fountain of wisdom and live by the 
pure milk of charity. 


CHAPTER 4 


The moon is subject to the sun: the soul united with 
God is truly enlightened. 


I come now to the night, in which the heaven by means 
of moon and stars maketh a step to ascend unto God, 
for thus saith David, ‘ I will consider the heavens, even 
the works of thy fingers: the moon and the stars which 
thou hast ordained.’ 

If we were able to see the heaven itself, the prophet 
would not have said, as though explaining what had 
gone before, ‘ the moon and the stars which thou hast 


t Ps, viii. 3. 


100 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


ordained ’: and indeed if our senses could reach out 
to heaven itself or if we could search out its nature 
and properties by certain reasoning, doubtless there 
we should have an excellent ladder by which to find 
God. 

We know that there were some who by the move- 
ment of the stars defined the nature of the sky as a 
fifth element, simple, incorruptible, and continually 
moving in a circle « and we know that there have been 
others also who would have heaven to be the element 
of fire, which does not move circularly and which is 
in some parts incorruptible. 

We do not seek opinions, but certain knowledge 
or teaching of faith, that of this we may build a firm 
ladder towards the knowledge of God. Therefore we 
will content ourselves with raising a ladder as the 
prophet does, of the moon and stars which we see, as 
a short while ago we made a step of the sun, which is 
the light and the chief light of all the luminaries. 

The moon has two properties which may help us to 
ascend and to reach to God. First, the nearer it 
approaches the sun, so much the more ‘does it shine in 
the upper part next to the sky and the darker it is in 
the lower part next to earth, and when it is altogether 
beneath the sun and entirely joined with it, then is it 
wholly light toward heaven and dark toward earth. 
Again, when it is opposite the sun, to those who dwell 
on the earth the whole appears to be shining, and the 
upper part towards heaven hath no light. 

This property of the moon may teach men to under- 
stand how careful they ought to be of their nearness 
to, and subjection to, and union with God, the true 
Father of lights. The moon signifies man, the sun 
/ signifies God. When the moon is opposite the sun, 
then with light borrowed from the sun it looks upon 
the earth alone, and as it were turns its back to the 
sky: whence it appears then most beautiful to those 


CoNSIDERATION OF THE HEAVENS IOI 


who dwell on the earth, but to the dwellers in heaven 
it appears misshapen. 

Thus mortal men, when like the prodigal son they 
have gone away from their father and departed to a 
far country, abusing the light of reason which they 
received from the Father of lights, looking only upon 
the earth and forgetting God, think only of earth, love 
only the earth, and are wholly occupied in amassing 
its good things: by the children of this world they 
are considered wise and happy, but by those who 
dwell in heaven are judged poor and naked and blind 
and crippled and wretched and miserable.t 

But, on the contrary, the moon in conjunction with 
the sun is completely subjected to it, and the whole 
shines from the upper part and looks at heaven only, 
while it turns as it were its back to the earth and 
vanishes from the eyes of men. 

Thus when a wicked man begins to turn to piety 
and by a perfect conversion is by humility made subject 
to God the true sun of souls, and united to him by 
charity, then he fulfils the admonition of the Apostle, 
that he should seek the things that are above, where 
Christ is at the right hand of God, and set the mind 
on things above, not on things on the earth; 2 and then 
by fools he is despised and reckoned as dead. For 
truly such a man is dead to the world and his life is hid 
with Christ in God: but when Christ, who is his life, 
shall appear, then will he also appear with him in 
glory, as the same Apostle adds in the same place. 

And this is the reason (as Saint Augustine notes 
in his epistle to Januarius) why the Lord’s Passover, 
both under the old and under the new law, cannot be 
rightly kept except after the full moon, that is when the 
moon, which at the full is opposite, begins, by turning, 
to return towards conjunction with the sun: for by 
this heavenly sign God willed to show that it was by 

Tt Apoc. iil. 17. 2 Col, iii\1, 2. 


102 Tue Munp’s Ascent To Gop 


the Passion and Resurrection of the Lord that man, by 
reason of his sin opposed to God, began to return to 
him and by the merits of Jesus Christ attained to his 
favour and to union with him. 

But, my soul, if by the inspiration of the grace of 
God thou findest thyself, perchance, subject by a true 
humility to the Father of lights and happily made one 
with him by ardent charity, do not imitate those 
foolish persons who change like the moon, but strive 
to be like the wise who remain as the sun, as the Wise 
Man testifies, ‘ A fool changeth as the moon.’ ! 

For while the moon quickly approaches conjunc- 
tion, it just as quickly turns back again : but if thou 
wilt be wise, do not waste the grace once received, do 
not depart, do not retire, for in no place wilt thou find 
anything better, and thou dost not know if a return 
will be granted thee, if thou departest of thine own 
accord : for he who promised pardon to the penitent 
and grace to those who return, hath not promised thee 
a long life nor the gift of penitence. Therefore be 
safe and turn thy back to the earth and gaze upon thy 
sun, rest in him and delight thyself in him, abide in 
him: say with the Apostle Peter, ‘ It is good for us to 
be here ’; 2 and with the martyr Ignatius, ‘ It is better 
for me to die for the sake of Jesus Christ than to reign 
over the boundaries of the earth.’3 And likewise let 
it be with thee, that those who savour of the earth 
judge concerning thee, ‘ For he is not approved whom 
the world commendeth, but whom God commendeth.’ 4 


CHAPTER 5 
The moon illumining the night and sometimes leaving 
it in darkness symbolizes divine grace. 


THERE is another property of the moon, which God 
also makes to serve his chosen. The moon rules the

night as the sun rules the day, as says Moses! and

little, and sometimes the darkness of night is not re-

with eternal brightness the holy angels and the souls 
of the blessed, with whom is perpetual day: ‘ And 
there shall be no night there,’ says Saint John,3 but

we walk ‘ by faith and not by sight ’;4 and where we 
are guided by the Holy Scriptures as a ‘light shining 
in a dark place,’ as Saint Peter speaks in his second

and by turns sometimes leaves them in the darkness 
of desolation. And, my soul, thou shouldst not be 
overmuch saddened, if thou enjoy no light of con- 
solation, nor shouldst thou be too greatly exhilarated 
if after a short space thou breathest once more in the 
light and consolation of devotion. For God shows 
himself in the night of this world not as a sun, but as 
a moon, and to us puny and insignificant creatures he 
appears now as the full moon with a light of consola-

CHAPTER 6 


The order and concord of the stars presents a picture 
of the heavenly hierarchy. 


Or the ornaments of the sky there remain the stars, 
concerning which the Wise Man says, ‘The beauty of 
heaven, the glory of the stars,’ but he adds at once, 
“giving light in the highest places of the Lord.’ 3 
For whatever is the splendour of the stars, as also that 
of the sun and the moon, is entirely an illumination 
from the Father, and it is not the sun which enlightens 
the world by day nor is it the moon or the stars that 
enlighten the world by night, but it is the Lord who 
dwelleth on high, who illumines the world by means of 
the sun and moon and stars. For it is he himself who, 
as the prophet Baruch says, “ sendeth forth light and 
it goeth, calleth it again and it obeyeth him with fear. 
The stars shined in their watches and rejoiced : when 
he calleth them, they say, Here we be; and so with 
cheerfulness they showed light unto him that made 
them.’4 In these words there is signified the infinite 
power of God, which with incredible ease in one 
moment produces bodies so immense and magnificent, 


1 2 Cor. i. 8. 2 Hom. viii in St. Matt. 3 Ecclus. xliii. 9. 
4 Baruch iil. 33. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE HEAVENS 105 


and adorns them and causes them to work. Our 
expression ‘ to call’ is, with God, ‘to create’: ‘God 
calleth those things which be not as though they 
were,’ * and by his call he maketh them to be what 
they are: and for the stars to say ‘ Here we be,’ is 
nothing else than continually to exist and to work 
according to the voice of their ruler; and ‘to show 
light with cheerfulness unto him that made them,’ is 
promptly and eagerly to obey their Maker and in obedi- 
ence to fulfil their task with great pleasure and joy. 

But an exceeding wonderful thing in regard to the 
stars is this, that though they move very swiftly and 
never pause in that most rapid course, some moving 
slowly and others quickly in their orbits, yet they 
always so maintain their distance and proportion in 
regard to others, that there arises thence a melodious 
and most sweet harmony. Concerning this symphony 
God saith, in the book of Job,2 ‘ Who can tell the 
method of the heavens, and who can still the symphony 
of the skies?’ This is not a symphony of voices or of 
sounds which is perceived by the bodily ear, but the 
harmony of proportion in the movements of the stars, 
which is felt by the ear of the heart. If indeed all the 
stars of the firmament traverse the whole circuit of 
the sky with the same speed once in twenty-four 
hours, yet the seven stars which are called planets or 
wandering stars are spurred on in different motions, 
some swifter, some slower, so that the stars of the 
firmament may be said to imitate the bass notes (to 
use ordinary expressions) and the planets make a 
constant and charming counterpoint. 

But these matters are beyond us, and only those who 
dwell in heaven understand the order of those move- 
ments and hear that harmony. And since the stars, 
keeping their order and always turning in the orbit, 
are not wearied, they seem to resemble noble virgins, 


t Rom. iv. 17. 2 Job xxxviii. 37, Vulg. 


106 Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


skilled in the dance, continually performing most 
delightful movements. 

But, my soul, rise thou a little higher, if thou canst, 
and as thou observest the great splendour of the sun, 
the beauty of the moon, the number and variety of 
other luminaries, the wonderful harmony of the heavens 
and the delightful movements of the stars, consider: 
what it will be to see God above the heavens, as it 
were a sun, ‘ dwelling in the light which no man can 
approach unto ’;! to gaze upon that Virgin, the Queen 
of heaven, who, ‘ fair as the moon,’? maketh joyful 
the whole city of God; to look upon the bands and 
ranks of many thousands of angels, who more in 
number than the stars and brighter than they, adorn 
the heaven of heavens ; to perceive the souls of holy 
men, amongst the choirs of angels, as planets mingled 
with the stars of the firmament ; and what it will be 
to hear the songs of praise and that endless Alleluia 
burst forth most sweetly from united voices in the 
streets of that city. 

Thus it will come to pass that the beauty of the 
heavens will not appear so very great, and the things 
that are beneath the sky will seem altogether insignifi- 
cant, indeed almost nothing, and to be considered 
despicable and worthy of contempt.

EIGHTH STEP 


CONSIDERATION OF THE RATIONAL 
SOUL 


CHAPTER I 


The Soul is a created spirit: God uncreated Spirit 
and the Creator of all things. 


UE to this point we have gone through all created 
things, striving with reflection of our mind to 
rise from created things to the Creator. 

Now we find human souls higher in dignity than all 
bodies, and we know that they belong to the lowest 
order of spiritual beings, between whom and God we 
find no intermediate being, save the hierarchies and 
ranks of angels. 

The human soul possesses such a likeness to God its 
Creator, that I hardly know whether by any other way 
a man could ascend to God more easily than by con- 
sideration of his own soul. 

Thus it is that God wills man to be without excuse 
if he has no knowledge of God, since by the help of 
God he may easily draw it from the knowledge he 
possesses of his own soul.t 

First, therefore, man’s soul is a spirit, for thus the 
holy Fathers explain those words in Genesis, ‘ The 
Lord formed man of the dust of the ground, and 
breathed into his nostrils the breath of life’; 2 and the 
word of Tobit, ‘ Command my spirit to be taken from 
me ’;3 and that of the Wise Man, ‘ Then shall the 


1 Rom. i. 20. 2 Gen. ii. 7. 3 Tobit iii. 6. 
107 


108 Tue Muinp’s Ascent tro Gop 


dust return to the earth as it was: and the spirit shall 
return to God who gave it.’ ! 

Although this word spirit means also wind, of which 
it is said in the Psalms, ‘ Wind and storm fulfilling 
his word,’2 and in the Gospel, ‘The wind bloweth 
where it listeth and thou hearest the sound thereof ’: 3 
yet there is no doubt that the wind is a very delicate 
body which on account of its extreme rarity resembles 
more the spiritual nature than any other body. 

The human soul is properly spirit, not body, and 
it is not produced from any substance, but is created 
by God, a truth concerning which there is no dispute 
among Catholics. Here, therefore, begins the excel- 
lence of the soul and its likeness to God: for God is 
Spirit, as the Saviour plainly declares, ‘ God is Spirit, 
and they that worship him must worship him in spirit 
and truth.’4 But although God is a Spirit and the 
human soul is a spirit, yet God is a Spirit uncreated 
and the Creator, and the soul is a created spirit : from 
this it follows that there is an infinite distance between 
the spirit which is the human soul and the Spirit which 
is God. Wherefore greatly may the soul rejoice in 
being spiritual in the nature of its being, and therefore 
by the nobility of its nature superior to the heavens 
and the stars, and also how deeply ought it to humble 
itself and submit to God its Creator, since from nothing 
it was made, and is of itself nothing ! 


CHAPTER 2 


The soul is immortal: God is eternal. 


Next, the human soul, because it is simple spirit, ts 
also immortal: for it possesses naught in itself whence 
it can be divided or be made to perish. 


t Eccles. xii. 7. 2 Ps. cxlviii. 8. 3 St. John iii, 8. 
4 ibid., iv. 24. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE RaTIONAL SOUL 109 


But in this matter also, in the degree in which it is 
able to boast in its superiority over the souls of brute 
animals which perish along with their bodies, to that 
degree also it ought to understand the nobility of its 
Creator and to marvel at it, for he is not only immortal, 
but also eternal. For there was a time when the 
human soul did not exist, and it came into being by 
the will of God alone, and by the will of the same God 
it can be reduced to nothing, although in itself it has 
no element of corruption. And so rightly the Apostle 
spake concerning God, ‘ Who only hath immortality ’:1 
for it is he alone who can be dissolved by no force, no 
change, no reasoning, since he is in himself Being, 
Life itself, and the Fountain of being and of life. 


CHAPTER 3 


The human soul is endowed with reason: God 
is Light and Intelligence. 


TurrRDLy, the human soul is endowed with intelli- 
gence: for it recognizes not only colours, flavours, 
scents, sounds, heat, cold, hardness, softness, and other 
things of that kind which are evident to the bodily 
senses; but it also discriminates substances, and not 
only separately but altogether ; and not only does it 
know present events, but conjectures the future, and 
by discoursing transcends the heavens, pierces. the 
depths, from causes searches out effects and from 
effects turns back again to the cause; and lastly, with 
the apex of his mind a man touches God himself, 
: Who dwelleth in the light which no man can approach 
unto ’;? and this is the light concerning which Saint 
John saith in his Gospel, “ That was the true light 
which lighteth every man that cometh into the world,’ 3 
and David in the Psalms, ‘ Lord, lift thou up the light 


t 1 Tim. vi. 16. 2 ibid. 3 St. John i. 9. 


ILO Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


of thy countenance upon us,’! and ‘ Be ye not like 
unto horse and mule which have no understanding.’ 2 

Great is the dignity of the soul, by possessing which 
a man is like unto God and unlike the beasts: and 
hence man can and should conjecture how great is the 
eminence and sublimity of God, his Creator. 

The soul is endowed with the light of Coe 
ing, but God is Light and Understanding. * 

The soul passes from cause to effect and ae effect 
to cause, and in the discourse it pursues knowledge, 
but with considerable labour; God by a single and 
simple intuition knows all things perfectly. 

The soul understands the things which exist and 
thus its knowledge depends on things; God by under- 
standing makes things to be as they are, and thus the 
existence of things depends on the knowledge of 
God. 

The soul conjectures concerning the future ; God 
always beholds all future things not less clearly than 
the past and the present. 

The soul is in need of many things that it may 
exercise the gift of intelligence, e.g. an object, species, 
an image or other things; God is in need of nothing, 
for he himself is his own Essence, and it is all things 
to him: so also his own Essence is his understanding. 

Lastly, the soul, so long as it is in the body, not only 
does not see God, but sees not the angels, nor does it 
even see itself or any corporeal substance properly; 
and it makes mistakes in many things, is ignorant of 
much, has an opinion about many things, but true 
knowledge of very few: whereas God is ignorant of 
nothing, surmises nothing, never is mistaken, never 
errs, ‘ All things are naked and opened unto his 
eyes,’ as the Apostle says in the Epistle to the 
Hebrews.3 

Therefore if a man makes so much of his knowledge, 


LOPS iwds 2 Ps, xxxil. 10. 3 Heb. iv. 13. 


CoNSIDERATION OF THE RaTIONAL SouL_ ITI 


as the Apostle says, “ Knowledge puffeth up,’* how - 
greatly should the Creator’s knowledge be admired, 
compared with which all our knowledge is not know- 
ledge but ignorance ! 


CHAPTER 4 


The soul has practical knowledge : in the Mind of God 


is the supreme law of righteousness and justice. 


FourTHLY, there flourishes in the human soul another 
kind of knowledge, which consists, not in speculation, 
but in action. 

Why are there so many books of philosophers con- 
cerning vices and virtues, so many laws of princes and 
counsels of lawyers, so many institutions and exercises 
setting forth the art of living well? In these there is 
wonderfully perceived the light of human reason, by 
which we are separated a very long distance from the 
beasts. But all these things are nothing to the eternal 
law which exists in the mind of the Creator, from which 
as from a most fertile source proceed all laws and all 
commandments: ‘ There is one lawgiver, God,’ says 
Saint James in his Epistle. He himself is truth and 
righteousness and wisdom, by whom ‘kings reign 
and princes decree justice.’3 Hence thou wilt never 
discover the art of living well and happily until, entered 
into the school of Christ who is the true and only 
master,4 thou hast learnt, by his word and example, 
that righteousness which exceeds the righteousness of 
the Scribes and Pharisees 5 and, I may even add, of 
the philosophers, and the end of which is ‘ Charity 
out of a pure heart and of a good conscience and of 
faith unfeigned.’ 6

CHAPTER 5 


The soul of man is an inventor of arts. God is the 
Fountain of genius and of the wisdom which invents 
things. 

FirrHty, the soul of man possesses a third kind of 

knowledge, which consists in cleverly fashioning things. 

Indeed, spiders know how to weave their nets, and 

birds know how to build nests, and bees know how to 

make honey, foxes how to seek out holes for houses; 
but these animals by a kind of natural instinct accom- 
plish one and the same thing in the same manner. 

But the human soul, endowed with reason of judgement, 

discovers. numberless arts by which it rules and dom- 

inates all animals, whether they will or no: so that 
birds find their wings of no assistance, fishes are not 
helped by deep water, lions and bears by their remark- 
able strength, nor horses and mules by their fierceness, 
nor stags and goats by their harmfulness. Indeed, 
even boys take birds with snares ‘and lime and nets, 
and fishermen catch fish with hook and nets. Men 
with skill and art shut up lions and bears in iron cages 
and lead them about, inclose bulls and stags with ropes 
or stab them with a spear, tame horses and mules with 

a bit and have learnt to subdue them to their control. 
What shall I say of the art of navigation ? Whata 

great light of genius in the human soul is shown forth 
when great ships laden with heavy weights pass over 
the sea, not only by means of oars, as though on foot, 
but even by sails, as though they had learnt to fly with 
wings. 

What shall I say of agriculture ? Who is not as- 
tounded at the intelligence of man, when he examines 
the cornfields, vineyards, orchards, gardens, wells, 
various streams of water led for watering the gardens 
and moistening the fields ? 

What of architecture ? Who does not wonder at 


CONSIDERATION OF THE RaTIONAL SOUL 113 


the palaces, temples, cities, citadels, towers, amphi- 
theatres, pyramids, obelisks ? 

I leave aside the arts of painting and sculpture by 
which the faces of men, and other things yet living, 
are so depicted with paint in pictures, or sculptured 
in marble that one would believe them real, not painted 
or carved. 

I leave aside other arts of human genius, which are 
either necessities of life or which minister to conveni- 
ence or pleasure; they are so numerous as scarcely to 
be counted. 

Therefore, my soul, give thanks to God who hath 
willed to separate by so great a distance thy nature 
from the nature of animals : and at the same moment 
lift the eyes of thy mind to thy Creator himself, in 
whom is the fount of intellect and of the wisdom that 
accomplishes all these things. From that fountain 
flowed whatever degree of intelligence hath come to 
thy nature: and if thou marvellest at the genius of 
man which has taught him by industry and skill to 
control those animals which lack reason, admire still 
more thy God, whom all things serve and obey, and not 
only animate creatures but inanimate. And if it seem 
to thee a great matter that a man should discover so 
many methods of navigating the seas, of cultivating 
fields, building houses, why doth it not appear to thee 
a far greater thing that God in his wisdom hath made 
heaven and earth, the seas and all things that are 
therein, and controlleth and ruleth all ? 

And if, lastly, thou art amazed at the art of painting 
pictures as though living countenances were drawn 
from marble, why art thou not amazed at the skill of 
thy Creator, who formed a real and living man from 
dust and from his rib formed a real and living woman, 
especially if thou add that what men do cannot be 
done without the help of God, and what God doth is 
done by himself alone, with aid from no other being ?

CHAPTER 6 


The free judgement of man is compared with the 
freedom of God. 


In the sixth place, the soul of man is endowed with a 
free judgement of the will, which is common to him 
with God himself and with the angels, and which 
separates him from other created beings. Great 1s 
this nobility and a wonderful excellence. 

But in God, the Creator of all things, liberty exceeds 
it so greatly that the freedom of the soul, when com- 
pared with it, seems scarcely to be its shadow. 

First, the liberty of the human will is weak, and 
easily prone to choose for itself evil and harmful things: 
the freedom of the divine will is very strong and can 
in no way fail or be inclined to evil. For as to be 
subject to death is a proof of the weakness of the mortal 
body, to be unable to die is a proof of the strength of 
the glorified body: so also to be able to sin is a weak- 
ness of the free judgement, not to be able to sin will 
be the health of the judgement when in the heavenly 
country God will bestow upon us by grace that which 
he himself for ever possesses by nature. 

Hence our free judgement is indeed free, for it 1s 
able to will and not to will, or even to will and to refuse: 
but it is not able to accomplish what it wills, or to cause 
that what it does not will should not take place, even 
as regards itself, and how much more in regard to 
others. Hear the Apostle lamenting in his letter to 
the Romans, * For the good that I would I do not, but 
the evil which I would not, that I do.’ And who of us 
has not experienced this? I will to pray to God with 
attention, and I bid my imagination not to wander 
while I am giving myself to prayer, and not to draw 
me away to think of other things and I cannot hold it 
to its duty: and when I| am less careful about this 

t Rom. vil. 19. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE RaTIONAL SouL I15 


matter I find myself deluded by imagination, prayer 
is interrupted and I have slipped off to the considera- 
tion of other things. I have a will not to desire evil 
things and not to be angered beyond reason, and by 
a free judgement I command the irascible and con- 
cupiscible faculties which are within me, and which 
ought to be subdued to reasonable law, I command 
them to submit entirely to the rule of reason and not 
to allow themselves to be led away in any manner by 
the bodily senses: and often they do not obey, and they 
do not what I desire, but what I do not will. But what 
is far more astonishing and wretched is that the mind 
commands the body and is obeyed immediately, the 
mind commands itself and is resisted. “Whence this 
monstrousness ?’ saith Saint Augustine. ‘The mind 
commands the hand to be moved: and such readiness 
is there, that command is scarce distinct from obedi- 
ence: yet the mind is mind, and the hand is body. 
The mind commands the mind, its own self, to will, 
and yet it doth not. . . . But it willeth not entirely, 
therefore doth it not command entirely. It is therefore 
no monstrousness, but a disease of the mind, that it 
doth not wholly rise, by truth up-borne, borne down 
by custom.’ ! 

But the freedom of will of the Lord God is so joined 
with absolute power that it is written concerning it, 
‘ He hath done whatsoever pleased him,’ 2 and ‘There 
is none that can resist thy will.’ 3 

Wherefore, my soul, if thou art wise, boast not con- 
cerning the power of a judgement free from control, 
until thou comest to ‘ the glorious liberty of the chil- 
dren of God,’ 4 when the heavenly Physician shall have 
healed all thy weakness, and shall have fulfilled thy 
desires for good things. In the meanwhile earnestly 
bemoan thyself and say to God with the prophet,

CHAPTER 7 


The will of the rational soul can seek after spiritual good 
things : God is himself the highest good, that is Love. 


SEVENTH : the soul of man hath a reasoning will, which 
not only is able to seek after present, particular, and 
corporeal goods, such as even animals desire; but also 
good things afar off, general and spiritual, which are 
made known by faith and reason, and so also that very 
highest and infinite good which is God. 

And this it is which makes a man capable of great 
virtues, and especially of the queen of all virtues, 
namely Love. 

Brute animals love, but with ‘a love of concupis- 
cence; they are altogether ignorant of the love of 
friendship. But thou, O soul, God has made capable 
of that very gift which is the fount of all gifts, and 
which so unites thee with himself, the highest good, 
that he abides in thee and thou in him, as it were a 
most beautiful charity. ‘God is love, and he that 
dwelleth in love dwelleth in God, and God in him.’ 3 

But if the good of the created will is so great, how 
great will be that good in which is the perfect uncreated 
will ! 

Only the will of God is capable of infinite love, by 
which the infinite goodness of God is worthy to be 
loved. And that will is not lacking in virtues, nor has 
it need of being directed by the intellect: for it and 
intellect are one and the same thing, as wisdom and 
love are one thing in God.

CONSIDERATION OF THE RationaL SouL 117 


CHAPTER 8 


The presence of the soul in the body is a picture of the 
existence of God in created things. 


EicHTH: the human soul is in the human body, but in 
a way very different from that in which the souls of 
beasts are within their bodies. 

The souls of brutes are material, and extend to the . 
extent of the body, so that part is in a part, and the 
whole in the whole. But the human soul which is 
indivisible spirit, is in a wonderful way wholly in the 
whole and also whole in each part: and when it fills 
the whole body it nevertheless occupies no place in 
the body: and when the body grows, the soul does not 
grow, but that which before was not, now begins to be: 
and if a limb is cut off or burnt, the soul is not on that 
account lessened or dried up, but in that limb, where 
it was before, it ceases to exist, and without any bruise 
or mutilation of itself. 

This is a true picture of the existence of God in 
created things. For God is indivisible Spirit: and 
although he fills the whole world and all its parts, he 
does not occupy any space, and he is wholly in the 
whole world and wholly in any part of it: and when a ~ 
new creature is produced, God begins to exist in it, 
and yet he is not moved: and when any creature ts 
perchance destroyed or dies, God is not destroyed, nor 
does he die, but he ceases to be in that creature, and 
does not change his place. 

God surpasses the soul by a long way. If the soul 
is in the body and controls and moves it, it has of 
necessity to take the form of the body, and to be united 
with it so that of soul and body one man should be 
made. God does not need that a form should be pro- 
duced, nor that the world should have a soul, nor that 
of himself and the world one substance should be pro- 
duced : but from his own immensity, he has this, that 


118 Tue Muinp’s Ascent To Gop 


wherever he is, because of his indivisible unity, he is 
wholly everywhere, and that because of his omnipo- 
tence he should rule all things, bear all things, move 
all things. 

Next, the soul, although it may be said to exist in 
the whole body, yet it does not belong to it, except in 
the living and animate parts; hence it is not in the 
humours, in the hair, nails, or in limbs which are 
withered or dead: but God is absolutely in all things, 
not only in corporeal, but also in spiritual, and it is not 
possible that anything should exist in which God is not. 
Besides, the soul does not exist except in its own body, 
and in that narrow and confined space all the parts are 
gathered together: for if any part is separated from 
the others, in that part the soul cannot exist. 

But God is wholly in this whole, although the whole 
is exceeding great, and its parts, though very close, not 
united together, and if another world were formed and 
yet another, God would be in them all. For thus it is 
written, “ Heaven and the heaven of heavens cannot 
contain thee.’ And if other heavens and other earths 
should be formed, in them also he would be: and if 
new heavens and new earths were multiplied without 
end he would fill all things, and where he was not there 
would be altogether nothing. 


CHAPTER 9 


The soul is a kind of image of the Most Holy. 
Trinity. 


Nintu : besides what has been said, the soul of man 
possesses in itself an image, though obscure, of the 
divine Trinity: since it has a fertile memory, strength 
of intellect, power of loving, and the mind also by its 
intelligence forms its own word, and from the mind 


1 2 Chron. vi. 18. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE RaTIoNAL SOUL II19 


and the word, love issues: because that which is under- 
stood by the mind and represented by the word, as 
good, is continually loved and desired. 

- But by a far higher and diviner mode God the 
Father begets God the Word, and the Father and the 
Word breathe forth God the Holy Spirit, who is living 
Love and the living Fount of all pure love. And for 
this reason the mystery of the Trinity surpasses natural 
means of knowledge, and it is not possible to attain 
to understanding it without supernatural light, how- 
ever learned a philosopher may be. 

And the soul of man produces the word and love, 
which are not substances but accidents, and therefore 
are not persons: but God the Father begets the Word 
of one substance with himself and the Father and the 
Word breathe forth the Holy Spirit, of like Substance 
with them both, whence the three Persons are rightly 
called Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. 

Next, the soul of man produces a word which does 
not last long, and the will produces love which has 
no long continuance ; but God the Father begets the 
eternal Word and the Father and the Son breathe 
forth the eternal Holy Spirit, for God without the 
Word and without his Spirit cannot exist. 

Lastly, the soul of man by one word represents one 
thing, and so he multiplies words, not only of the mind, 
but of the lips, and the human will is forced to produce 
many acts of love if it desire to love many objects: but 
God speaks all truth by one single Word, and loves 
all good things by one act of love. 


CHAPTER IO 


The soul contributes natural gifts to the body: God 
bestows heavenly gifts on the soul. 


Last y, the human soul has this quality, that while it is 
in the body, although unseen and unheard, though it 


120 THe Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


does not move and is scarce known to exist in the body, 
and though when it is withdrawn nothing appears to 
be wanting to the body, yet it bestows all good things 
upon the body—sense, movement, speech, substance, 
‘ beauty, strength. 

For why does a man live, see, hear, speak, walk, 
subsist ; why is he strong, handsome, lovable, unless 
there is a soul within him? And why is it that after 
death he neither sees, hears, speaks, nor moves but lies 
misshapen, useless, and unseen, unless the soul have 
departed, whence all those good things are derived ? 

Thus, O soul, thy God, while he lives in thee by his 
grace, himself causes thee to see what faith shows thee: 
causeth thee to hear what the Lord speaketh within 
thee, to walk by the way of his commandments to the 
heavenly Jerusalem: to speak in prayer unto God and 
to thy neighbour in holy exhortation: to live by per- 
severing in good works and to be strong in battle 
against invisible enemies, and glorious in the eyes of 
the invisible God and of his angels. 

But beware, lest the grace of God be withdrawn, 
for it is the life of thy soul, and if it be withdrawn thou 
wilt experience loss which will bring with it the first 
death: whence thou mayst be snatched away to the 
second death, from which no resurrection is vouch- 
safed.1 

O if the Lord would open thine eyes and thou 
couldst gaze upon the glorious form and exceeding 
splendour bestowed upon the soul that is pleasing to 
God and is united to him by true charity: and if thou 
couldst see with what eyes God beholdeth her when 
he prepares a place for her, what joys he promises, 
and with how great a longing she is awaited by the 
angels and other blessed spirits: thou certainly wouldst 
not be able to suffer such beauty to be defiled by any, 
even a slight, stain: and if perchance that should

happen, thou wouldst forthwith strive to wipe away 
those spots, even the smallest, in rivers of tears. 

To this indeed Saint Bonaventura refers when he

without contracting any stain, he took the greatest care

as a putrid corpse, and how God and the holy angels 
refuse to look upon it, although perchance it dwells 
within a beautiful body and is in the eyes of men adorned 
and lovely: then without any doubt thou wilt be so 
horrified that for no cause whatever wilt thou suffer 
thyself to become such, or to abide any longer in that

NINTH STEP 
CONSIDERATION OF THE ANGELS 


CHAPTER I 


An Angel is entirely spirit and created perfect: God 
is uncreated Spirit and altogether perfect. 


\ X J E have reached the highest step in our ascension 

to God, the highest of the steps which may be 
taken by created beings: for no created substance 1s 
more sublime than the angelic if we are speaking 
only concerning natural perfection. Therefore we will 
first consider the angels in respect of the excellence of 
their nature, next in regard to their exalted grace, and 
last in respect of the offices which they perform. 
We do not propose to set out a complete discourse 
concerning angels, but only to touch on such matters 
as may assist us in raising our mind to God. 

Now if an angel be compared with a human and 
rational soul, it may fitly be called a perfect soul, while 
the soul may be called an imperfect angel. The 
prophet speaking of the soul of a reasoning man, says, 
* Thou makest him a little lower than the angels ’:? 
for an angel is a whole and perfect spiritual being ; 
the human soul is divided and imperfect, for it has the 
form of a body and for this reason is part of the man. 

And so an angel is pure spirit, man partly spirit, 
partly flesh, or partly an angel and partly a beast, as if 
one were to say, an angel is all gold, man partly gold, 
partly clay. 

1 Ps. viii. 5. 
I22 


CONSIDERATION OF THE ANGELS 123 


Wherefore it is true, as the prophet says, that man 
was made a little lower than the angels, and it is also 
true that the human soul, being part of a man, is a 
little less than an angel. Whence it follows that an 
angel is more like unto God than is a man or his soul, 
because God is Spirit, not body, and hath no bodily 
shape. Nevertheless the greater likeness of the angel 
to God makes plain that God is not a Spirit raised 
above the angelic sublimity by an infinite distance of 
honour, for God is uncreated Spirit, eternal, bound- 
less, alone powerful, alone wise, alone good, alone 
most high. 

Wherefore, my soul, if thou dost acknowledge that 
the angelic nature ought rightly to be reflected by thee, 
how much more shouldst thou respect and admire the 
divine nature which surpasses the dignity of angels in 
every way and beyond all measure. 


CHAPTER 2 
Of the intelligence and knowledge of the Angels. 


Bur not only may it be said that in nature and sub- 
stance an angel is perfect man and man an imperfect 
angel, but it is so in regard to intelligence and know- 
ledge. Thus a man, or a human soul, labours hard in 
matters which have to be learnt, and he must make use 
of the service of the senses and acquire knowledge 
little by little as he passes from effect to cause and from 
cause to effect, while he often remains uncertain, often 
makes serious mistakes, and seldom arrives at certain 
knowledge: whereas an angel by one simple intuition 
understands the matter, perceives at one and the 
same time causes and their effects, penetrates not 
only the accidents, but also to the very substance of 
a matter, and sees not only corporeal things but 
spiritual. 


124 Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


And so man, while he wanders in this world, in 
respect of his understanding is not a little less and 
lower than the angels, but he is so much lowered that 
a man, though in talent he may be skilled and may 
give great attention to the pursuit of wisdom, yet when 
compared with an angel may truly be called a child, or 
even an infant, a babe. 

For rightly did the prophet sing concerning us 
mortals, ‘ Out of the mouths of babes and sucklings 
thou hast perfected praise.’ Hear Solomon, that very 
wise man, and what he thinks of the knowledge by 
which we are so puffed up, ‘ All things are full of 
labour ; man cannot utter it.’2 And again: ‘He 
[God] hath set the world in their heart, so that no man 
can find out the work that God maketh from the 
beginning to the end.’ 3 

If all things are difficult and inexplicable to man, 
and if a man understands nothing of this visible world 
from the first created thing unto the last ; if, I say, he 
understand nothing perfectly, as he seeks to unfold 
its nature, properties, accidents, powers, and the rest 
which lie hidden: in how many mistakes will he be 
involved if he attempts to investigate those things 
which are above the heavens ! 

Wherefore, O soul, if thou art wise, pursue the 
knowledge of salvation and the wisdom of the saints 
which lies in this, that thou fear God and keep his 
commandments: that prayer delight thee more than 
disputations, and the charity which buildeth up more 
than the knowledge which puffeth up ;4 for this is 
the way that leadeth unto life and the kingdom of 
heaven, where to us little ones shall be given equality 
with the angels who ‘always behold the face of the 
Father which is in heaven.’ 5 


t Ps, viii. 2. 2 Eccles. i. 8. 3 ibid., iii. rr. 
4 x Cor, vill. I. 5 St. Matt. xviii. ro.

CHAPTER 3 


The rule exercised by Angels over bodies and the 
omnipotence of the Creator. 


But there is a third point in which the human soul is 
not only a little less but very much beneath an angel: 
namely, the power and control over bodies. For the 
human soul has power to move its own body only, by 
the exercise of its will, it cannot in this way move other 
bodies; it moves its own body by a progressive move- 
ment over the ground, but it cannot suspend it over 
water or lift it above the air and carry it whither it 
wills: but angels, by a single motion of their spirit, 
that is by the exercise of the will, carry heavy 
bodies aloft and bear them whither they will: thus 
one angel seized Habbacuc and in a very short 
space of time carried him to Babylon that he might 
bring food to Daniel, and then carried him back to 
Palestine. 

Secondly, a man cannot fight against his enemies 
with his spirit alone, he needs hands and arms : but an 
angel, without hands and without arms, can war against 
a whole host of armed men by a single onset of his 
spirit and can conquer: thus did one angel once slay 
a hundred and eighty-five thousand Assyrians. And 
if an angel is able to do this, what cannot the Creator 
and Lord of the angels do? He who made everything 
out of nothing could, if he willed, bring all things back 
to nothing. 

The human mind, exercising great industry and 
labour, by the arts of painting and sculpture can form 
pictures of men, which so represent the living men that 
they seem to breathe and to be alive ; angels, without 
labour and without hands or tools and almost in a 


1 Bel and the Dragon, 32. 2 2 Kings xix. 36. 


126 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


moment of time, can so fashion for themselves a body 
out of the elements, that it is taken to be a human 
body, even by wise persons, since it walks, speaks, 
eats, drinks, is able to be touched and felt and even 
to be bathed. Thus Abraham prepared food for the 
angels and washed their feet; and as the Apostle 
explains he received angels as his guests, believing that 
he was receiving men. And the same thing happened 
to his nephew Lot, when he admitted into his house 
two angels, taking them to be pilgrim men.3 Again, 
Raphael the angel spent many days with the young 
man Tobias, walking, speaking, eating, and drinking, 
as if he were truly and really a man, and yet afterwards 
when he was about to depart he said, “ All these days 
did I appear unto you; but I did neither eat nor drink, 
but ye did see a vision,’ and then suddenly he vanished 
from their sight.4 

Great and wonderful power indeed, suddenly to form 
a body which seemed to differ in, no respect from a 
living human body, and suddenly to dissolve that same 
body whenever he willed, so that no trace of it re- 
mained ! And if this power of the angels is so great 
and so admirable, what must be the power of the 
Creator of the angels, who made them and gave them 
the degree of power which he willed? And so our 
knowledge and that of the angels compared with the 
knowledge of God is ignorance, and our righteous- 
ness and that of the angels is unrighteousness : thus 
our power and that of the angels, compared with the 
power of God, is weakness: and therefore in truth 
God is said to be only wise,5 only good,® the only 
Potentate.7

CHAPTER 4 


Of the place and motion of the Angels and of the 


omnipresence of God. 


Last iy, if we consider the position of angels and of 
men, we shall find out here also whether or no the soul 
is not only a little less but very much beneath the 
angels:! I may freely use the word which the Apostle 
uses. For God gave to the human soul a place on 
earth, to the angels a place in heaven, that is in his 
palace, ‘ All the whole heavens are the Lord’s: the 
earth hath he given to the children of men.’ 2 

Hence in Saint Matthew the Lord calls them ‘the 
angels of heaven,’3 in Saint Luke he says, ‘ Joy shall 
be in heaven over one sinner that repenteth,’ and a 
little later, “ There is joy in the presence of the angels 
of God over one sinner that repenteth.’ 4. 

And moreover God has so bound the soul to the 
body that apart from it, it cannot change its place: 
but God has bound the angels to no body, and he has 
bestowed on them power to pass very swiftly from 
heaven to earth and from earth to heaven, or wherever 
they will. Thus an angel, by the dignity of his nature 
nearer to God, also by his subtilty, as it were, imitates 
the omnipresence of God. For God is always every- 
where by the immensity of his nature, and since he 1s 
everywhere, he is in no need of change of place: an 
angel by a very swift movement passes from place to 
place and manifests his presence in all places, so that 
he appears in some way to be everywhere. 

But, my soul, if thou desirest to hear the Lord of the 
angels, there is no reason why thou shouldst envy that 
sublime place in which they are, or their exceeding 
swift motion without fatigue, for not only wilt thou, 
as a soul, when loosed from the body, be equal unto

4 St. Luke xv. 7, 10. 


128 Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


the angels, but when thou hast returned to the body 
which Christ will fashion like unto his glorious body,? 
with that same body thou shalt possess heaven as thine 
own abode, and that body will become spiritual,? and 
without labour or weariness will forthwith be in that 
place where thou, its soul, desirest and biddest it to

in the Gospel : ‘In my Father’s house are many man- 
sions,’ and, ‘I go to prepare a place for you, and if | 
go and prepare a place for you I will come again and 
receive you unto myself, that where I am there ye may 
be also’;3 * Father, I will that they may be with me 
where I am: that they may behold my glory, which 
thou hast given me.’ 4 

And where Christ is, thou knowest, and what sort 
of body he hath; for thou dost daily acknowledge and 
dost say, ‘ The third day he rose again from the dead : 
he ascended into heaven.’ 5 

And thou knowest that after his resurrection his 
body used to enter among the disciples, the doors being 
shut; ® and when he departed he was accustomed to 
leave them, not by walking, but by vanishing: that is, 
he was used to move bodily from place to place by a 
swift motion, as if he were spirit not body. 

But if thou aspirest to that glory, it behoves thee 
first to conform thy body to the body of Christ’s 
humility, that so Christ may conform thy body to his 
glorious body. 

Next it SH Gils thee to follow his steps, ‘ For Christ 
also suffered for us, leaving us an example that we 
should follow his steps,’ says the Apostle Peter: and 
what are his steps? ‘Who did no sin,’ he saith, 
‘neither was guile found in his mouth: who when he 
was reviled reviled not again, when he suffered he 
threatened not.’ 7
