The steps of Christ are two, and if thou wander 
from them, thou wilt lose thy way and never wilt reach 
thy home: it behoves thee not to do evil, but to suffer, 
and as a consequence to do good and not look for good 
things to come to thee here: and, that which is the 
highest of all, it behoves thee to love thy neighbour for 
God’s sake with a true and pure love, a love of friend- 
ship and not of passion, freely and not for the sake of 
a reward from men, content with the reward of God 
which surpasses all measure. 


CHAPTER 5 
The kind of grace that is given to Angels and to men. 


Let us now come to the dignity of angels according to 
grace. Truly in this also man is more than a little 
made lower than the angels. 

In the beginning God created the angels separately 
that he might at the same time form their nature and 
infuse grace, as Saint Augustine testifies in the books 
on the City of God.t Then as by a single turning of 
the mind towards God they clave to him by love, they 
were crowned with bliss and glory, while the wicked 
were cast down. And so their pilgrimage was very 
short and their abode in their native land eternal : if 
indeed that delay can be said to be very short which 
intervened between their creation and their beatitude. 

But we men indeed received grace with our nature 
when we were created, but in our first parent not in our- 
selves, and so when he fell we all fell: as the Apostle 
says, ‘In whom all have sinned’;? and although we 
have been reconciled to God by the Mediator between 
God and men, Christ Jesus,3 yet we are condemned to 
a long exile, and while we remain in the body, wander- 
ing apart from the Lord, we walk by faith and not by 
sight: and because there is much to distress devout 

1 Aug. de Cio. Dei, xii,c.9. * Rom.v.12. 3 1 Tim. ii. 5. 

K 


130 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


men who long for their native land, that is, that we 
have to live in the meanwhile amongst most cruel 
enemies, there is a danger lest we be surrounded and 
taken captive and at length be shut out from the 
possession of that most sweet home. 

Hence these words, ‘ Woe is me that I am con- 
strained to dwell with Mesech: and to have my habita- 
tion among the tents of Kedar.’ ! 

But although in this respect we are inferior to the 
angels, yet the loving-kindness of God doth wonder- 
fully console us, not only in that from our race sprang 
Christ and Mary whom he placed in heaven above all 
angels, but also because he hath willed to make not a 
few of mankind superior to many angels by the gift 
of grace, though they are inferior to them in all 
the endowments of nature, and some he hath made 
even equal to the highest angels. Indeed Saint John 
Chrysostom, expounding the Epistle to the Romans, 
did not hesitate to place Peter and Paul, the chief of 
the Apostles, in that place where seraphim fly and 
glorify God; 2 and the same thing is piously believed 
of Saint John Baptist and some other saints. 

Add to which the good angels after their first service 
attained to glory, and the evil angels after their first 
sin were condemned to everlasting punishment: and 
so men ought not to bemoan the length of the 
way, since in the course of it they may again and 
again correct their failings and by penitence attain 
forgiveness. 


CHAPTER 6 
The Offices of Angels considered. 


Ir remains for us to say a few words concerning the 


duties of angels. They may be said to be five in 
number. 


1 Ps cxx. 4 2 Chry. Hom. xxxii in Ep. ad Rom. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE ANGELS 131 


The first is continually to sing praises and hymns 
to their Creator. And that we may understand how 
much God values this service, we will consider that 
the highest angels are appointed to this duty, and that 
all the choirs of angels follow them as their leaders, 
and join in chorus with wondrous delight. Hear 
Isaiah : “I saw the Lord sitting upon a throne, high 
and lifted up, and his train filled the temple. Above 
it stood the seraphims: each one had six wings; with 
twain he covered his face, and with twain he covered 
his feet, and with twain he did fly. And one cried 
unto another and said, Holy, holy, holy is the Lord 
of hosts: the whole earth is full of his glory.’! Here 
thou hearest the name seraphim, which are the chiefs 
of the highest order: thou beholdest them covering 
face and feet, which signifies their reverence, as though 
they durst not gaze upon their faces or touch their 
bare feet: thou seest them flying eagerly while they 
sing, and this signifies their love and desire always 
increasing of drawing near to God: and these two 
things are necessary for those who desire to please 
God, while they sing his praises, namely that to love 
they should join reverence, and to reverence love, as 
David the prophet expresses when he says, ‘Serve 
the Lord with fear and rejoice unto him with rever- 
ence.’ From this thou mayst learn, O my soul, 
what great veneration is due to God, when those 
highest princes of heaven who always stand before him 
and always see his face, though in such exceeding high 
place and in long familiarity, yet never dare to neglect 
fear and reverence while they sing his praises. 

And what wilt thou answer, thou dust and ashes, 
when in the judgement thou art charged with sleepi- 
ness and wanderings of mind in such a divine work, 
for which thou wast not worthy? Learn at least this, 
since thou art taught by so great an example, that

thou oughtest to perform thy praises and sing thy 
hymns with fear and trembling, with attention and 
watchfulness, with love and longing for thy God. 

The second office of angels is to offer to God the 
prayers of mortal men, and to commend them by their 
own petitions: for thus saith the angel Raphael in 
the book Tobit, ‘ When thou didst pray with tears, 
and when thou didst bury the dead... and when thou 
didst leave thy dinner, I offered thy prayer unto the 
Lord ’;! and in the Apocalypse John saw an angel 
standing before the altar with a golden censer: ‘ And 
there was given unto him much incense, that he should 
offer it with the prayers of all saints upon the golden 
altar which was before the throne.’2 Here indeed is 
seen the incredible clemency and pity of our God ; 
for, not content with exhorting us first by his prophets 
and then by his Son and by his Apostles to pray and 
make petition, he also added the promise to give what- 
soever we shall ask: “Ask,’ he saith, ‘and ye shall 
receive ’;3 and elsewhere, “ Whatsoever ye shall ask 
the Father in my Name, he shall give it you.’4 And 
not content even with this promise, he adds also that 
he will give a reward to them that ask: ‘ But thou, 
when thou prayest, enter into thy closet, and when 
thou hast shut thy door, pray to thy Father which 1s 
in secret, and thy Father which seeth in secret, shall 
reward thee’;5 a reward, namely, beyond that for 
which thou hast asked; for thus saith the Lord in the 
same place concerning almsgiving and fasting, © Thy 
Father which seeth in secret shall reward thee.’® But 
not content with this argument of his fatherly kind- 
ness, God hath appointed angels as it were our most 
intimate attendants, to take charge of the prayers as 
being memorials of the poor, and to offer them before 
him and read them, lest any petition of the poor

should ever be forgotten. Who of earthly princes ever

for them, it ought not to seem much to receive them 
freely and to appoint a faithful attendant to preserve 
the petitions carefully, and to be solicitous for their 
quick despatch. 

The third office of angels consists in this, that they 
are sent as ambassadors to declare those things which 
God desires to make known, and especially those 
things concerning redemption and eternal salvation: 
for thus the Apostle speaks to the Hebrews, ‘Are they 
not all ministering spirits, sent forth to minister to 
them who shall be heirs of salvation ?’! 

Thus in the Old Testament we see that angels 
appeared to patriarchs and prophets and made known 
to them what God had commanded them to make 
known.? So also in the New Testament we read that 
the archangel Gabriel was sent as a messenger to 
Zacharias and to the Virgin Mother of God,3 then that 
angels were sent to the shepherds, 4 to Saint Joseph,5 
after the Resurrection of our Lord to the women 
going to the sepulchre,® and after the Ascension to 
all the disciples.7 

And this seems to be the reason why God, who 1s 
in every place and who can easily speak himself within 
the hearts of men, yet wills to send angels, namely 
that men may understand that human interests are 
matters of concern to God and that by him all things 
are governed and directed; for men might easily

The fourth office of the angels is to protect men,

mortals and to place them in authority, now as it were

to little children, of patron to followers, of shepherd

us with guardians lest we should be snatched away:

TENTH STEP 


CONSIDERATION OF THE ESSENCE 
OF GOD BY COMPARING THE MAGNITUDE

CHAPTER I 


The greatness of God considered in a general way from 
the four dimensions of bodtes. 


Wye have ascended as far as we are able by means 
of created substances: but we have not yet 
attained to that degree of the knowledge of God which 
it is possible to reach by consideration, even in this 
vale of tears. 

And so it remains for us to see whether, by the 
dimensions of bodily quantity which we understand, 
we can rise to the length and breadth and height and 
depth of the Essence of God: for in things created 
those are considered to be great whose four dimensions 
are great. 

Now in the Psalms and elsewhere God is said to be 
great,! and of his greatness there is no end. 

Indeed, Saint Bernard, a remarkable contemplative, 
made for himself from these dimensions steps to the 
knowledge of God, which he wrote in his books con- 
cerning contemplation to Pope Eugenius. And he 
was not the first to invent ladders of this kind; he 
learnt this method of ascension from the Apostle who 
penetrated into the third heaven and into Paradise; 3 


1 Deut. x. 173; Pss. xcv. 3, cxlvii. 5, and others. 2 Ps. cxlv. 3. 
Sa (Core xii..2. 


137 


138 Tue Mrinp’s Ascent to Gop 


for thus speaks the Apostle in his Epistle to the 
Ephesians, ‘ That ye may be able to comprehend with 
all saints what is the breadth and length and depth and 
height.’ 

If any one will consider carefully, he will surely find 
that there is nothing complete or solid outside of 
God, and that everything is narrow, short, lowly, and 
empty, or superficial: but that in God there is true 
breadth, immensity; true length, eternity; true height, 
sublimity of nature ; true depth without limit, incom- 
prehensibility : and again true height, omnipotence ; 
true depth, infinite wisdom ; true breadth, fullest 
bowels of mercy ; true length, discerning judgement, 
that is perfect righteousness. 

Now, if we wish to ascend and to find what we are 
seeking, it is not enough for us to touch lightly on 
these considerations, but it behoves us to understand 
fully, as the Apostle says, ‘That ye may be able to 
comprehend with all saints what is the breadth and 
length and depth and height.’ That person compre- 
hends who, considering very attentively, is altogether 
persuaded that he grasps the matter, and is so per- 
suaded that he sells all his possessions and hastens to 
buy the treasure he has found; and so the Apostle 
adds ‘ with all saints.” Indeed the saints alone com- 
prehend fully, and no one comprehends those things 
as he ought without becoming holy. 

And what we have said agrees with the words of 
Saint Augustine in his letter to Honoratus. He says 
that the Apostle is portraying the Cross of Christ when 
he writes of breadth and length and height and depth; 3 
for as he himself teaches, the breadth corresponds to 
the transverse beam to which the hands of the Cruci- 
fied were fixed: the length corresponds to the long 
beam to which the Body of the Crucified was fastened; 
the height corresponds to the portion placed at the 


1 Eph. ii. 18. 7 St. Matt. xiii. 44. 3 St. Aug. &p. cxx. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE ESSENCE OF GoD 139 


head of the Cross, on which the title was; the depth 
corresponds to that part of the tree which lay hidden 
fixed in the ground. I say that Saint Augustine does 
not contradict our explanation, but rather wonderfully 
assists it, for the Cross of Christ is the way to attain 
to true breadth and length and height and depth: for 
though to the eyes of men the Cross may seem narrow 
and short and low and not very deep, yet in truth it 
spreads its arms from east to west, and from north to 
south, that is, it spread its glory far and wide by the 
preaching of the Apostles, and it raised its top to the 
highest heaven, and as a key opened it to the elect and 
penetrated even to the lowest depth of hell which it for 
ever closed to these elect. 


CHAPTER 2 


The breadth of the Being of God is the immensity 

of his perfections. 
Let us begin with the Essence and then pass on to the 
attributes. The Essence may well be called very wide 
for many reasons. First it is in itself very wide, and 
specially immense because it embraces all perfections 
of created things and also the perfections of those 
things which might be created, and numberless others: 
for whatever hath been made or will be made, or could 
be made, without doubt is contained in a more remark- 
able degree and manner in God. All other things are 
good with some qualification, such as a good man, a 
good horse, a good house, a good garment, and so of all 
the rest: but God is All-good: for when Moses said, 
* Show me thy glory,’ God answered him, ‘ I will make 

all my goodness pass before thee.’ ! 
If a man possessed a thing at home which contained 
all objects of the senses in highest perfection, so that 
he never wished to leave his house in order to see or 


t Exod. xxxiil. 18. 


140 Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


hear or smell or taste or touch elsewhere, since he had 
at home in that one thing a degree of pleasure as great 
as any man could wish for, would not that thing be 
something remarkably precious? But if in addition 
the object contained within itself such great wealth of 
all kinds of riches, as a man covetous beyond measure 
can desire, so that he had no wish to leave his house 
to gain something more, would not that thing be still 
more precious and costly ? 

And again, if that thing could offer to those who 
possessed it honours and dignities, such as the most 
ambitious of men can conceive, would it not seem to 
be beyond all price? And if that thing could fulfil 
the desire not only of men, but of angels, which is a 
matter of greater importance, since angels know more 
and better things than men, what wilt thou say? And 
yet the goodness of the thing would still be far inferior 
to the goodness of God, which is so great that it is able 
to satisfy and to fulfil the infinite longing, or rather 
the infinite capacity of God himself. 

O admirable breadth of the perfection of the Being 
of God, which embraces such immensity of good that 
it is altogether sufficient for the infinite capacity which 
isin God !_ For God never goes beyond himself, since 
he has all good within himself : and before the world 
was made he was so rich and happy and afterwards was 
so, because God made nothing which did not always 
exist in himself in a higher mode. 

Dost thou understand, O soul, what is the kind of 
good thou wilt enjoy in thy native land if thou hast 
loved God on the way thither ? And of what kind of 
good thou wilt be deprived if thou hast not loved him ? 
For God will show himself, that is, the All-good, to be 
enjoyed by those who love him, when he will say to 
the good and faithful servant, ‘ Enter thou into the joy 
of thy Lord.’ :

CHAPTER 3 


The breadth of the Divine Essence is the immensity 
of God which filleth all things. 


Next, God is immense in another way, because he 
altogether fills all the things which are in creation : ‘Do 
not I fill heaven and earth ? saith the Lord.’! And if 
there were more worlds, he would fill them all: ‘ If I 
climb up into heaven,’ saith David, ‘thou art there: 
if I go down to hell, thou art there also.’? I will add 
also this, if I go above heaven, or beneath heaven, or 
beyond heaven, I shall not be alone, for thou wilt be 
there, and I cannot exist unless I am in thee, and thou 
bearest me, thou who bearest all things along by the 
word of thy power. 3 

Not only does God fill all bodies with his immensity, 
but also spirits, hearts, minds: for how should he 
search the heart unless he was within it,4 and how 
should he hearken unto the prayer of our hearts 5 
unless he held his ear to our hearts ? And how should 
the prophet say, ‘I will hearken what the Lord God 
will say concerning me,’ °® unless God brought near his 
mouth to the ears of our heart ? : 

Happy soul who loveth God, since he keepeth his 
beloved always beside him, cherisheth and will cherish 
him in his bosom : * He that dwelleth in love dwelleth 
in God, and God in him.’7 Not only does God fill all 
things with his presence, but he also fills them with 
his glory. For the seraphim cry, “ The whole earth is 
full of his glory,’8 and David adds, *O Lord our 
Governor, how excellent is thy Name in all the world: 
thou that hast set thy glory above the heavens ’;9 as if 
he said: not only hath thy name, thy fame, and thy

CHAPTER 4 
The length of the Divine Essence is his Eternity. 


Now the length of the divine Essence is his eternity, 
which hath no beginning of duration nor will ever have 
an end, and will always abide the same without any 
change: ‘ But thou art the same and thy years shall not 
fail,’ saith David,t and so Tobit,2 and after him the 
Apostles They call God the Everlasting King, be- 
cause he alone is not subject to time, but is before all 
ages and rules all ages, since he alone ‘precedes all ages. 
Other things either have a beginning and end and 
never abide in one state, or they have a beginning and 
no ending and that without change of substance, but 
they can cease to exist, if it so please their Creator. 
And thus eternity so belongs to God alone that it is 
unsuitable to a created thing, and there was never a 
prince, however arrogant, who presumed to arrogate 
to himself amongst the titles, however many, which 
he might take, that of ‘ everlasting,’ unless perchance 
it might be with another meaning, in the way in which 
Constantius was called “ Eternal Emperor,’ because he 
was not emperor for a certain time, but for his life. 
But, my soul, thou canst be reckoned amongst both 
kinds of creatures: for thou hast a body which had a 
beginning when it was conceived and born, and little 
by little grew to that size ordained for it by God: but 
later it begins to diminish, and a little after, dying, it 
ceases to exist, and thus it never remains in the same 
state in every part at every hour: it is subject to change. 
Concerning thy body, the prophet put forward this 
opinion under the similitude of grass: ‘ They fade 


t Ps. cil. 27 2 Tobit xiii. 6. 3h ro Tims tar7. 


144 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


away suddenly like the grass. In the morning it is 
green and groweth up: but in the evening it is cut 
down, dried up, and withered.’! Jn the morning, that is 
in boyhood, the human body flourishes like the grass, 
but soon it passes on to youth: in the noontide of 
youth it flourishes and forthwith passes to old age: 
in the evening of old age it fails, it is cut down in death, 
it is withered in the grave and turns again to its dust. 
See therefore, O soul, how far thy body is from being 
eternal ! 

But thou wast created in time, when before thou 
wast nothing, and in this thou art very unlike thine 
eternal Maker. But being created, thou dost not 
perceive the end of duration, a quality which thou 
possessest in common with thy Creator: and because, 
whilst thou livest in the body, thou art changeable, 
thou passest from vice to virtue and from virtue to 
vice; and in whatever condition thou art found when 
thou departest from the body, in that thou wilt be 
judged, either to reign for ever with God, or to endure 
torments for ever with the devil. And so there is 
nothing more important for thee than to see that thou 
fleest vice and followest virtue. 

Beware, therefore, lest thou be led by the deceit of 
the flesh, to the eternal harm thereof and loss to thy- 
self: but crucify thy flesh with its affections and lusts,? 
that after a little while not only mayst thou live for 
ever happy, but thy flesh also will rise again in glory 
and will itself abide for ever united with thee in the 
eternity of God. 

Although the souls of other happy beings, as the 
holy angels, will be partakers of the eternity of God in 
that most sublime and happy union with him in the 
beatific vision and love, in a union which not only will 
have no end, but will be always stable and unmoved: 
yet in many other ways they will be able to change and 

LPs. xen.) 2 Gal. v. 24. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE Essence oF Gop 145 


vary their thoughts, their affections, and their abode: 
they will always wonder and rise up above themselves 
to the eternity of God in which there can be no change 
of mind or will or place; yet nothing will be lacking in 
this union, since it will always possess all those things 
which by various changes throughout eternity it could 
gather to itself. 

Wherefore the length of eternity is an infinite thing 
and no less appropriate to God than the breadth of 
immensity. 

CHAPTER 5 


The height of God signifies the nobility of the Divine 
Nature, which is the first and highest efficient cause of 
all things, their pattern and their end. 


Tue sublimity of God is next to be considered, accord- 
ing to which it is said to God, ‘ Thou only art the Most 
Highest.’ Now God alone is most high by the 
nobility of his nature: for things are nobler and higher 
in proportion as they are purer and more withdrawn 
from material things. We perceive this first of all in 
corporeal things: for water is more noble than earth, 
because it is purer; and for the same reason air is more 
noble than water, fire than air, and the heaven than 
fire. 

Next, we perceive it in spiritual matters: for the 
intellect is higher than sense, for sense has a bodily 
organ which the intellect does not require, and the 
angelic intelligence is higher than the human, since 
the latter needs the service of the imagination and 
fancy, which the former does not require; and among 
the angels those are the higher who by means of the 
fewer forms understand more things. 

Therefore God, who alone is pure Act and is in 
need of nothing outside himself, of no instrument or 
imagination or form and hot even the presence of any 

t Py, Ixxxiii. 18. 
L 


146 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


object outside himself, but his own Essence is all things 
to him, can have nothing which he has not always by 
his act, and he possesseth himself by act and his being 
is always a pure and simple act: and so his Nature is 
the very highest and most sublime and can in no way 
have an equal; and for this reason, that one who said, 
‘I will be like the Most High’ was suddenly over- 
thrown and fell from heaven into deepest hell, as 
Isaiah describes, and Christ the Lord saith concerning 
him, ‘I beheld Satan as lightning fall from heaven.’ ? 

There is another way in which God is most High, 
namely that he is the first and highest efficient Cause 
of all things, their Pattern and their End. He is the 
highest efficient Cause, since there is nothing created 
which has in any way a power of working which does 
not derive its strength from God, and God derives 
strength from none. Again, there is no cause which 
can exercise its strength unless it be moved by God, 
and God is moved by none: and lastly, all other higher 
causes in created things are those which are called 
universal, and are derived from these particular causes, 
as the heavens and the angels who move the heavens, 
but God made both the heavens and the angels. 
Therefore he alone is the first and highest efficient 
Cause. 

And again, he is the first exemplary Cause, for God 
made all things according to forms or ideas, which he 
holds in himself. And, lastly, he is the final Cause, be- 
cause he created all things on account of himself, that 
is, for the setting forth of his glory, as the Wise Man 
saith in Proverbs.3 But far more fittingly is it said 
and is the fact, that God is Most High because he 
sitteth on the highest throne : ‘ I saw the Lord,’ saith 
Isaiah, ‘ sitting upon a throne, high and lifted up’;4 
and since he useth two thrones, the one for judgement, 
the other for rest, let us consider each separately.

God hath the highest throne: for he is supreme King

cause he alone gives laws to all and receives laws 
from none, judges every one and is judged by none. 

Next, God is not only judge, but he is also king, 
and this not as a judge appointed by a king, but as 
king and supreme ruler ; whence he is called * King 
of kings,’5 and ‘A great king above all gods,’ ® and 
‘Wonderful among the kings of the earth ’: 7 because 
when he wills he transfers kingdoms and govern- 
ments from nation to nation, and when he wills he 
withdraws the spirit of princes. 

Again, not only is God supreme judge and king, but 
also absolute Lord, which is the highest title of all. 
For kings are not lords over their subjects in such a 
way that they can, by their own decision, despoil them 
of goods or of life: of this let King Ahab be a wit- 
ness, for he desired to possess the vineyard of Naboth 
and was unable to get it without the treachery and 
intrigue of his wife,’ and so both perished miserably. 

But God is truly and properly Lord, all things 
serve him, and he himself serves no one, and he could, 
if he willed, bring all things to naught, since he made 
all things out of nothing.

holy men: for none are more humble than those who 
are seated high in heaven, since they who are nearer 
to God see more clearly how great a distance separates 
the greatness of the Creator from the smallness of the 
creature.

love humility; imitate the Lamb without spot, imitate 
the Virgin Mother, imitate the cherubim and sera-

they are.

CHAPTER 7 


The Throne of the Most High signifies the blessed 
and beatifying rest of God. 


Nort only hath God a very high throne, because he 
judges all men, but also because he rests beyond all 
and makes those to rest in whom he has his dwelling. 
The most high throne of God is his highest rest: for 
although he rules the whole world, in which there is 
a perpetual warfare, conflicts of elements, of beasts 
and of men, yet he himself judges with equity, as is 
said in the book of Wisdom,! and he even rejoices 
in the highest peace, and there is naught able to 
disturb his repose and the contemplation of himself 
in which are his eternal delights. For this reason 
he is called King of Jerusalem, which is ‘ Vision of 
peace.’ 2 

But his proper thrones are the blessed spirits, whence 
it is said, “ Thou that sittest upon the cherubims.’ 3 
God is said to sit upon the cherubim rather than 
upon the seraphim, because the cherubim signifies the 
greatness of knowledge, seraphim rather the ardour 
of charity: repose follows wisdom, and care and 
anxiety follow charity, unless it be joined with wisdom. 
And so the soul of the righteous man is called the home 
of wisdom. 

And lastly when Isaiah says, ‘ Heaven is my throne,’ 5 
and when David says, ‘ All the whole heavens are the 
Lord’s,’® by the heavens are to be understood the 
spiritual heavens dwelling above the corporeal heavens, 
that is, the blessed spirits, as Saint Augustine explained 
in his exposition of the hundred and thirteenth Psalm. 7 

But God made these heavens to rest in such wonder-

peace in their heart where God dwelt, and therefore 
they were always joyful, always appeared calm and 
indeed were so: for the Truth itself said to them, 
‘Your heart shall rejoice and your joy no man taketh

CHAPTER 8 
Of the manifold depth in the Being of God. 
THERE remains the fourth dimension of magnitude, 
which is called depth. Now depth in respect of the

Being of God is found to be multiform: first divinity 
in itself in very deep, because it is not superficial or 
slender, but very full and very solid. The Deity 1s 
not, as it were, a gilded mass, which has gold only on 
its surface, but within is brazen or wooden, but it 1s, 
as it were, a mass wholly gold, and a great and immense 
mass, or rather a mine of gold so deep that it caa 
never be exhausted by digging; thus God 1s altogether 
incomprehensible. Just as a mine of gold, whose 
depth cannot be reached, is never exhausted by dig- 
ging, so also God, of ‘ whose greatness there is no end,’ * 
is never so perfectly known by a created mind but 
that it always seeks to know more and more, and God 
alone comprehends the infinite depths, for he alone has 
an infinite power of understanding. 

Next, God is deep by reason of place: for as he is 
Most High because he rules over all things and is 
above all, so also he is most deep because he is beneath 
all things, that he may uphold them, and he is within 
all things, that he may bear them: for he himself, as 
the Apostle says, “ Upholdeth all things by the word 
of his power.’ And so God is, as it were, the founda- 
tion and the roof of the building, ‘in whom we live 
and move and have our being ’:3 and therefore very 
rightly did Solomon say, ‘ The heaven and heaven of 
heavens cannot contain thee,’4 or rather God indeed 
contains the heavens and those things that are under 
the heavens, since he is above the heavens and beneath 
the earth. 

Secondly, the depth of God is his invisibility. For 
God is Light but Light unapproachable:5 he is 
Truth, but exceeding profound, and more interior than 
any interior thing : “He maketh darkness his secret 
place,’ saith David; ® and as Isaiah says, ‘ Verily thou 
art a God that hidest thyself.’7 Saint Augustine, at

CONSIDERATION OF THE GREATNESS 
OF THE POWER OF GOD BY COMPARISON 
WITH THE MAGNITUDE OF BODIES 


CHAPTER I 
Concerning the breadth of the power of God. 
Gees is the Lord and of his greatness there is 


no measure or end: not alone is he great because 
his height is omnipotence, his depth wisdom un- 
searchable, his breadth mercy shown forth in every 
place, his length righteousness like unto a rod of iron: 
but also because each of these separate attributes 1s 
great with the magnitude of infinite breadth, length, 
depth, and height. 

And as by a power, or rather by omnipotence, we are 
kept in place: the power of God has its own breadth 
which exists in him, so that he reaches forth to things 
which have no limit. 

For first, he reaches forth to all things that are made: 
for there is nothing in this whole universe of things 
from the first angel to the last little worm, and from 
the highest heaven to the lowest hell, which was not 
made by the power of God: ‘ All things,’ saith Saint 
John, ‘ were made by him, and without him nothing 
was made that is made’; and further on, ‘ The world 
was made by him.’ ! 

Secondly, he reaches to all things which throughout 
eternity shall ever be. For as nothing could be made 
except by him, so also nothing will ever be made 

1 St. John i. 3, 10. 
155 


156 THe Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


except by him: for thus saith the Apostle, “ Of him, 
and through him, and to him are all things.’ ! 

Thirdly, he reaches to all things which might be, 
though they should never exist: for thus saith the 
angel, ‘ With God nothing shall be impossible’: and 
the Lord himself said, ‘ With God all things are pos- 
sible.’ 3 

Fourthly, he reaches out to the complete destruction 
of things that are made. For as God was able to 
destroy by the flood of waters all the men and all the 
living creatures which were upon the earth, except 
the few whom he willed to preserve in the ark of Noah,‘ 
so will he be able by a flood of fire in a moment to 
destroy not only all men and all living creatures which 
will be found living at the last day, but also all trees and 
all cities and everything else that is in the earth. The 
Apostle Peter says in his second Epistle, ‘ The day of 
the Lord will come asa thief in the night; in the which 
the heavens shall pass away with a great noise, and the 
elements shall melt with fervent heat, the earth also 
and the works that are therein shall be burned up.’ 5 

Great indeed is this breadth of the power of God, 
which man cannot sufficiently admire unless he be 
able to enumerate the multitude of things which God 
hath made, will make, or can make. 

But who can number this multitude save he whose 
knowledge is infinite? And again, the greatness of 
his power increases exceedingly. Let any one con- 
sider how great it must be in order to dissolve almost 
in one moment, with the greatest ease, what has taken 
so much power and such long ages to form: or as Judas 
Maccabeus says, ‘ Who at a beck can cast down both 
them that come against us, and also all the world.’ © 

Let us say therefore with Moses, ‘ Who is like unto 
thee, O Lord, among the mighty ones ?’” 7

CHAPTER 2 
Of the length of the power of God. 


Now the length of the divine power is perceived in 
this, that God himself works continuously with all that 
he has made, and he will never be weary: indeed that 
power of God cannot be lessened or weakened or 
broken in any way, since it is united with true eternity, 
or rather it is itself the eternity of true divinity. 

Many persons marvel how the sun and moon and 
stars can be moved in such regularity and such swift- 
ness from rising to setting, and without intermission 
turn in their own orbits; and indeed the matter would 
be most worthy of all admiration, did we not know 
that they are borne along by the Almighty God, who 
‘upholds all things by the word of his power.’ * 

And others wonder because God bears and sustains 
all things and is not wearied by bearing and sustaining 
such an immense mass of wellnigh infinite weight : 
for a strong man, or a horse, or an ox, or an elephant 
can bear a great weight for a short time, or carry a 
heavy burden for a short time: but to bear an immense 
weight always without fatigue is beyond the strength 
of all created beings. These persons would rightly 
marvel if God had strength in such weight and meas- 
ure as all created things possess it: but since the 
strength of God completely surpasses all measures, and 
since he himself is unlimited in every respect, it is 
not wonderful :f his infinite strength upholds a mass, 
however weighty it be, for an unlimited time and with- 
out weariness. 

Therefore again let us say with the holy prophet 
Moses, ‘ Who is like unto thee, O Lord, among the 
mighty ones ?’ 2

CHAPTER 3 


Of the height of the power of God. 
Tuer: follows the height of the power of God, which 


consists chiefly in two things. 

First, omnipotence may be called very high, because 
it alone makes exceeding high things. In the first 
creation of things God alone made those things which 
are beneath the moon, but they have the power, through 
the action of other created things, to be generated, 
transformed, corrupted: for the elements mutually 
work changes one on another, according to their func- 
tions: thus from the earth herbs and trees are brought 
forth : animals are produced from animals: fishes are 
born in water, clouds and rain in the air, comets in 
fire. But the heaven and the stars, which are the 
highest bodies, God alone created, alone he preserves 
them, and no created being can’ possess the power of 
forming or transforming, of dissolving or even pre- 
serving, any. “I will consider,’ saith the prophet, ‘ thy 
heavens, even the works of thy fingers: the moon and 
the stars which thou hast ordained’:! for the Most 
High reserved to himself the highest works, he him- 
self began to build for the foundation, and he himself 
brought them to full completion. 

So also spiritual things, angels and the souls of men, 
which are the most noble and sublime of all works, 
the Most High alone, by his infinite power, formed and 
preserves and will preserve eternally from destruction: 
and in these actions created beings have no share: and 
although they were all to join in one act, they could 
never produce or destroy one single angel or one 
single soul. 

Next, the height of the divine power is seen very 
clearly in miracles which, as Saint Augustine teaches, 


A Paes 


CoNSIDERATION OF THE Power oF Gop 159 


are works which are wrought apart from the usual 
cause of nature, and which are the admiration and 
wonder of nature, even of the angels themselves.t 
Which of the angels was not astonished when, at the 
command of Joshua, the sun and moon, always being 
borne along in a very swift course, stood immovable ?2 
And lest it should be thought that this happened by 
accident (and no one could imagine that such an un- 
usual thing could be done by any mortal man in the 
world), the Holy Spirit speaks and says, * The Lord 
hearkened to the voice of man.’3_ For Joshua did not, 
strickly speaking, address the sun itself or the moon, 
for he knew they could not hear his command, but he 
prayed the Lord, as if he had said by the command 
of the Lord, ‘ Sun, stand thou still upon Gibeon, and 
thou moon in the valley of Ajalon.’ * And the Lord 
hearkened to the voice of a man, that is, he caused 
those luminaries to obey the voice of a man. For 
often in sacred speech the Lord is said to do things, 
by reason of which they happen: as in Genesis the 
Lord says to Abraham, “ Now I know that thou fearest 
God.’ 4 The meaning of these words is, ‘ Now I have 
caused it to be known by thee and by others that thou 
truly fearest God.’ 

Such also was the work which manifested the height 
of divine power when, in the Lord’s Passion, the moon, 
which was at that time a long distance from the sun, 
approached it by a very swift motion, and being be- 
neath it, caused darkness over the earth for three hours, 
and when those three hours were passed, returned with 
a marvellous rapidity to that place whence it had 
moved: Saint Dionysius the Areopagite testifies in his 
Epistle to Saint Polycarp that he saw and observed all 
these things. 

And this is indeed a contrary sign to the former,

4 Gen. xxii. 12. 


160 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


but not less to be wondered at, since it is an equally 
new and unusual thing and one surpassing the power 
of all nature, to cause the moon to stand still or to 
make it move out of its usual course. 

I omit the enlightening of the blind, the raising of 
the dead, and many other miracles of that kind which 
God hath done and still does by his prophets and 
apostles and other of his faithful servants: these all 
cry aloud, ‘ Who is like unto thee, O Lord, among the 
mighty ones ?’ 

But I cannot omit that highest and greatest miracle 
which God will show in the Last Day when all the 
dead will rise at once, when many bodies having been 
reduced to ashes and dispersed, having been either 
consumed by beasts and converted into other things 
and other bodies, or having been buried in gardens 
and fields, have been transformed and passed into 
herbs of various kinds, shall then rise. 

What angel will not be astounded when he sees in 
the twinkling of an eye so many thousands of thou- 
sands of men, at the command of the Almighty, take 
again their bodies, though through many ages and in 
divers ways these have been dispersed or consumed and 
hidden away. This is therefore the height of the power 
of God, and by reason of it again we may say, ‘ Who 
is like unto thee, O Lord, among the mighty ones ?’ 


CHAPTER 4 
Of the depth of the power of God. 


THERE remains the depth, which seems to me to con- 
sist in the method which God uses in working: for 
who can penetrate the manner in which something is 
made out of nothing? To this depth it was not 
possible for those to turn their eyes, who declared that 
the beginning was explored and certain, that nothing 
was made of nothing. And we also in this matter 


CONSIDERATION OF THE Power oF Gop 161 


believe what we have not seen: ! but we believe a true 
God who cannot lie.2 We believe, I say, that the 
heaven and the earth and the things in them were 
made by God himself, while nothing had been in exis- 
tence before, from which they could be made. But 
how a thing could be made when nothing went before 
it, is a profound abyss which we cannot scrutinize or 
investigate. 

Next, not only did God make everything out of 
nothing, but he made them in nothing, that is without 
space or place existing before, in which what was made 
could be placed, a thing which can scarcely be under- 
stood, especially in regard to corporeal things. Hence 
this abyss is also impenetrable. Take away the spaces 
of places from bodies, says Saint Augustine in his 
letter to Dardanus, and they will be nowhere: and 
since they will be nowhere, they will not exist. 3 

Now then, if before God created heaven and earth 
there was nothing, where did God place heaven and 
earth? They certainly could not be placed within 
nothing, and yet they were created, and those very 
things are to themselves places, because he who can 
do all things willed it, even if we are unable to under- 
stand in what way those things happened. God him- 
self had regard to this, when desiring to show his 
Almighty power to Job he said: ‘ Where wast thou 
when I laid the foundations of the earth? Declare if 
thou hast understanding. Who hath laid the measures 
thereof if thou knowest ? Or who hath stretched the 
line upon it ? Whereupon are the foundations thereof 
fastened ? Or who hath laid the corner stone there- 
of ?’4 

And that we may understand that these works 
of the Almighty power of God are most worthy of 
all praise, immediately the Lord adds, ‘ When the

3 St. Aug. Ep. clxxxvii, c. 6, no. 18, 4 Job xxxvili. 4.

morning stars sang together, and all the sons of God 
shouted for joy.’ ! 

Doubtless, the holy angels who were created at the 
same time as the heaven and the earth and who are, as 
it were, most brilliant spiritual stars, and may also be 
called sons of God, when they saw heaven and earth 
produced from nothing and placed in nothing and yet 
most firmly grounded on their own stability, did indeed 
with immense astonishment and delight praise the 
omnipotence of the Creator. 

And no less is it a depth for the understanding to 
penetrate, that God by the sole exercise of his will 
should raise such immense masses, for we know that, 
in buildings incomparably smaller, how many tools, 
what machinery, and how many assistants a builder 
needs. 

Who can follow, even in thought, how it can happen 
that by a single interior will, which goes not forth 
from him who wills, such immense and various works 
are produced ? 

God said, certainly in regard to himself, for the 
Word of God is in God and is God himself 2—he said, 
I say, that the sovereignty of his will is the same in 
commanding and in carrying out the command: let 
the heaven be made, and it was made: let the earth be 
made, and it was made: let there be light, let there 
be sun, stars, trees, living creatures, man, angels, and 
all things were made. Add to this that God could, if 
he so willed, also destroy all these things and the whole 
world with one look, as we read in the books of the 
Maccabees.3 But this depth is to be added, that God 
made all these things, so many and so great, and things 
composed of so many members or parts, in one mo- 
ment. With us both art and nature need a long space 
of time in order that the works may be accomplished: 
we see herbs sown long before they spring up, trees 

© Job xxxviil. 7. 2 St. John i. 1. 3 2 Macc. viii. 18. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE PowER oF Gop 163 


often requiring many years to take root and spread 
their branches and bring forth fruit: animals bear 
their young for a long time and also nourish them 
for a long time before they grow up: I say nothing 
about art, since it is well known that our workmen 
accomplish nothing without taking time and that often 
a long time. 

How great then is the power of God pict swifter 
than a word, accomplishes the greatest works ? I do 
not discuss the question whether God finished the 
heaven and earth and all things in them in one moment, 
or whether he took the whole six days in the first 
creation of things; for I did not undertake to explain 
problems, but to make means of ascent to God by the : 
consideration of things. The fact that I adduce and 
at which I marvel is, that each separate thing was made 
perfect in a moment by the Almighty Creator. And 
in the case of earth, water, air, and fire, no one doubts 
that they were formed at once and in a moment, 
and also that all the angels were made at the same 
moment. 

Concerning the firmament and the separation of the 
waters, it is known that all that was done by the sole 
power of the Word who said, * Let there be a firma- 
ment in the midst of the waters’; and that happened 
in a moment, for it follows ‘ and it was so.’ On which 
passage Saint John Chrysostom says: ‘ He spake only, 
and the work followed: so again he was the Maker, 
according to those words, “‘ Let the earth bring forth 
grass and it was so.”’’ Who when he thinks on this will 
not marvel how the word of the Lord, ‘ Let the earth 
bring forth,’ adorns the face of the earth with a variety 
of flowers as with a wonderful robe ? Thou seest the 
earth, which before was shapeless and uncultivated, all 
at once as if contending with the sky in regard to its 
glory and adornment. And afterwards on those words

CHAPTER 5 


The soul 1s admonished to fear God and keep his 


commandments. 


AND now, my soul, thou understandest clearly how 
great is the power of thy Maker, which stretches to all 
things by its breadth; by its length endures for ever, 
and bears all things and rules them without being 
weary; by its height goes so far as to accomplish 
what seems to be impossible and what is indeed 1m- 
possible to every other being, itself excepted; by its 
depth does those things in such a manner that the 
method of working surpasses all created intelligence, 
since from nothing and in nothing, and without instru- 
ments and without time, it accomplished them by 
word and command alone: ‘He spake the word 
and they were made: he commanded and they were 
created.’ 2 

If thou art wise thou canst gather from these facts 
how much it matters whether thou hast him for an 
enemy or for a friend, as angered or as pleased: for if

thou hast him as an enemy or angered, he is able 
suddenly to despoil thee of all thy goods and to fill

deliver thee out of his hands, for who will dare to con- 
tend with the Almighty? If alone and naked thou 
meetest an implacable enemy, seeking thee with a 
sharp sword, what wilt thou do but fall into sweat 
and become pale and trembling, and louting on thy 
knees beg his pity! And yet he is but a man, and 
perchance thou wilt be able to free thyself from death, 
by flight or by fighting, and snatching the sword from 
his hands. But with an angry God what wilt thou do, 
from him thou canst not flee, since he is everywhere, 
and thou canst not resist him, for he is omnipotent, 
and thou canst not presume on his delay, since he acts 
in a moment, and on his sole authority. With reason 
did the Apostle say, ‘ It is a fearful thing to fall into} 
the hands of the living God.’! But if, on the other 
hand, thou hast God well pleased with thee, and as 
thy friend, who happier than thou? For he is able 
to do what he wills, and if he is thy friend, to fill 
thee with all good things and to deliver thee from all 
evils. | 

But so long as thou art in this life, it lies in thine own 
hands to have God as an angry enemy or a living friend. 
For God by the prophets, and then by his Son, and 
by his Apostles in the Holy Scriptures is continually 
calling, inviting sinners to penitence and the righteous 
to keep his commandments, that thus he may have 
both for his friends, or rather as very dear children and 
heirs of his eternal kingdom. MHear Ezekiel: ‘As | 
live, saith the Lord God, I have no pleasure in the

will not be possible with all the riches of the world

TWELFTH STEP 


CONSIDERATION OF THE GREATNESS OF 
THE WISDOM OF GOD BY THE LIKENESS 
OF CORPOREAL MAGNITUDE 


CHAPTER I 


The breadth of the Wisdom of God consists in the 
perfect knowledge of all things. 


Ho truly the Apostle wrote at the end of his 

Epistle to the Romans, that God is alone wise, _ 
we may easily understand, if we will consider carefully 
the breadth, the length, the height, and depth of the 
wisdom of God. 

And to begin with the breadth. The wisdom of 
God is described as very wide, because God knows 
distinctly and perfectly all things that are in nature, 
from the first angel to the last worm; not only does he 
know their whole substance, but also their parts, pro- 
perties, powers, qualities, actions. Hence those words, 
‘Thou numberest my steps,’? and, ‘ The ways of man 
are before the eyes of the Lord, and he pondereth all 
his goings.’3 And if he numbers and considers each 
step, how much more the actions of the mind, good or 
bad. And if God reckoneth the hairs of our head, 
according to the Word of the Lord, ‘ The hairs of 
your head are all numbered,’4 how much more doth 
he know all the members of the body and all the 
powers of the soul. And if he knows the number of 


t Rom. xvi. 27. 2 Job xiv. 16. 3 Prov. v. 21. 
4 St. Matt. x. 30. 


168 


CONSIDERATION OF THE WispoM oF Gop 169 


the sands of the sea and the drops of rain, as we 
gather from the Wise Man,t how much more may 
we believe that he knows the number of the stars and 
of the angels. And if all the idle words of all men are 
to be brought into judgement, as the Lord himself 
declares,? certainly his ears will at once hear all the 
voices of all men, and not only the voices of the body, 
but also of the mind, that is, the thoughts and desires. 
How great then, and how boundless, is this breadth 
of wisdom which comprehends at once all things that 
are, that have been, that will be, and that can be. 

And this divine mind is not worn out by such a 
variety of separate and inferior things, as the foolish 
wisdom of some philosophers would have it. Per- 
chance we might imagine this, if God had to obtain 
knowledge from things as we do: but since he sees 
all things in his own being, there is no danger of wear- 
ing out: although the acquiring of knowledge by 
seeking after it, which is the method of mankind, is 
far more noble than the entire lack of knowledge which 
is the portion of brutes, in the same way as it is more 
excellent to be blind, in the way animals are, than not 
being blind, to be unable to see, as stones: and other 
members of the body are not more worthy of honour 
than the eyes because they can not be blind, but the 
eyes although they can be blind, are more worthy 
of honour since they have the faculty of seeing, as 
Saint Augustine rightly argues in his books on the 
City of God. 3 

Hence, my soul, thou oughtest at every time and 
in every place to be careful what thou doest, what thou 
sayest, what thou thinkest, since thou dost never act, 
speak, or think without God seeing, hearing, observ- 
ing thee: for if thou wouldst not dare to do or say 
anything evil though urged thereto by the strongest 
desire, if thou thoughtest that thou wert heard or seen

170 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


by man, how darest thou think those things with an 
angry God looking upon thee P 

Be it so,’ saith Saint Augustine in his rule for 
nuns, ‘ that what you do is seen by no man, it is beheld 
by him who looketh from above, and from him nothing 
can be hid.’ ! 

And Saint Basil, in his book on virginity, addressing 
a virgin shut up alone in her cell, warns her to rever- 
ence her Bridegroom present everywhere, and his 
Father and the Holy Spirit and the countless multi- 
tudes of angels, and with them the holy spirits of the 
Fathers : ‘ For there is no one of them,’ he says, ‘ who 
does not see all things in every place.’ 2 

O thou blessed soul of mine, if thou considerest 
always that thou art continually in view of this assem- 
bly, even in the darkness and silence of midnight : 
what a perfect life wilt thou spend, how carefully wilt 
thou avoid all levity and wandering abroad. 

This is indeed the word which the Lord spake once 
to the patriarch Abraham, ‘ Walk before me and be 
thou perfect’;3 that is, bethink thee that thou art 
always seen by me, and without any doubt thou wilt 
be perfect. 


CHAPTER 2 


The length of the Wisdom of God is displayed in the 
knowledge of things to come. 


Tue length of wisdom appears in knowledge of future 
events: for God so clearly sees, that from eternity 
itself he saw what will happen in the last days and 
beyond, even in eternity, and indeed nothing greater 
than this length can be thought of. 

‘ Thou understandest my thoughts long before,’ said 
David in the Psalms ; and a little after, ‘ There is not

a word in my tongue but thou, O Lord, knowest it 
altogether ’;! that is, all things future, and all things

and truest predictions, which were not spoken by 
themselves, but, as sings Zacharias, ‘God spake by

And this foresight and prediction is proper to God

distinctly describes everything in detail as if he were 
telling, not of future, but of past, events. 

I omit numberless other prophecies, of which, as I 
said, the books of the prophets are full. 

Next are the astrologers and certain other diviners, 
who as though desirous of being imitators of God, are 
altogether ridiculous: for it cannot be that they pre- 
dict the truth concerning future events, unless per- 
chance now and again they happen to get near the 
truth. For, since in all causes and happenings the will 
of God presides and rules, and can hinder the lesser 
causes when it wills, no one can foretell the truth unless 
God hath willed to manifest his will to him, as he often 
manifested it to his prophets. 

And so this is true that demons desired to be con- 
sidered gods specially because they established oracles 
and foretold future events, as Saint Augustine testifies 
in his books on the City of God. And the same cele- 
brated doctor of the Church in his book on the divining 
of demons openly shows their divination to be false, 
as their divinity is false: for they declare nothing 
plainly but that which they themselves are about to 
do; or what has already taken place, they by their 
natural swiftness represent to those at a distance as 
about to take place; or again, that which from daily 
experience they consider will take place, as mariners 
foretell many things of the winds, husbandmen of the 
weather, physicians of diseases; and when the devils 
are asked about things to come which they know not, 
they make answer in ambiguous words or in equivo- 
cation, and when those things prove false, they lay the 
fault on their interpreters or soothsayers.t 

Therefore our Lord only, ‘ Whose wisdom is infi- 
nite,’ founded true oracles, and foretelleth the truth 
in all things which shall come to pass both by happen- 
ings and by choice.

CHAPTER 3 
Of the manifold height of the Wisdom of God. 


Gop’s wisdom is most high and far surpasses the 
wisdom of men or of angels. The height of wisdom 
is discerned by nobility of the object, of its power, of 
its form, and of its action. 

The object of divine wisdom both natural and pro- 
portionate is the divine Essence itself which is so high 
that it has no proportion to any understanding either 
angelic or human: and angels of the highest order 
cannot ascend to see God unless they are lifted up by 
the light of glory: and this is the reason why in holy 
writ God is said to be invisible: ‘ To the King eternal, 
immortal, invisible, the only God,’ saith the Apostle 
in his former Epistle to Timothy, and he afterwards 
adds: that God dwelleth in light that cannot be 
approached. 

Then secondly, power, which in us is an accident, 
is in God a divine substance, and therefore exists in 
him in a much higher and nobler degree than in us. 
The form also is the higher the more it doth represent: 
and therefore those angels that have fewer and more 
universal forms are said to have so much the higher 
knowledge. 

How great then is the height of divine wisdom, 
which has no form but its own Essence, which is but 
one, and is sufficient of itself alone for God to represent 
and to know himself and all things which are made, 
shall be made, or may be made. 

And in the last place, that wisdom is accounted most 
sublime which knoweth most by fewest acts: but God’ 
by one single act of intuition perfectly knoweth himself 
and all other things. 

The wisdom of God therefore hath the pre-eminence 
and ought to be called most high. 

t y Tim. i. 17. 2 ibid., vi. 16.

Lift up thine eyes now, O my soul, and see how 
vast a distance there is between thy knowledge and that 
of thy Maker. For thou by many acts in running 
hither and thither canst hardly know any one thing 
perfectly, but thy Maker doth by one only act most 
clearly and distinctly see himself and all others. And 
yet thou thyself, now lying in darkness, mayst, if thou 
wilt, ascend so high upon the wings of faith and love 
that after laying down this mortal body, being changed 
from glory to glory, thou mayst, in the light of God, 
see God who is light.1_ And being make like unto God, 
thou also at one beholding and that lasting for ever,

creatures in God. ‘ For what is there that he doth not 
see,’ saith Saint Gregory, ‘who seeth him that seeth 
all things ? How great then shall be that pleasure,

admitted to that inaccessible light and art a partaker

thy men, happy are these thy servants, which stand con-

mon and the wisdom of God, who is the only wise, 
yea Wisdom itself? And what is the order of his 
servants compared with the nine orders of angels of 
God of whom ‘ Thousand thousands ministered unto 
him and ten thousand times ten thousand stood before 
him’ ?4 

Surely if thou couldst taste of these things even in 
a small measure, thou wouldst most willingly do or 
suffer anything that thou mightest obtain the favour

CHAPTER 4 


The depth of the Wisdom of God is manifest in the know- 
ledge of our thoughts and especially of what is to come. 


Ir remains for us to consider the depth of the divine 
wisdom, which consists chiefly in the discerning of the 
reins and the heart, that is, in the knowledge of men’s 
thoughts and desires, especially those which are to 
come. For we read, ‘ Man looketh on the outward 
appearance, but God looketh upon the heart’; 3 ‘ Thou 
alone knowest the hearts of the children of men’; 4 
‘Thou understandest my thoughts ’;5 ‘He knoweth 
the hidden things of the heart’;® and ‘ The heart is 
deceitful above all things and desperately wicked,’ 
where the Septuagint translates, ‘the heart of man is 
deep and unscrutable,’ and Saint Jerome expounding 
this passage, rightly shows that Christ is truly God be- 
cause he saw the thoughts of men, which God alone can 
see;7 ‘ And Jesus knowing their thoughts said, Where- 
fore think ye evil in your hearts ?’;8 ‘ But he knew 
their thoughts ’;9 “ Why reason ye these things in your 
hearts ?’ 1° ‘Therefore every thought and desire of men, 
although present and really existing, is so deep that 
neither angels nor demons nor men can search or

penetrate into it, but far deeper is the thought or desire 
of things to come: for not only are men and angels 
not able to penetrate it, but they cannot penetrate the 
way by which God, who alone knows it, cometh to the 
knowledge thereof. And this David seems to wish to 
signify when he says, ‘ Such knowledge is too wonder- 
ful and excellent for me.’! For that ex me signifies 
in Hebrew phrase, “ before me’ or ‘over me,’ and the 
sense is ‘ Thy knowledge is more wonderful than I 
am able to understand how it exists.” For he adds, 
‘It is high and great and I cannot attain unto it,’ that 
is, it is raised above my knowledge, and | cannot by 
any reasoning reach up to understand it. But he 
speaks of the knowledge of future thoughts: for he 
had said, “ Thou knowest my thoughts long before’: 
of the foreknowledge of these thoughts and ways he 
says, ‘I cannot attain unto it.’ It may perchance be 
answered that God sees those future thoughts in his 
eternity, to which all things are present, or in the 
predetermination of his will: but if it were so, such 
knowledge would not be wonderful, for we ourselves 
are easily able to know what we are about to do, or 
what things are before our eyes. But the Scripture 
saith that God searcheth the reins and the heart and 
that there he sees what man desires and thinks or what 
afterwards he will desire and think. But this is ex- 
ceeding wonderful, that God by searching the hearts 
and reins sees what is not yet in being there, and what 
depends on the choice of the will whether it ever shall 
be. Therefore as it pertains to the depth of the power 
of God to make something out of nothing, and to call 
those things that are not, but shall be, as the things 
which are, so it belongs to the depth of the divine 
wisdom that by searching out the reins and the heart, 
to see what is not yet there and as if it already were, 
and which doubtless will exist.

CHAPTER 5 


Elevation of the soul to the divine Physician who 
searctheth the heart and healeth it. 


But since I undertook not to dispute questions but to 
stir the soul to rise up to God, rouse up thyself, my 
soul, and mount above thyself, as Jeremiah advises, 
* Let us lift up our heart unto God in the heavens,’ ? 
and consider that profound abyss of the divine wisdom 
which searches the depth of the heart and sees many 
things which the heart cannot see. O blessed Peter, 
when thou didst say to thy Lord, ‘ Though I should die 
with thee, yet will I not deny thee,’ surely thou didst 
not speak with a double heart, but with sincerity and 
uprightness, nor didst thou see the infirmity of thy heart 
which thy Lord saw in it when he said, ‘ Before the 
cock crow twice, thou shalt deny me thrice’:3 for 
that most skilful Physician saw the weakness in thine 
heart which thou sawest not, and that was true, and 
the Physician foretold not what the sick man boasted 
of. But give thanks to the Physician who, as he fore- 
saw and foretold the disease, breathed into thy soul 
from above the powerful medicine of repentance and 
quickly cured it. 

O good, O kind, O most wise and powerful Physi- 
cian, cleanse me from my secret faults. How many 
are there which I do not bewail nor wash away with 
tears because I do not see them. Let thy grace be | 
ever present with me, by which thou searchest the 
reins and the hearts, and do thou discover to me the 
evil desires and evil deeds which I do not see, but which 
thou seest, and looking mercifully upon me, open such 
a fountain of tears that while there is time, they may 
be cleansed and washed away by thy grace. Amen.

THIRTEENTH STEP 


CONSIDERATION OF GOD'S PRACTICAL 
WISDOM 


CHAPTER I 


The breadth of God’s practical wisdom is clear from the 
creation of things and their disposal in number, measure, 
and weight. 


E have considered the wisdom of God as it is in 
theory: his practical wisdom 1s now to be con- 
sidered, which we may also call his effective wisdom. 
This wisdom has its breadth, length, height, and depth: 
the breadth is known by creation, the length by the 
preservation of things created, the height by the work | 
of redemption, the depth by providence and predes- 
tination. 

And to begin with creation. ‘God hath made all 
things in wisdom,’ as it is said in the Psalms," and 
poured it forth over all his works, as the Wise Man 
writes.2_ As we therefore know the power of the worker 
in the creation of all things out of nothing, so by the 
wonderful workmanship which we discern in every one 
of them do we admire the wisdom of the Creator: for 
‘ He hath disposed all things in measure, number, and 
weight.’3 With this savour God hath seasoned all 
things and by this savour we come to understand 
how delightful is his wisdom, how amiable and how 
desirable. 

All created things, then, have a certain number, a 


Pole. err 2e. 2 Ecclus. i. 10. 3 Wisd. xi. 20. 
178 


CONSIDERATION OF Gop’s Practica WisDoM 179 


certain measure, a certain weight both to distinguish 
them from God, who hath no measure because he is 
immense, nor number, because he is One and simple 
in his Essence; nor weight, because his value and his 
price surpass all price and value: and also since 
they are all good and beautiful, as Moses most truly 
said, “God saw everything that he had made, and 
behold it was very good.’* All created things, then, 
have the measure which is necessary to attain the end 
for which they were made: nor can anything be added 
to or taken from that measure without making the 
thing deformed and useless, and therefore not so good 
as it was: ‘God hath made everything beautiful in his 
season.’ ‘Therefore to the heavens God gave a very 
spacious measure, since they have to embrace all things 
beneath them; to the air, far shorter measure than the 
heaven, but greater than to the earth and waters which 
form one globe and are enclosed on all sides by the air. 
He gave to the elephant a body of huge bulk that it 
might carry very heavy weights and even a tower full 
of men: to the horse less, because it was made to 
carry only one rider; he made birds small that they 
might build their nests on branches of trees; bees and 
ants very small, that they might hide themselves in the 
holes either of hives or in the earth. 

The same thing we may say of number. God made 
one sun, since one was enough to lighten the whole 
earth and to make day by his brightness: he also made 
one moon, since one is enough to give light in the 
night: he willed that there should be many stars, 
that in the absence of sun and moon, as happens at 
their conjunction, they might in some measure dis- 
perse the darkness of night. And not only to all 
things in general did he assign the fitting number, 
but also established the number of parts in each 
separate thing, that no addition or diminution can be 

t Gen. 1. 31. 2 Eccles. ili. rr. 


180 Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


made. God hath given man two eyes, two ears, two 
hands, two feet, one nose, one mouth, one breast, one 
head, and thus he appears a very beautiful and comely 
creature: invert the order, put on any man one eye, 
two noses, one ear, two mouths, one hand, and one 
foot, two hearts and two heads, and nothing more 
useless or more hideous could be made. 

Lastly, God hath given to each thing weight, that 
is, he has given to each thing the value that its nature 
requires. By the word weight or price we understand 
the qualities which make things good and precious, and 
there are three things necessary to perfection: number, 
that nothing be wanting;. secondly a proper proportion 
of parts; and lastly, internal and external qualities, as 
loveliness of colour in the outward surface of the body, 
and inward power which is useful or necessary for our 
various actions. But indeed it is wonderful to con- 
sider what virtue God gave to each thing, however 
tiny and small, so that it would seem he willed to show 
his power in great things, and in small his wisdom. 
Who can conceive what virtue there is in a grain of 
mustard seed, which is so very small that it can scarce 
be discerned by the eye and yet it produces a large 
tree, so that even birds lodge in its branches, as he who 
is truth itself says in the Gospel.t And this is not 
peculiar to mustard seed, but is common to all seeds 
in the virtue of which lie hid roots, trunks, branches, 
leaves, blossoms, and fruit of the greatest trees. Indeed 
if we had not learnt this by experience we should not 
easily persuade men that from so small a seed so many 
and such different things could ever spring. And who 
could conceive that in an ant, a fly, a flea, and such very 
tiny creatures, there should be nimble feet, a head, a 
heart, exterior and interior senses, and that in their own 
way, although as yet imperfect, they possess wisdom 
and judgement ? And lastly, who would suppose that 


x St. Matt. xiii. 31. 


CONSIDERATION OF Gop’s Practica, WIspDoM' I8I 


in these and other very small creatures there should be 
power to pierce and enter the living flesh, so that not 
only do they become most troublesome to men, but 
also to elephants and lions which they terrify ? 

Great, therefore, is the Lord, and great is his wisdom 
as well in the smallest as in the greatest things. Galen, 
that prince of physicians, though a heathen, admired 
the divine artifice when he perceived it in the human 
hand, and cried out in praise of the Creator. What 
then, O Christian, oughtest thou to do, who art assured 
that not only the bodies of men and other creatures but 
also the heavens, the stars, the angels, and the immortal 
spirits were made with incredible wisdom by the same 
most wise Creator ? 


CHAPTER 2 


The length of the practical wisdom of God manifest 
in the preservation of things. 


Now the length of the practical wisdom of God appears 
in the preservation of things; as we have already said, 
its breadth is shown in their creation. Therefore the 
great and admirable wisdom of God is seen in his 
preservation of created things and giving them power 
to continue, and especially such as are of a corruptible 
nature. And first, if a man considers how God nour- 
ishes herbs, plants, living creatures, and the very 
bodies of men, and makes them thrive and grow so that 
they are preserved for a long time, he cannot sufficiently 
wonder at the wisdom of God. For with earth and 
water he nourishes plants and herbs and causes that 
the nourishment passes from the root to the trunk, 
and from the trunk to the branches and fruits by a 
certain force drawing it upwards and making it run 
into every part in a wonderful order and measure. 
Men likewise, and some beasts, he nourishes by herbs 
and fruits, and so orders that the nourishment enters 


drugs that they may be taken by the sick man not only 
easily but with pleasure, for meats are certainly physic, 
which unless mortal men frequently take, they cannot 
escape death. But God, our most loving and wise 
physician, first gives a savour to the food, that it may 
be eaten with pleasure; next he varies it in numberless 
ways so as to remove distaste; and lastly by various 
changes in the mouth, stomach, liver, and heart the 
meat is changed into a juice, so thin and subtile that, 
without cutting out a way and giving pain, it passes 
through all the arteries and veins and pores of the body 
and into all parts of the flesh, bones, and nerves when 
we do not perceive it, and even when we are asleep. 
Philosophers wonder at this skill and art of nature when 
they examine it, but what cunning can there be in 
inanimate things void of sense and skill in reasoning ? 
It is therefore not natural skill but the wisdom of the 
Creator that is to be admired, for he not only made 
nature, but found out a way by which these wonders 
might be done. Hear the wisdom of God speaking 
in the Gospel : ‘ Consider the lilies of the field, how 
they grow, they toil not, neither do they spin. . . and 
God clothes them.’! It is therefore not natural skill, 
but God, who makes the lilies grow, and clothes them 
with beauty as with a garment; and the same may be 
said of the nourishment and increase of all things 
living, as the Apostle bears witness, when he says, 
‘ Neither is he that planteth anything, neither he that 
watereth ; but God that giveth the increase.’ 2 

And if the wisdom of God feeds, nourishes, and 
preserves in mortal life plants and animals in so won- 
derful a way, think, if thou canst, O my soul, how God 
doth feed the minds of angels and of men in life which 

r St. Matt. vi. 28. 2 x Cor, il. 7. 





thoroughly understand what this is : ‘ God shall be all 
in all’;* what, I say, when God the highest and in- 
finite good shall be to all the saints food and clothing 
and life and all things: surely thou wouldst loathe all 
things present and only mind and seek the things that 
are above. But let us proceed to the rest. 

It is also almost like a miracle that for the preservation 
and continuance of man’s life God hath given to the 
smallest things continual motion which, though lasting 
long, yet causes no fatigue. Men take great pains to 
make a clock in which the wheels by force of weights 
turn twenty-four hours without stopping. With what 
great wisdom, then, does God act, who causes the 
nourishing faculty to act also continually and with- 
out stopping so long as plants and animals live, and 
also that lungs and arteries should move continually 
for seventy years or more. For of necessity the nour- 
ishing faculty must work, and the heart and arteries 
be in movement from the beginning of life to the end. 
And so in those who reach eighty and ninety years, it 
is necessary that their hearts and arteries should be 
moving all that time. And before the Flood, when 
men lived to nine hundred years, it was necessary that 
the heart and arteries, things fragile and delicate, 
should work continuously without any pause or rest 
for nine hundred years. ‘Truly those who do not 
wonder at these things and do not in them perceive 
and adore the divine wisdom, must be altogether 
deprived of the light of wisdom. 

To this we may add, thirdly, that the wisdom of God, 
although it can, without any labour of men or other 
animals, and even without the service of the sun and 


te Cor; xv; 28.

other secondary causes, both produce and preserve 
herbs and trees, so that all living things may have their 
proper food: yet he willed to use the service of second- 
ary causes and by the labour and industry of men and 
beasts, that no one might be idle, but that every 
one should exercise their strength. And he willed 
also that, among men, some should be rich and some 
poor, that all might have opportunity for cultivating 
virtue and might be united in the bond of charity. 
For the rich may exercise liberality and mercy, the 
poor patience and humility. ‘The rich need the labour 
of the poor to till their fields, to feed their cattle, by 
divers trades to prepare the things which are necessary 
for all. The poor again need the help of the rich to 
give them money and tools with which they may 
provide for themselves food and clothing and other 
necessaries. And there is no reason why the poor 
should complain of the divine wisdom: for God, who 
knows all and loves every one, gives to each what will 
bring him to everlasting life, foresees what will be 
useful; just as earthly physicians order fasting for 
some persons and cause them to be bled, and to others 
they order meat and wine and bid them enjoy them- 
selves that they may recover health. Doubtless very 
many poor folks attain eternal salvation, who if they 
had been rich would have perished eternally, and 
although the rich may be saved if they seek to be rich 
in good works and give that willingly which they 
received from our common Lord, not to store but to 
bestow, yet it cannot be denied that poverty is a safer, 
smoother, and quicker way to heaven than wealth. Our 
divine Master doth not deceive us. He saith, ‘ Verily 
I say unto you, that a rich man shall hardly enter into 
the kingdom of heaven ’;! and again, “ Blessed be ye 
poor, for yours is the kingdom of God,’ and ‘ Woe to 
you that are rich, for ye have received your consolation’ :?

nor does the Apostle deceive us when he says in his

sons and disciples of wisdom made choice of the 
smoothest and safest road to life. But because ‘ the 
number of fools is infinite’4 few choose the way of 
their own accord, but many turn aside from it with 
their whole affection and strength. 

Lastly, the length of the divine wisdom is seen in 
that, as it is itself eternal, so it hath implanted in all 
things a most vigorous eaniet to preserve themselves, 
to prolong their life and being as far as they can. We 
see men, when they perceive that their lives are in 
danger, moving all things, sparing no cost or pains 
to preserve them; we see creatures of all kinds struggle 
beyond their strength with stronger, lest they should 
lose their life; we see a candle when burning and near 
going out bestirring itself twice or thrice and putting 
forth a great flame, so that it seems to be striving as 
much as it can not to go out. We see drops of water 
hanging some time upon wood or stone, becoming 
globes and holding together as long as they can lest 
they should fall down and perish. We see heavy 
things ascending contrary to nature and light things 
unnaturally fall that there may be no vacuum, lest

they, being apart from other things, should not be

But it is yet more wonderful how for the propagation 
of the species God has endowed parents with so strong 
and almost inconceivable affection for their young. 
We see a hen, weakened by bringing up her chickens, 
although weak and feeble, fight bravely against kites 
and dogs and foxes: and how many troubles and 
labours women gladly bear in bringing forth and rear-

this is the counsel of the divine wisdom who to keep 
up this propagation as a shadow of his eternity hath 
ingrafted in all animals, even in brutes and wild beasts, 
a very strong love for their young. For as there are 
many animals for whose destruction men lie in wait, 
either for their own profit, such as hares, bulls, deer, 
thrushes, quails, partridges, almost all fishes; or lest 
they should do harm, as wolves, foxes, serpents, and 
numberless other such creatures: many kinds of ani- 
mals would have perished long since had not the 
wisdom of God, by this very affection, provided for 
their preservation and propagation. But if a natural 
love a this life so short and full of trouble exists in 
all living things, what should be our love for the blessed 
and eternal life? O blindness and folly of man! All 
things strive even beyond their, strength to preserve 
this very short life, a mere shadow of eternity; and 
man, endowed with reason, will not work for the true 
eternity of a most happy life, I do not say beyond, but 
even according to his strength. All things by natural 
instinct fear temporal death and shun it beyond all 
evils; man, endued with reason and taught by divine 
faith, neither stands in fear of everlasting death nor 
spurns it, at least not as he is accustomed to fear and 
shun the evils of this temporary life. And so truly did 
the Preacher say, ‘ The number of fools is infinite ’: 1
