CHAPTER 3 


That the height of the practical wisdom of God ts 
perceived in the work of Redemption. 


Now the height of the practical wisdom of God is seen 
in the work of our redemption; says Saint Augustine, 
“I was not sated in those days with the wondrous 
sweetness of considering the depth of thy counsels 
concerning the salvation of mankind.’ 2 

It was surely a most high counsel by the shame of 
the Cross to repair all the damage which the craft of 
the devil had caused through the sin of the first man, 
and so to repair it that the work repaired should become 
more beautiful than it was before it needed restoration. 
Four evils sprang from the sin of the first man: an 
affront to God by the pride and disobedience of Adam; 
the punishment of the first man and of all the human 
race by the loss of the grace of God and of eternal 
happiness; the sorrow of the angels who were greatly 
saddened by the injury done to God and the misery 
fallen upon man; the triumph of the devil and all the 
evil spirits who rejoiced over man overcome and cast 
down by them. All these evils the wisdom of God 
bore away and turned to greater good by the mystery 
of the Cross, so that not without cause does the Church 
sing, ‘O happy fault which had such a Redeemer.’ 3 

For if a tailor by his own industry and the addition 
of some ornament could so amend a garment old and 
precious, but cut and torn by some accident, that it 
became more beautiful and precious than before, it 


1 St. Matt. vil. 14. a Conf. ix. 6. 
3 Benediction of the Paschal Candle: Exu/tet. 


188 THe Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


might truly be called a fortunate rent, which gave 
occasion to such’ mending. The first man waxed 
proud by the craft and subtlety of the devil, sought 
to be like God, disobeyed him and transgressed his 
commandments, so in some way he took to himself 
the honour due to God. But Christ, the Second Adam, 
who is the wisdom of God, ‘ Humbled himself and 
became obedient unto death,’! and restored to God 
honour much greater than that which the first Adam 
by pride and disobedience had taken away. For Adam 
was a mere man, and if he had obeyed God he would 
have obeyed him in a very slight matter. For what 
great matter would it have been for our first parents 
to abstain from the fruit of the forbidden tree when 
they had plenty of others better? Therefore the sin 
was indeed the greater and so much the more serious 
as the obedience would have been easy and have in- 
volved no labour. But Christ was God and Man and 
humbled himself to obey God his Father in a thing 
most hard and laborious, to wit in the death of the 
Cross, which was full of sorrow and shame. Where- 
fore, if we consider the eminence of the Person and 
the depth of humility and obedience, nothing can be 
imagined greater or more meritorious or more honour- 
ing to God than that humble obedience of Christ. 
Therefore our Lord said very truly in the Gospel, ‘I 
have glorified thee on the earth,’? for indeed Christ 
Jesus glorified God the Father with an unspeakable 
glory before the angels of God and before all the holy 
spirits of the prophets and others to whom those things 
were made known. And if the angels sang together 
at the birth of Christ for the humility of the manger, 
“Glory to God in the highest,’3 much more did they 
sing the same song with greater joy for the humility of 
the Cross. 

Moreover, man himself, if he had not sinned, would

have reached only to an equality with the angels, but

Son glorified the Father by the humility of the Passion 
after an unspeakable measure, and the Father also after 
an unspeakable manner hath glorified the Son by exalt- 
ing him to his Right Hand, which glorification hath so 
affected the whole human race, that they are indeed 
ungrateful who do not recognize the immense benefit 
and who do not give God thanks. Because not only is 
Christ God and Man, but his Mother, who neverthe- 
less is human only and not God, was exalted above all 
the choirs of angels ; wherefore as they are possessed 
of so great an increase of the glory which they would 
not have possessed had not man sinned, men may 
rightly say, ‘O happy fault which deserved to have 
such a Redeemer ! ’ 

Moreover, the holy angels, as they were saddened 
by the fall of the first man, the sore disgrace of their 
younger brother, so did they exceedingly rejoice over 
the full redemption wrought by Christ. For if there 
be joy in heaven among the angels in heaven over one 
sinner that repenteth,3 how much more may we believe 
that there was very great rejoicing in the presence of 
those same angels that, in behalf of mankind, Christ

as Man had fully satisfied the justice of God and that

things in perfect wisdom and justice, and their will is 
so inseparably united to the will of God by the bond of

can never displease them. But the devil, who rejoiced 
for a time because he had overcome and cast down the 
first man, became afterwards more sorrowful over the 
victory of Christ than he was joyful before. For from 
that victory of Christ it came to pass that not only men, 
as was Adam, but even women and children insult the 
devil and triumph over him. For it would not have 
been a disgrace to the devil to be conquered by Adam 
in Paradise when he had no ignorance nor weakness 
but was armed with original righteousness, which so 
subdued to reason the lower part of his nature that it 
could not rebel, unless the mind itself first rebelled 
against God. But for the devil now to be overcome by 
a mortal man, a pilgrim subject to ignorance and con- 
cupiscence, is a very great disgrace; and yet he is 
overcome by the grace of Christ, and so overcome that 
many display trophies of chastity, patience, humility,

charity, although the devil eagerly and constantly casts 
his fiery darts of temptation and persecution. And in 
this matter the height of the divine wisdom is specially 
to be admired. For God saw struggle to be necessary 
for the human race in order that man should escape 
worldly honours and the like, which are the snares of 
the devil and draw such as are led captive to destruc-

in order that those things should become distasteful 
to man and that the contrary, purity, poverty, humility,

taking the form of a servant, made this bitter medicine, 
which was necessary to the sick man, so sweet and 
pleasant by his example, that many men now love fasting 
more than feasting, poverty more than riches, virginity 
more than wedlock, martyrdom more than daintiness, 
to obey than to command, to be despised than to be 
honoured, to be governed than to rule, to be humbled 
than to be exalted. For who, seeing God in human 
form, full of wisdom and grace, and who can neither 
deceive nor be deceived, poor, lowly, patient, and pure, 
and what is more wonderful, for the redemption of 
mankind nailed to the Cross, and willingly shedding 
his precious Blood, dying of most ardent charity, who, 
I say, would not be encouraged and incited to imitate 
him ? This was a wonderful and high contrivance of 
God’s wisdom, of which Isaiah sings, ‘ Declare his 
doings among the people.’! But even to this day this 
most high wisdom of God seems to be foolishness, not 
only to the wise of this world ’ as saith the Apostle in 
the former Epistle to the Corinthians, but also to the 
carnal and sensual men who believe in Christ, but 
refuse to follow his steps, whom the same Apostle 
called the enemies of the Cross of Christ. 3

out of the rock and oil out of the flinty stone, that is,

length thou mayst also desire to share in the Cross of 
thy Lord and to glory in tribulation and afflictions, that 
hereafter thou mayst also be partaker of his resurrec- 
tion glory, and not with the wicked to punishment. 


CHAPTER 4 


The depth of the practical wisdom of God consists in 
providence and predestination and the judgements of 
God. 


Tuere remains the depth of practical wisdom which 
consists in the providence and predestination and in the

judgements of God: for it is written, ‘ Thy judgements

First, then, the providence of God is exceeding 
wonderful, because it governs all created things imme- 
diately, and directs them to their end. ‘He careth for 
all alike’; that is, God cares for all without excep- 
tion, so that no sparrow falls to the ground without the 
providence of God, as the Saviour saith.3 He that can 
count the multitude of things which are in the whole 
universe, the same may in some measure guess at the 
greatness of the wisdom of God, ruling and directing 
all things in general and particular. Indeed one chief 
Pastor might rule the whole Christian world by a 
general providence, but not by particular, which com- 
prehends every Christian, and therefore many bishops 
are called to the charge: and one may rule many prov- 
inces by a general providence, but not by particular, 
which concerns every citizen, and therefore he employs 
viceroys or deputies or presidents. But God takes 
care of every one as of all, and of all as of such. “A 
sparrow shall not fall on the ground without your 
Father. ‘The very hairs of your head are all num- 
bered ’;4 and lest even one should perish, his provi- 
dence is always watching over us. ‘The young of the 
ravens forsaken by their parents are not forsaken by 
God.5 How safely, therefore, mayst thou rest, my 
soul, in the bosom of such a Father, even in the midst 
of darkness, among the mouths of lions and dragons, 
among numberless legions of spirits; cleave only to 
him in true love, holy fear, with certain hope, assured 
faith, undoubting. . 

But not only doth the providence of God care for 
each singular and present thing, but it reaches from 
one end to the other, ordering all things sweetly.®

Therefore God is called the King of ages,! because he 
himself established the order of the ages and disposed 
the succession. of kingdoms and the vicissitudes of 
times and changes from all eternity. To God nothing 
new, nothing unforeseen, nothing unthought of can 
happen. ‘The thoughts of mortal men are miserable 
and our devices are but uncertain,’ but God knows 
all things to come as certainly as things past and 
present, and disposed in his own mind before the 
foundation of the world the order and succession of 
all things. ‘Therefore our holy Mother Church sings 
publicly and securely that God’s providence erreth not 
in the order and disposition of thought. 3 

But because the reason of divine providence is en- 
tirely hidden and his * judgements like the great deep,’4 
it comes to pass that some, seeing that very many 
evils are committed among men and left unpunished, 
rush into the mistake of believing either that human 
affairs are not governed by the providence of God, or 
that all those evil things are done by the will of God; 
both of which opinions are wicked, but the latter is 
worse than the former, as Saint Augustine writes. 5 

But what misleads men into the greatest of errors 
is their seeing one part of the divine providence and 
not discerning another part, whereas they ought to wait 
for the issue of things which will be manifest to all 
in the last judgement ; they venture rashly to judge 
before the time, and are greatly deceived. Therefore 
the Apostle cries in the former Epistle to the Corin- 
thians, ‘ Judge nothing before the time, until the Lord 
come, who both will bring to light the hidden things 
of darkness and will make manifest the counsels of the

a remarkable parable: ‘If any one,’ he says, ‘ were to 
look only on a single mosaic pavement, he might blame 
the workman as ignorant of order and composition, 
namely because he sees a little part of his work, the 
greatest part he sees not: if he saw all the parts 
and their connection, doubtless he would praise with 
wonder the work and the workman.’ ! 

So therefore many see the wicked prosper and the 
righteous punished and afflicted, and they do not know 
what God reserves hereafter either for the iniquity of 
the wicked or for the patience of the righteous, and so 
they break forth into blasphemous words or say with 
those who spoke with Job, ‘God walketh in the cir- 
cuit of heaven: he seeth not,’2 or with those others 
which say, ‘Every one that doeth evil is good in the 
sight of the Lord, and he delighteth in them.’ 3 

Saint Augustine frequently uses another parable 
taken from poetry. For if any one begins to listen 
to an heroic song and says in the beginning or the 
middle, the verse is not good, he is rightly considered 
foolish: for he should wait until all the syllables are 
sounded and then he may find fault if the song does 
not please. And so those persons are altogether foolish 
who dare to blame the most orderly providence of God 
before the whole scheme of providence has run out. 

Therefore, my soul, if thou be wise, hinder, as much 
as thou canst, the doing of evil, for so God commands 
thee; but why he suffers the evil to be done, leave to 
his judgement, which may be hidden but cannot be 
unjust.

he may purge their light sins in this life, sometimes 
that he may crown their patience and humility and 
other good deeds with greater glory in eternal life.

FOURTEENTH STEP 


CONSIDERATION OF THE MERCY 
OF GOD 


CHAPTER I 


The width of the Mercy of God which bears our 


miseries. 


Cee Holy Spirit sets forth the mercy of God in 
the Scripture, praising it so wonderfully that he 
does not hesitate to magnify it above all the works of 
God: for thus the prophet David sings, “The Lord is 
loving unto every man and his mercy is over all his 
works.’* We shall easily perceive the greatness of this 
divine attribute if we consider it in its width, length, 
height, and depth. 

The breadth of the divine mercy consists in this, 
that God, and he alone, can take away all miseries from 
all creatures ; and some he takes away for the love 
which he bears towards his creatures, not on account of 
their profit to himself. Certain created things take away 
certain miseries, as bread takes away hunger, drink, 
thirst, clothing nakedness, knowledge ignorance and 
the like ; but no created thing can take away every 
kind of trouble. Besides, there are certain troubles so 
much the more serious the more secret and inward they 
are, for which God alone can supply a remedy: such 
as the assault of devils, who are very many, very crafty, 
very powerful, and are most wickedly disposed towards 
us: such also are errors and blindness of mind and 


t Ps. cxlv. 9. 
299 


200 Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


warped conscience, which things we in ourselves do 
not perceive, so that often we are deceived, thinking 
ourselves to be very sound in our interior life when 
in truth we are very sick. Who can snatch us from 
troubles of this kind but only our Almighty Physician ? 
And since God often delivers us from this kind of 
trouble without our being aware of it, all men may 
deservedly be called ungrateful towards God, as God 
himself witnesses when he says that the heavenly 
Father is ‘ kind unto the unthankful and to the evil.’ ! 
For we know hardly the least part of God’s kindness, 
yet for that part we do not give thanks with the devotion 
and humility that we ought. 

Next, creatures cannot take away all but only a few 
miseries, and not even from all persons, but from a few: 
but God can take away all miseries from all persons, 
and although he does not take away all from all, yet 
there is no one who is not in some way partaker of his 
mercy. Truly, therefore, does the prophet sing, ‘ The 
earth is full of the goodness of the Lord.’ The Church 
says in her collect, ‘O God, whose nature is ever to have 
mercy and to forgive’;3 since it appertains to him who 
is free from all manner of miseries to remove them, and 
it pertains to him alone to take away all miseries from 
all men, who alone is entirely without miseries. But 
who is entirely free from all misery but God alone, who 
is pure act, and the highest good, and whose Essence 
is beatitude ? O my soul, if by consideration thou 
couldst conceive what is the life of thy Lord and thy 
Father, a life raised above all misery, and pure and 
unmixed felicity, how wouldst thou sigh for thy heart 
to be in his bosom, that of thee it might also be said, 
* There shall no evil happen unto thee: neither shall 
any plague come nigh thy dwelling.’4 But thou wilt 
say, if God can take away all trouble from all things,

4 Ps. xci. ro.

why does he not do it, when he is the Father of mer- 
cies,! that is, a most merciful Father: whence comes it 
that, under the rule of the Father of mercies, so many 
afflictions abound amongst mankind, and why is it 
said, ‘ The earth is full of the goodness of the Lord,’ 
and not, on the contrary, “ The earth is full of misery ’ ? 
God can indeed take away all troubles, yet he takes 
away only those which his wisdom judges ought to be 
taken away, and the divine wisdom judges it inexpe- 
dient for men that all miseries should be taken away, 
and it is mercy sometimes not to take away some 
misery, that greater mercy may afterwards be shown. 
The Apostle thrice prayed the Lord that the thorn in 
the flesh might be removed from him: and he was not 
heard, because ‘ strength was to be made perfect in 
weakness.’3 God did not take from Lazarus the misery | 
of poverty and sores, that by a greater mercy he might 
be carried by angels into Abraham’s bosom.4 And 
how should the rich exercise the works of mercy, if 
there were no poor, hungry, thirsty, naked, sick, trav- 
ellers, prisoners? And if temptations and combats 
with demons were lacking, where would be the tri- 
umphs and crowns of virgins and confessors? And 
if there were no labours and sorrows, where would be 
the crown of patience? And if there were no perse- 
cuted, where would be the palm of the martyr? And 
so in this state of exile both are true: the earth is 
full of miseries because our very sins alone are great 
miseries, and also it is true that the earth is full of the 
loving-kindness of the Lord, because the conversion 
of sinners and the merits of the saints and other almost 
infinite benefits of God, both spiritual and temporal 
blessings, are nothing else but the great and continual 
mercies of God our Creator. 

Let us therefore give thanks unto our gracious God, 
because as our tribulations in this pilgrimage abound,

so also by his mercy our consolation abounds.: ‘ Thy 
mercy, O Lord, reacheth unto the heavens,’ saith

because mercy shall take away all misery whatsoever. 


CHAPTER 2 


The length of the Mercy of God ts his long-suffering 


or patience. 


Now the length of mercy is long-suffering or patience, 
which Scripture usually couples with mercy, as a part 
thereof: for thus David speaks, ‘ The Lord is full of 
compassion and mercy: long-suffering and of great 
goodness’ ;3 and again, ‘The Lord is gracious and 
merciful : long - suffering and of great goodness.’ 4 
Wonderful indeed is this long-suffering and patience 
of God our most merciful Father towards the human 
race, such as we do not find in a master towards his 
servants, nor in parents towards their children, though 
all are men. 

And first, God is long-suffering towards sinners, 
waiting for them with incredible patience, sometimes 
from their early youth to extreme old age, permitting 
them to blaspheme his Name and to neglect his law, 
and in the meanwhile doing good to them and giving 
them rain from heaven, and fruitful seasons, filling 
their hearts with food and gladness,5 as saith the 
Apostle. And who is there among men, lord or par- 
ent, so mild and easy, who if he sees himself despised 
and harmfully used by slaves or sons, and knowing 
that they have continued a long time in this perversity, 
would not at length drive them out of his house ? Yet 
the mercy of God is not overcome by the malice of 
men: but he is patient, ‘ not willing that any should 
perish,’ as Saint Peter declares in his Epistle,6 and

Wisdom saith, ‘ Thou hast mercy on all... and 
winkest at the sins of men.’! Moreover, patience 
seemeth greater in this, that many sinners by the grace 
of God having been drawn out of the pool of misery 
and the mud of pollution, and the children of darkness 
having been made the children of light and from guilti- 
ness of eternal death been called to the adoption of 
the children of God and to the hope of heaven, again 
and again, and often and often slipping back to their 
former vileness and ingratitude, yet are not forsaken 
by the long-suffering of God, but are most lovingly 
waited for and invited to repentance, and if they truly 
repent are received even as prodigal sons by the most 
merciful Father to the kiss of peace and to their 
former honours and dignity.2 It was with good reason 
that the Lord answered Saint Peter when he asked 
how many times he should forgive an injury done to 
him by his brother, until seven times? ‘I say not 
unto thee, until seven times; but until seventy times 
seven.’3 What he does himself in extending pardon 
to the penitent, that he wills us also to do. But he 
himself puts no limit to reconciliation except the end 
of this life: as long as the sinner lives, even should 
he reach to a hundred or more years, rising and falling, 
he 1s received and pardoned by his most kind Father: 
no repentance is too late for the mercy of the Father if 
only it spring from a truly contrite and lowly heart.4 
But no one ought on this account to abuse the good- 
ness of God and put off conversion from day to day, 
since no one knows in what day or hour he will depart 
from the body and be summoned to the tribunal of a 
most righteous judge. But so much the more ought 
all men to be called and drawn to repentance by such 
incredible goodness of God, for if the Lord is so kind 
towards sinners who often fall back, what will be the 
sweetness of the Father towards those who, after once

tasting the grace of God can never by any temptation 
be separated from it ? 

But there is another longanimity of God, beyond 
measure wonderful and ‘lovable, which is exercised 
in suffering the offences of the righteous. For albeit 
from enemies God hath made us friends by his bound- 
less goodness, slaves to become sons, those condemned 
to death to be heirs of his kingdom, yet so great is our 
ingratitude that daily we return him evil for good: for 
if the Apostle James says, ‘ In many things we offend 
all,’ what must we say, who are very far removed 
from the perfection of Apostles? Behold we speak 
with God in prayer, and forthwith, snatched away by 
imagination to think on other things, we as it were 
turn our backs on God. What master in the world 
would suffer his servants when standing in his presence 
and talking with him, to turn away suddenly and 
neglecting him to chat with their fellow servants? 
What shall I say of idle words, vain thoughts, fruitless 
works, excess in eating and drinking, sleeping and 
recreation, of neglect of holy things, of the omis- 
sion of brotherly correction and of numberless other 
offences in which we all offend ? And yet our God ts 
‘ good and gracious, and of great mercy unto all them 
that call upon him ’:2 he suffers, so to speak, boor- 
ishness and impoliteness, follies and incivilities of his 
children which truly men will not suffer in their fellow 
men. 

This Saint Augustine remarks in his tract on Psalm

gracious,’ where he bewails human weakness in avoid-

his servants. 

* For he knoweth whereof we are made,’ 4 and he deals 
with us as a mother with her babe, whom she cherishes

and feeds, even if perchance she be struck by him.

thing happened, he spared no confession, but forth- 
with made satisfaction. This course he practised so 
often that seldom was he troubled with these molesta- 
tions. Once in a time of Lent he had made a little 
basket, to pass away some short time of leisure without 
being altogether unoccupied: which coming to his 
mind when he said his Hours, did somewhat distract 
it: wherefore being moved with zeal in his spirit, he 
burned the basket, saying, I will sacrifice it to God, 
whose sacrifice it hindered.’2 And so we see that the 
wandering of the mind in time of prayer and praise of 
God is not so light a fault as many think: but great 
is the mercy and long-suffering of our God, because he 
is not grievously wrath and does not punish us un- 
endingly on account of it. 


CHAPTER 3 


The height of God's Mercy is understood ahi the 


moving cause. 
Tere follows the height of God’s mercy, whichis taken 
from the cause moving God to pity. And this indeed 
1 St. Matt. xii. 36. 2 St. Bonaventura, Life of St. Francis, ¢. 10. 


206 Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


is very high and above all heavens exalted, according 
to the Psalms, *‘ Thy mercy, O Lord, reacheth unto the 
heavens,’ ! and, ‘ Thy truth shalt thou stablish in the 
heavens.’ 2 | 

For some men have pity on other men because they 
are in need of their help, and this is indeed the lowest 
degree of pity, since it does not rise above our con- 
venience; and after this measure we have pity on 
horses and dogs and cattle. Others have mercy on 
others on account of relationship or friendship, namely 
because they are children, brothers, acquaintances, or 
friends, and this degree is a little higher, and begins 
to have the form of virtue. Certain, again, have mercy 
on others because they are neighbours, that is they are 
men as they themselves, created by the same God and 
of the same mould: therefore they do not discriminate 
between friends and enemies, good and evil, their 
own countrymen or foreigners, but they have pity upon 
all men, because they know them to be created in the 
image of God: and this is the highest degree to which 
mortal men can rise. But God hath pity upon all things 
because they are his creatures, and specially he hath 
pity upon men, because they are in his image, and more 
especially he hath pity on the righteous, because they 
are his children, heirs of his kingdom and co-heirs 
with the only-begotten Son. But if thou inquirest 
why God created the world, why he made man in his 
image, why he adopted him as his child and made him 
inheritor of his kingdom, there is no answer but this, 
because he so willed. And why did he will it, except 
that he is good? for goodness pours itself forth and 
communicates itself freely. Therefore his mercy is 
established in the heavens and from that highest dwell- 
ing, namely from the Heart of the highest Father, it 
came down to earth and filled it, as the prophet sings, 
‘The earth is full of the goodness of the Lord.’ 3 


r Ps. xxxvi. 5. 2 Ps, lxxxix. 2. 3 Ps. xxxill. 5. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE Mercy oF Gop 207 


And so God finds in himself what prompts him to 
have pity upon us, as in us he finds matter for 
punishment. | 

Raise now, my soul, the eyes of thy mind to that 
most highest fountain of mercy: consider that absolute 
purity, unmingled with any purpose of private good. 
And when thou hearest the Master of all exhorting 
and teaching, ‘ Be ye therefore merciful as your Father 
also is merciful,’ ! strive as much as thou canst, not 
only to have compassion on thy fellow servants, but to 
be merciful with that pure love with which the heavenly 
Father is merciful to us. If thou forgivest an injury 
from one who maligns or abuses thee, forgive him with 
a true heart and cast into oblivion every offence, for our 
Father forgets our offences, as Ezekiel the prophet 
writes, ‘ All his transgressions that he hath committed 
they shall not be mentioned unto him,’? and, as 
David says, ‘ Look how wide also the east is from the 
west: so far hath he set our sins from us,’ 3 lest, namely, 
they should seek to hurt us more; if thou givest alms 
to the poor, understand that thou rather receivest than 
givest, since, ‘ He that hath pity upon the poor lendeth 
unto the Lord,’ 4 and therefore give with humility and 
reverence, not as alms to the poor, but as a gift to a 
prince; if thou sufferest any inconvenience in order 
to be of service to a needy neighbour, think how far 
thou comest short of thy Lord, who to serve thee 
gave his life and his blood. So shalt thou, without 
hope of earthly reward and without any motion of 
vainglory, but out of the simple and pure love of 
God and thy neighbour, make progress in the virtue 
of mercy.

CHAPTER 4 


The depth of the Mercy of God is seen in its 
effects. 


Ir remains to consider the depth of the mercy of God. 
As the height of the mercy is most manifest in the 
cause, so the depth would seem to be in its effects. 
That mercy therefore is not said to be deep, but 
rather shallow, and superficial, which descends to words 
alone : that is deeper which raises and refreshes the 
unhappy, not only with consolations but with benefits: 
that very deep which comforts the needy not only by 
speech and deeds, but also by suffering and under- 
going labours and sorrows for their sakes. God, whose 
mercy is infinite, hath pity upon us in all these ways. 
And first, he hath sent to us letters of consolation which 
are the divine Scriptures, of which the Maccabees say, 
‘ We have the holy books of Scripture in our hands to 
comfort us.’t And not in letters only, but also by 
sermons of preachers, ambassadors for Christ to our 
reconciliation ;2, and God speaks to us by interior 
inspirations, promising us his help and protection: ‘ I 
will hearken,’ saith David, ‘ what the Lord God will 
say concerning me: for he shall speak peace unto his 
people and to his saints, that they turn not again.’ 3 
_ Secondly, the blessings of the mercy of God against 
our manifold miseries, both spiritual and temporal, are 
so many that they cannot be counted, for “He crowneth 
us with mercy and loving-kindness ’;4 that is, he sur- 
rounds us on all sides with the benefits of his mercy. 
Thirdly, the mercy of God descended by the mercy 
of the Incarnation, met labours and sorrows, hunger 
and thirst, ignominy and reproach, stripes and wounds, 
the Cross and death, to redeem us from all iniquity 
and from everlasting death, which by our sin we had 


ty Macc. xii.9. 72 Cor.v.19. 3 Ps. lxxxv. 8. 4 Ps. ciii. 4. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE Mercy or Gop 209 


deserved. Is there any greater depth to which the 
mercy of God could descend ? Yes, indeed; for this he 
did, not as compelled, but of mere grace. ‘He was 
offered because he himself desired it.’ For who com- 
pelled the Son of God, who thought it not a thing to 
be clutched at to be equal with the Father, and emptied 
himself, and took the form of a slave,? and became poor 
for our sakes, that through his poverty we might be 
made rich,3 and humbled himself to death, even the 
death of the Cross, that he might give us life, and exalt 
us?4 Indeed, love alone forced, mercy alone con- 
strained him. | 

But there is something deeper yet: for in the work 
of our salvation he willed to bestow on us glory and 
honour. Well fitting seemed that angelic distinction: 
‘Glory to God in the highest and on earth peace.’ 5 
Glory to God, benefit to men: but the mercy of God 
willed all the benefit to be ours, and part of the glory 
to be his own, part ours. For he willed to bestow 
grace upon us, by which we ourselves should co- 
operate in the work of our salvation and truly merit 
eternal life, which Christ merited for us, not because 
the merit of Christ was insufficient, but that he might 
communicate to us the praise and glory of our own 
salvation: whence it is said in the Gospel, ‘ Give them 
their hire,’® and the Apostle glories and says, * There 
is laid up for me a crown of righteousness.’ 7 

Lastly, the mercy of God towards men is most deep, 
especially to devout and God-fearing men, because it 
surpasses the affection of father or mother, which is 
the greatest affection we know on earth. Hear the 
prophet Isaiah, ‘Can a woman forget her sucking 
child, that she should not have compassion on the son 
of her womb ? yea, they may forget, yet will I not for- 
get thee’;8 hear David, ‘ Like as a father pitieth his

own children: even so is the Lord merciful unto them

Corinthians he calls God the ‘ Father of mercies and 
the God of all comfort.’ 2 

And so God is not only the Father of them that fear 
him, but a most merciful Father, very ready to console; 
for he takes away the afflictions and troubles from his 
children which he judges to be expedient to be taken 
away from them, and therefore he shows himself to be 
the Father of mercies: and he provides ineffable con- 
solation for the easy bearing of those troubles which he 
judges expedient not to be taken away, and in this he 
shows himself to be the God of all comfort. 

And the Apostle says ‘of all comfort’ for two 
reasons: first because God knows how to console his 
children in every kind of tribulation, which indeed the 
world cannot do, because it often does not understand 
the cause of the troubles; so the friends of Job were 

‘ troublesome comforters,’ 3 as he himself says, because 
they knew not the cause of his sickness, and applied 
a medicine which was unsuitable: or because often the 
trouble is so heavy that no human consolation can meet 
it; but God, the most wise and skilful physician, can 
cure every infirmity, and therefore the Apostle says, 
‘Who comforteth us in all our tribulation.’ 

Next, he is called the God of all comfort, because he 
knows how to console so fully and richly that it were 
better to suffer the tribulation joined with such a con- 
soler than to be without both, as it happened to a 
young man called Theodore, a confessor in the perse- 
cution under Julian the Apostate, who for ten whole 
hours was tortured with such great cruelty, and with 
such change of executioners, that no age records the 
like, and yet joyfully all that time sang the Psalms of 
David: and when orders were given to take him down

he began to be sorrowful on account of the great con-

What thinkest thou, my soul, of this so abundant 
and continual, so pure and infinite mercy of God, who 
needeth nothing of ours, and yet out of the abundance 
of his love is as much concerned for his little servants, 
as if all his own happiness depended on them? What 
thanks therefore wilt thou give him ? What canst thou 
ever do not to be ungrateful for such mercy ? Strive 
then as much as thou canst to please him, and to carry 
out what will please him: and since it is written, ‘ Be 
ye merciful, as your Father also is merciful,’4 begin 
diligently first to search out the miseries of thy soul: 
for indeed the miseries of the body are plain, and there 
is no need to admonish a man to take care of his body, 
for if the body even for one day is in want of food or 
drink, or passes one night without sleep, or by a fall or 
wound be hurt, it presently cries out, and is attended 
to with great care: but the soul fasts whole weeks from 
her food or languishes under wounds received, or even 
lies dead, and no one gives a care to her, no one has 
compassion. 

Therefore again and again visit thy soul: examine all 
its faculties and see if they are in good health, whether 
they are going forward in the knowledge and love of 
the true God, or whether, on the contrary, they are 
working in ignorance and ‘languishing i in various evil 
desires, or whether even the understanding is blinded

FIFTEENTH STEP 


CONSIDERATION OF THE GREATNESS 
OF THE RIGHTEOUSNESS OF GOD BY 
THE LIKENESS OF BODILY SIZE 


CHAPTER I 


The breadth of the righteousness of God is universal 
justice. 


Rae righteousness of God is taken in Holy Scrip- 
ture in four ways: first as universal righteousness, 
which comprises all virtues and is the same as holiness 
or uprightness. Thus, ‘ The Lord is righteous in all 
his ways and holy in all his works.’! Next it is taken 
as truth or fidelity, as in another Psalm, ‘ That thou 
mayst be justified in thy saying ’;? thirdly it stands 
for justice distributing rewards, as in the second 
Epistle to Timothy, ‘There is laid up for me a crown 
of righteousness, which the Lord, the righteous judge, 
shall give me at that day ’;3 and lastly for justice 
punishing sins, as in another Psalm, ‘ Upon the un- 
godly he shall rain snares, fire and brimstone, storm 
and tempest: this shall be their portion to drink, for 
the righteous Lord loveth righteousness.’ 4 

Therefore the greatness of the divine righteousness 
may be noted thus, by considering the breadth of his 
universal righteousness: the length thereof, that is of 
his truth and fidelity: the height of his righteousness 
distributing rewards in heaven: and its depth scourg- 
ing the wicked in hell with everlasting punishments. 


i Pevorlyy Tae bs ie Ae). 3y Si Cimiv Say to Ps Asin 
213 


214 Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


And to begin with the breadth: that righteousness 
is called by men universal, which so places a man that 
in every action he may conduct himself according to 
all the laws: and in this it comprehends all virtues, 
both theological and moral. But there is also a certain 
virtue called charity, which embraces all virtues in her 
bosom, commands the action of all virtues, and directs 
them to their final end. And this virtue, although it 
be a particular theological virtue, may yet be rightly 
called universal righteousness : for it so places man 
that he may conduct himself rightly towards God and 
towards his neighbour and thus fulfil the whole law. 
Thus the Apostle says, ‘ Love worketh no ill,’ and, 
“He that loveth, hath fulfilled the law,’ and, ‘ Love 
is the fulfilling of the law,’! and Saint Augustine in 
his book on Nature and Grace says, ‘ Love begun is 
righteousness begun, love increased, righteousness in- 
creased, great love, great righteousness, perfect love 
is perfect righteousness.’ 2 

Moreover, in God are all virtues without any imper- 
fection, and in the place of those which may imply 
imperfection there is something far better and more 
excellent ; and thus no goodness is wanting to him, 
since in him goodness and holiness are so great and so 
boundless, that in the best sense he is worthy to be 
called the only good and only holy.3 Faith is therefore 
in God not a theological virtue, because faith is ‘of those 
things which are not seen ’;4 but God sees all things. 
Nor is there hope in God, because hope is the expec- 
tation of things to come; but God expects nothing, 
since from eternity he possesses all things. Neither is 
there in God repentance for sin, because God cannot 
sin: nor humility, since humility hinders a man from 
rising vainly above himself, and causes him to remain 
in his proper place; but God has nothing above himself 


1 Rom. xiii. 8, 10. 2 ¢.70,no.84. 3 St. Matt. xix. 17. 
4 Heb. xi. 1. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE RIGHTEOUSNESS OF GoD 215 


by which he may seek to rise, because he is the Most 
High. Yet charity, the queen of virtues, is in God 
most complete: for he loves himself with an infinite 
love, because he alone perfectly knows the infinite 
good of his own Essence. And he loves also all things 
which he made: for thus saith Wisdom, ‘ Thou lovest 
all the things that are and abhorrest nothing which 
thou hast made,’! for God knows by his wisdom how 
to separate evil from good, that is defect from nature, 
even in the demons and the most wicked man, and he 
loves the nature which he made; the defect which he 
did not make, he hates. And further, there is such 
true charity in God that God himself willed to be called 
Love, as Saint John speaks when he says, ‘God is 
love.’2 But our love, when compared with the divine, 
is very narrow: for there are very many things which 
we do not love, because in them we do not easily 
separate good from evil, and many good things we do 
not love well and therefore we do not love with true 
charity, because we are evil, and follow rather cupidity 
than charity. And we love God unperfectly, not only 
because we do not love him as much as his goodness 
deserves to be loved, to which measure not even the 
angels reach, but also because we love less than we 
ought and also less than we might if we gave our- 
selves with more attention and diligence to prayer and 
meditation. 

This queen of virtues in the Lord of virtues is accom- 
panied with singular magnificence, unbounded liberal- 
ity, incredible kindness and beneficence, patience and 
long-suffering passing thought, goodness and sweet- 
ness more than fatherly, unfailing truth and fidelity, 
mercy filling heaven and earth, righteousness most 
upright and unswerving, and lastly holiness most 
pure and unspotted; so that ‘in his sight the stars 
are not pure,’3 and the seraphim, amazed in astonish- 

t Wisd. xi. 24. 2 1 St. Johniv. 16. 3 Job xxv. 5.

ment, cry aloud, ‘ Holy, Holy, Holy, Lord God of 
hosts.’ # 

O my soul, if thou didst carefully consider these 
things, with what fear and trembling wouldst thou wait 
upon God in praise and prayer: and especially with 
what reverence and humility wouldst thou at the holy. 
altar offer to the eternal Father, in the sight of the 
angels, his only-begotten Son, for the salvation of the 


living and the dead. 


CHAPTER 2 


The length of the righteousness of God manifested in 
truth and faithfulness. 


Bur let us pass on to what remains. The length of 
the righteousness of God shows itself by the truth and 
fidelity thereof. God is faithful in all his words,? saith 
the prophet; that is, the promises of the Lord, although 
pronounced many ages before by the mouth of the 
prophets, never will be frustrated, but fixed more firm 
than heaven and earth: for thus saith the Lord, “ It is 
easier for heaven and earth to pass than one tittle of the 
law to fail.”3) And our Lord means by the law not 
only the truth of the commandments, but also that of 
all his promises, for what the Lord commanded must 
be observed, or punishment follows, and what he prom- 
ised is by everlasting truth established and performed. 

Whence the Lord also saith, ‘ Heaven and earth shall 
pass away, but my words shall not pass away’; 4 and 
Isaiah, ‘The word of our God shall stand fast’ for 
ever . 5 and David, ‘ His commandments are true, they 
stand fast for ever and ever’; ® and the Apostle, * Let 
God be true but every man a liar,’7 and ‘ It is impos- 
sible for God to lie.’® The reason of this saying is 

t ‘[sa.V12)3.), 408, cHUV.112,, Vulg. 3. ot. Lake xvi. 17. 


4 St. Matt. xxiv. 35. BAS pe a SPs. Cxit. 
7 Rom. ill. 4. 8 Heb. vi. 18, 


CONSIDERATION OF THE RIGHTEOUSNESS OF GoD 217 


that he, being wisdom, cannot be deceived, being 
omnipotence he cannot fail: but men, however wise, 
however good, however mighty, may both deceive 
and be deceived, because they neither know all things 
nor can they do all that they will, and they also that 
are good when they promise may soon become evil 
and not fulfil their promises. 

Therefore if thou be wise, my soul, trust only in 
God, cleave only to him, and on him alone cast all thy 
caret Be anxious to walk with the Lord thy God and 
he will care for thee; beware with all thy might not to 
offend his righteousness, and his mercy will always 
defend thee, that thou shalt not need to fear what man 
or devil can do unto thee. 


CHAPTER 3 


The height of the righteousness of God is percetved 
in the bestowal of heavenly rewards. 


Tue height of the righteousness of God is perceived 
in the bestowal of heavenly reward which God himself, 
as the supreme and most righteous judge, hath prepared 
for those that have lived godly and righteously. And 
first we shall discern how great is the magnitude of his 
righteousness if we compare God as a Judge, with men 
that are judges: and next if we compare reward with 
reward, that 1s, the reward which God will give, with 
that which men usually give. 

Men as judges, even when princes or prelates, who 
have subordinates or servants, for the most part do not 
give just rewards to those that deserve them, for either 
they are unable for want of ability to give all according 
to their deserts, or because they do not know accurately 
what they deserve, since this depends on sincerity of 
heart and devotion: or again, because of their own 


hax St. Pet, vs 7- 


218 © Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


covetousness or malice or some other perverse inclina- 
tion, they will not bestow a just reward on the right- 
eous labours of their underlings: or lastly, because 
they themselves are overtaken by death before they 
can pay the rewards they owe: or they to whom the 
rewards are due depart this life before they begin to 
taste the fruits of their labours. But God bestows 
upon all righteous men rewards for all their good 
deeds, not only just rewards, but even beyond their 
deserts; for what good deed can be more lowly and 
more hidden than to give a cup of cold water to the 
thirsty ? And yet for this the Lord hath promised a 
reward,! and of the magnificent reward promised by 
the Lord, Saint Luke writes, ‘Good measure, pressed 
down and shaken together and running over, shall 
men give into your bosom.’2 Nor is there any danger 
that means will be wanting to God for giving rewards 
to all the righteous, since he himself is Lord of all things 
and can with his word alone multiply and increase all 
things to an infinite degree. And there is no fear 
that perchance he will be deceived in the number or 
value of the good deeds, since he is most wise, and all 
things lie open before his eyes and he himself trieth 
the reins and the heart of his well-deserving servants, 
and therefore he understands the mind, intention, 
fervour, and diligence with which they did all these 
things. And no one could suspect that there should 
be in God any ill will to defraud his servants and his 
children of their just recompense, since he is faithful 
in all his words. And lastly, he cannot die, since 
before all he is immortal, and all things live unto him, 
so that there is no danger of any, on account of death, 
being deprived of the due rewards. 

Therefore it remains sure and established that all 
good works of all righteous men are justly rewarded 
by God, and thus it is very safe to treat with God in 

1 St. Matt. x. 42. 2 St. Luke vi. 38. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE RIGHTEOUSNESS OF GoD 219 


the matter of labour and reward, and dangerous and 
foolish to put one’s trust in men and to expect from 
them just rewards for our works. 

Let us now compare rewards with rewards, divine 
with human, heavenly with earthly. What, I ask, can 
men bestow upon those who work for them all day, 
who pass whole nights without sleep, or lay down their 
very lives in battle ? O blindness of men !_ What can 
they bestow but small, base, and abject things which 
will last but a very short time? God gives great, 
sublime, and eternal things, and yet the former are 
sought after, the latter despised. 

Saint John Chrysostom compares the palaces, cities, 
and kingdoms of this world which men admire, with 
those mud buildings which children fashion out of 
clay.t_ And indeed little houses and cottages are made 
by children with much labour, but they are laughed 
at by the elders, and often when the father or master 
sees the children leaving their books and too much 
taken up with these trifles, he throws all down with his 
foot and overturns in one moment the things which 
they very carefully and slowly constructed. 

Even so, great palaces, towers, citadels, towns, 
cities, kingdoms of mortal men, in comparison with 
heavenly and eternal riches are as mud houses, and are 
laughed at by those blessed angels who behold them 
from above, and are often overturned by our heavenly 
Father and Master, that we may thereby understand 
how entirely vain and empty all these things are. And 
although now only a few observe this, yet in the Day of 
Judgement all shall see, when it will profit them little 
to understand. Saint Hilary in his commentary on 
Saint Matthew says that the Day of Judgement will 
show all these things to have been emptiness and 
naught. 

But we will explain a little more clearly the nature

of the heavenly rewards which are now spurned by 
many in favour of these earthly small rewards. 

First, there will be in the heavenly kingdom many 
good things or rather all good things which can be 
desired, for all that live there will be happy, and happi- 
ness is defined to be a meeting together of all goo 
things. And so there will be the good things of the 
mind, wisdom and virtue: goods of the body, beauty, 
health, strength: external good things, riches, pleas- 
ure, honour. 

And next, all these things will exist in the highest, 
perfect, and most eminent degree: for God, who shows 
his power in the creation of the world from nothing, 
and his wisdom in guidance and providence, and his 
love and goodness in the redemption of mankind by the 
mystery of the Incarnation and Passion of his Son, 
will then show the magnificence of his bounty in the 
distribution of rewards, of palms and crowns to those 
who shall triumph over their enemy the devil. 

And this wisdom will not be the beholding of 
divinity in the glass of the creature, but the very open 
vision of the Being of God, of the Cause of all causes, 
and of the first and highest truth, from which most 
glorious vision the souls of the saints will shine with 
such splendid light, that Saint John, speaking of that 
future glory, saith, ‘ We shall be like him, for we shall 
see him as he is.’!_ From the highest wisdom pro- 
ceeds charity, the queen of virtues; charity so fervent, 
that always abiding in the highest good, it neither will 
nor can be separated from it. So the whole soul and 
all its powers shall remain established in the most com- 
plete happiness. The body will shine like the sun, 
as the Lord himself testifies when he says, ‘ Then shall 
the righteous shine forth as the sun in the kingdom 
of their Father,’ 2 and such will be its beauty: health 
will be immortality, strength will be impassibility ;

and lastly, the body which is now animal will then be

riches will be so great that it will want nothing but 
God, and in God to possess all things: for he hath set

entering into the joy of the Lord, that is, to participate

and glory of the saints surpasses all speech, for in the 
sight of the whole world, of all men and angels, every 
saint will be praised by God himself and will be crowned 
as conqueror, and, the highest honour of all, will be 
placed as sharer of his kingdom on the very throne of 
Christ: for in the Apocalypse we read thus, ‘ To him 
that overcometh will I grant to sit with me in my throne, 
even as I also overcame and am set down with my 
Father in his throne.’4 The prophet marvels at this

felicity? and yet what we can scarcely conceive by 
thought, we shall prove by possessing, if by living 
soberly, righteously, and godly® we shall at length 
arrive unto that happy country; for those goods which 
the children of Christ acquire by momentary labours, 
shall indeed last for ever.

CHAPTER 4 


The depth of righteousness by which God hath prepared 


eternal punishment for sinners. 


THERE remains to be considered the righteousness 
which God will exercise towards sinners in the deep 
abyss of hell. If this is to be done attentively and 
seriously, let us first understand how very true is 
that which the Apostle teaches in the Epistle to the 
Hebrews, ‘ It is a fearful thing to fall into the hands of 
the living God.’ ® For if as we follow the order which 
we have followed in regard to righteousness rewarding 
the merits of saints, God the righteous Judge will 
punish all sins, even the lightest, such as an idle word, 
for thus we read in the Gospel, “‘ Every idle word that 


SP Ss 1XPTO8 2 Gal. v. 24. 3 St. Matt. xi. 29. 
453 StoPetun, eons o Litisiiiesy ee Ll eb exams 


CONSIDERATION OF THE RIGHTEOUSNESS OF GOD 223 


men shall speak, they shall give account thereof in the 
Day of Judgement.’! There are many sins indeed 
which men do not punish, either because they are 
unable to do so, the criminals withstanding or running 
away, or because they do not know whether the sins 
have been committed or not, or perhaps have not been 
sufficiently proved by lawful witnessess: or again, 
because they are unwilling to punish, being either 
bribed or influenced by favourites, or degraded by 
their own malice. 

_ But God is Almighty, and therefore no one can 
resist his power. He is in every place and so no one 
can hide from him: ‘ Whither shall I go then from thy 
Spirit, or whither shall I go then from thy presence ? 
If I climb up into heaven, thou art there: if I go down 
to hell, thou art there also.’ 2 

He is most wise and knows all things, even those 
obscure and hidden in the inmost recesses of the heart: 
and he is in no need of witnesses in order that crimes 
may be proved, since the consciences of men are to the 
Lord as a thousand witnesses: and lastly, no gifts, no 
favours, can corrupt the justice of God, since he is in 
no need of our goods. And therefore it remains that 
there is no sin, great or small, grave or light, which 
can escape the judgement of God and the avenger of 
sinners, unless it have been purged beforehand by 
penitence : for in the degree in which in this life his 
mercy is generous in pardoning, in that same degree 
after this life will his justice be severe and rigid in 
punishing. 

Of the time of this life Isaiah says, ‘ In an acceptable 
time have I heard thee, and in a day of salvation have 
I helped thee ’;3 and the Apostle explaining this in 
the second Epistle to the Corinthians says, “ Behold, 
now is the accepted time; behold, now is the day of 
salvation ’;4 and of the future time after this life

Zephaniah cries, ‘ That day is a day of wrath, a day of 
trouble and distress, a day of wasteness and desolation, 
a day of darkness and gloominess, a day of clouds and 
thick darkness, a day of the trumpet and alarm.’ ! 

And not only will all sins be punished with terrible 
torments which will be so great that man can now 
scarcely imagine them: for as ‘ Eye hath not seen, 
nor ear heard, neither have entered into the heart of 
man the things which God hath prepared for them that 
love him,’? so neither hath eye seen, nor ear heard, 
neither hath it entered into the heart of man to con- 
ceive what the Lord hath prepared for them that hate 
him. For there will be very many punishments of 
sinners in hell, very many, and pure, that is, un- 
mingled with any consolations, and (which infinitely 
increases the misery) everlasting. 

There will be very many punishments, I say, since 
each power of the mind and each bodily sense will 
have its own torture. Weigh the words of the sen- 
tence of the supreme Judge, which are contained in 
the Gospel, ‘ Depart from me, ye cursed, into everlast- 
ing fire.’3 ‘ Depart,’ he saith, that is, depart from the 
company of the blessed, deprived for ever of the vision 
of God, which is the highest and essential beatitude and 
the last end for which ye were created. 

‘ Cursed,’ that is, hope no longer for any kind of 
blessing, for ye are deprived of all the influence of 
grace, of all hope of salvation: no longer shall there 
rain upon you the water of wisdom nor the dew of good 
inspirations ; no longer shall a ray of heavenly light 
shine upon you, nor grace of penitence take root in 
you; no flower of charity, nor fruit of good works, nor 
shall the ‘ Dayspring from on high’ visit you hence- 
forth for ever. Ye shall lack not only spiritual goods 
but also bodily, not only eternal but temporal; there 
shall be for you no riches, no delights, no consolation, 

a Zephe ae. 1) n9 pt Corea 9 3 St. Matt. xxv. 41, 


CoNSIDERATION OF THE RIGHTEOUSNESS OF GoD 225 


but ye shall be like the fig-tree which, cursed by me, 
at once dried up from the roots.! 

* Into fire,’ that is, into a fire burning and unquench- 
able which will embrace and torment with sharpest 
pains, not one member, but all the members. 

‘ Everlasting,’ that is, into fire which needs no feed- 
ing of wood in order to burn for ever, but is kindled 
by the breath of Almighty God, so that as your sin 
can never be wiped away, so your punishment will 
never end. And so most rightly doth Isaiah the pro- 
phet cry, ‘ Who among us shall dwell with the devour- 
ing fire? Whoamong us shall dwell with everlasting 
burnings ?’2 and so he says no one can patiently bear 
this, but they are compelled to bear it, impatient and 
wrathful and hopeless, though unwilling; and he adds, 
‘Their worm shall not die, neither shall their fire be 
quenched,’ 3 and these words the Lord pronounces 
not once only, according to Saint Mark.4 

The worm of conscience and recollection of the 
present time is added, for the wicked, if they willed, 
by a little care could have escaped the punishment 
and could have enjoyed everlasting delights. And lest 
any one should think that the condemned can take 
some refreshment by walking about and changing their 
place,. hear what the Lord himself says, ‘ Bind him 
hand and foot and take him away and cast him into 
outer darkness: there shall be weeping and gnashing 
of teeth.’ 5 

And so those wretched ones bound hand and foot 
in everlasting chains will lie always in the same place, 
deprived of the light of sun and moon and stars, 
scorched with fiery heat, weeping and groaning and 
gnashing their teeth with rage and despair. And not 
only do they suffer in hell most grievous pains, thrust 
into that one place most full of horrors, but beyond

4 St. Mark ix. 43-45. 5 St. Matt. xxi. 13.

226 Tue Minp’s Ascent To Gop 


that, the extremest want of all things, disgrace, dis- 
honour, with the utmost shame and confusion: for 
they shall lose in a moment palaces, fields, vineyards, 
cattle, clothing, gold, silver, and precious stones, and 
be brought to such destitution that with the rich 
glutton they shall desire a drop of cold water and shall 
not be heard. These proud and boastful men who, 
impatient of all trouble in this present life would 
suffer no disgrace, but preferred dignity and honour 
before all things whatsoever, in that theatre of the 
whole human race and all the angels, than which a 
greater never was nor shall be, will see revealed and 
publicly shown all their evil deeds, even those com- 
mitted in darkness or hidden in the recesses of the heart, 
the most shameful betrayals, robberies, incest, sacri- 
lege ; for as the Apostle says in the former Epistle 
to the Corinthians, when the Lord shall come to judge 
the world he ‘ will bring to light the hidden things of 
darkness and will make manifest the counsels of the 
hearts, and then shall every man have praise of God,’ 
and doubtless there will be also blame from God to 
each impious and wicked man. 

But so great will be the disgrace and confusion of 
wicked men in this assembly, that Saint Basil, in 
explaining the thirty-third Psalm, fears not to say that 
this will be the heaviest punishment of all, especially 
to the hypocrites and proud and all those greedy of 
honour, to whom honour has been in this world in 
place of God, an idol. But if these things which we 
have said concerning the loss of all good things as well 
heavenly as earthly, and concerning the most bitter 
sorrows and ignominy and disgrace, were to have some 
end, or at least some kind of consolation, or had some 
mingling of refreshment as all troubles in this life 
have, it might be thought that in some way these ills 
could be borne; but as it is quite certain and beyond

CONSIDERATION OF THE RIGHTEOUSNESS OF GoD 227 


all doubt that the happiness of the blessed without any 
admixture of misery will last for ever, so also the 
unhappiness of the damned will be always without 
any admixture of solace, that is, it will endure for 
ever: and they certainly are most blinded and foolish 
who do not endeavour with all their power, through 
any tribulation, danger, infamy, and death, which the 
Apostle calls momentary and light afflictions,! to come 
to the kingdom of heaven and the happiness thereof. 


CHAPTER 5 


Why the punishment of the lost is everlasting. 


Awnp if perchance any should wonder why God, who 
is most merciful, hath assigned so cruel and lasting 
punishment to man’s sins which very quickly pass 
away and which do not seem to be so grievous: let 
_ him hear Saint Augustine on the City of God: ‘ He 
that holds the condemnation too severe or even unjust, 
cannot truly apportion the guilt which incurred it, 
or the ease in avoiding the sin’; and further on, 
‘Who can fully relate the guilt of disobedience to a 
precept so easily kept, standing neither in awe of the 
commander’s majesty nor in Ae of the terrible punish- 
ment awaiting disobedience ?’ 

Saint Augustine indeed is Seas of the sin of 
the first man: but the condition is the same in all sins. 
For if we weigh it truly and not deceitfully, we shall 
find that every mortal sin is exceeding great and in 
three respects: First, it is a dreadful thing that the 
creature should not obey the Creator, when the dignity 
of the Creator is separated by an infinite distance from 
the lowliness of the creature, and by nature the crea- 
ture is servant and by nature the Creator is Lord, 
and whatever the creature is or has he owes to the 
Creator and the Creator owes nothing to the creature. 


Ba Corsivesly. 2 St. Aug., de Civ. Dei, xiv, ¢. 15. 


228 Tue Minp’s Ascent to Gop 


Second, if the commands of the Creator were griev- 
ous, yet the creature ought to obey, but, © His com- 
mandments are not grievous,’ ! and his yoke is sweet 
and his burden is light, as the Saviour himself saith, 
therefore how great and inexplicable is the fault for 
worms of earth not to obey their Creator and in so easy 
a matter; besides, if God had not threatened sinners 
with the punishment of eternal death, perchance man 
might have brought forward an excuse for his sin, but 
since he has been so often and so specially warned 
by prophets and apostles that the punishment will be 
eternal, who will excuse the rebellion of sinners ? 

Lastly, if the sin of men condemned were not eternal, 
we might wonder why the punishment of sin should 
be eternal; but when the obstinacy of the condemned 
is eternal, what marvel if the punishment be also 
eternal? And the persistent pleasure in evil which 
will be common to condemned men and to devils, this 
perverse will, I say, turned away from God the highest 
good which shall so for ever remain, makes holy men 
have a greater horror of a deadly sin than of the fire 
of hell. Hear what Edwin the Englishman wrote of 
Anselm in the second book of his life: ‘ Conscience,’ 
he says, ‘is my witness, I lie not, that often we have 
heard him [Anselm] profess under the witness of truth, 
that if on one side he perceived the horror of sin and 
on the other he perceived the bodily pain of hell and 
must of necessity be overwhelmed by one of the two, 
he would choose rather hell than sin. And another 
thing perchance no less wonderful to some, he was 
used to say, namely, that he would rather be pure and 
innocent from sin and be in hell, than being defiled by 
polluting sin, possess the kingdom of heaven.’ If this 
holy man spoke and felt thus because being illuminated 
by God he knew sin to be more grievous than the pains 
of hell, how much more will God, who pierces even 

t rt St. John v. 3. 2 St. Matt. xi. 30. 


CONSIDERATION OF THE RIGHTEOUSNESS OF GoD 229 


to the depth, the malice and defilement and perversity 
of sin, truly judge and award the punishment which 
he hath appointed from all eternity as most due thereto. 
Therefore, O my soul, make no mistake, be not led 
away, be not like unto those ‘ Who profess that they 
know God, but in works deny him.’! For many have 
faith, but habitually not actually, like a sword hidden 
in its scabbard; for if they actually believed, and 
believing, seriously considered, that God is faithful and 
just and that in truth punishments very heavy and 
unending and without any admixture of consolation 
are prepared for the wicked: it could not be that they 
would do things they do, and ‘ drink,’ as it is said 
in the book of Job,? ‘iniquity like water,’ that is, 
commit so many and so great sins as easily and fear- 
lessly and joyfully as if reward, not punishment, were 
due to sinners. 

Do thou, I say, believe firmly, and believing, oft 
remember, that God is in this present life ‘ the Father 
of mercies,’ 3 and ready mercifully to forgive the sins 
of all that truly repent, and that after this life he will 
become the God of vengeance,4 and will exact from 
sinners those penalties which he hath prepared and 
which he commanded to be preached by his prophets 
and apostles, and to be handed down in writing for 
the remembrance of years to come. And in so doing 
it will come to pass that, being lifted up as it were by 
two wings, the fear of unutterable torments and the 
hope of exceeding great rewards, thou wilt pass in 
safety through the dangers of this present life and wilt 
come to life and rest everlasting. Amen.
